\cmdl[ampT]{\&T\p{n}} & Loop tests. & \cmdl[ampV]{\&V\p{n}} & View active configuration, call statistics. \\ \hline
\cmdl[ampW]{\&W\p{n}} & Store profile (accepted). & \cmdl[ampY]{\&Y\p{n}} & Profile at power on (accepted). \\ \hline
\cmdl[bangC]{!C, !CF, !CT} & Configurators. & \cmdl[bangR]{!R} & Init string of the settings. \\ \hline
\cmdl[bangM]{!M} & Settings model, for programs. & & \\ \hline
\cmdl[bsA]{\bs A\p{n}} & \std{MNP} block size. & \cmdl[bsG]{\bs G\p{n}} & \std{XON}/XOFF pass through (accepted). \\ \hline
\cmdl[bsJ]{\bs J\p{n}} & Rate adjust (accepted). & \cmdl[bsK]{\bs K\p{n}} & Break control (accepted). \\ \hline
\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N\p{n}} & Error control mode. & \cmdl[bsQ]{\bs Q\p{n}} & Flow control (accepted). \\ \hline
\cmdl[bsR]{\bs R\p{n}} & Ring indicator (accepted). & \cmdl[bsT]{\bs T\p{n}} & Inactivity timer (accepted). \\ \hline
\cmdl[bsV]{\bs V\p{n}} & Protocol in \rc{CONNECT}. & \cmdl[pctB]{\%B, *B} & Blacklisted numbers. \\ \hline
\cmdl[pctC]{\%C\p{n}} & Data compression. & \cmdl[pctE]{\%E\p{n}} & Line quality monitor (kept). \\ \hline
\cmdl[hashCID]{\#CID} & Caller ID. & \cmdl[hashUD]{\#UD} & Unimodem diagnostics. \\ \hline
\cmdl[starG]{*G, *H\p{n}=, *HS=, *V} & ZyXEL security. & \cmdl[starG]{\&Z\p{n}=} & Stored numbers. \\ \hline
\cmdl[CK]{CK<\p{key}>} & ZyXEL encryption key. & \cmdl[CK]{S102} & ZyXEL encryption. \\ \hline
\cmdl[hashCLS]{\#CLS} & Data, fax or voice (Rockwell, US Robotics). & \cmdl[hashV]{\#V\ldots} & Voice, Rockwell's and US Robotics' commands. \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{-C, -K, -Q} & \std{MNP~10}. & \cmdl[mnp10]{)M, @M, *H, :T19} & \std{MNP~10}. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} & Modulation. & \cmdl[plusGMI]{+GMI, +GMM, +GMR} & Identification. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusGSN]{+GSN} & Serial number. & \cmdl[plusGOI]{+GOI} & Object identifier. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusGCAP]{+GCAP} & Capabilities. & \cmdl[plusGCI]{+GCI} & Country. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusES]{+ES} & Error control. & \cmdl[plusESA]{+ESA} & \std{V.80} synchronous access. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusITF]{+ITF} & \std{V.80} transmit buffer. & \cmdl[plusDS]{+DS} & \std{V.42bis}. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusDS44]{+DS44} & \std{V.44}. & \cmdl[plusDSZ]{+DSZ} & zstd compression. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusB2]{+B2, +CBST, +CRC} & B-channel protocol, \std{V.110}, ring type. & \cmdl[plusIFC]{+IFC, +ICF, +IPR} & \std{DTE} interface. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusILRR]{+ILRR, +IDSR, +ILSD} & \std{DTE} interface (kept). & \cmdl[plusMR]{+MR, +ER, +DR} & Reports (kept). \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusPCW]{+PCW, +PIG} & \std{V.92} options (kept). & \cmdl[plusPMH]{+PMH, +PMHT} & Modem-on-hold. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusPMHR]{+PMHR} & Ask for modem-on-hold. & \cmdl[plusCVM]{+CVM} & \std{V.cvm}. \\ \hline
\cmdl[minusSEC]{-SEC} & \std{MNP~10EC} (kept). & \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS} & Data, fax or voice. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusEF]{+EF} & \std{ETC} transmit filter. & \cmdl[etc]{\&F5, \&F6} & \std{ETC} settings (S116). \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusX2UP]{+X2UP} & x2 upgrade host. & \cmdl[plusDEBC]{+DEBC} & EBCDIC \std{DTE}. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusFAA]{+FAA} & Adaptive answer. & \cmdl[plusFMI]{+FMI, +FMM, +FMR} & Fax identification. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusFTS]{+FTS, +FRS} & Fax silence. & \cmdl[plusFTM]{+FTM, +FRM} & Fax data. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusFTH]{+FTH, +FRH} & Fax \std{HDLC} frames. & \cmdl[plusFPR]{+FPR} & Fax \std{DTE} rate (kept). \\ \hline
\cmdl[vcmds]{+V\ldots} & Voice (\hyperref[sec:voice]{Section~\ref*{sec:voice}}). & \cmdl[plusFLI]{+FLI, +FPI} & Fax \std{Class 2} identity. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusFLP]{+FLP, +FSP} & Fax \std{Class 2} polling. & \cmdl[plusFCC]{+FCC, +FIS, +FCS} & Fax \std{Class 2} capabilities. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusFBO]{+FBO, +FHS, \ldots} & Fax \std{Class 2} settings. & \cmdl[plusFDT]{+FDT, +FDR, +FET, +FKS} & Fax \std{Class 2} pages. \\ \hline
\cmdl[hashF]{\#F, \#B\p{n}, \#V\p{n}, \#P, \ldots} & ZyXEL fax mode (\cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=6}). & \cmdl[hashF]{\&N32} & ZyXEL fax call (accepted). \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusMV18S]{+MV18S, +MV18R} & \std{V.18} text telephone. & \cmdl[plusMV18AM]{+MV18AM, +MV18P} & \std{V.18} answer message. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusMBAUDOT]{+MBAUDOT} & 5-bit (\std{TTY}, telex) settings. & \cmdl[plusA8E]{+A8E} & \std{V.8} and \std{V.8 bis}. \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusDSVD]{+DSVD} & \std{V.70} voice and data (\std{DSVD}). & \cmdl[plusASVD]{+ASVD} & \std{V.61} voice and data (\std{ASVD}). \\ \hline
\cmdl[plusTMO]{+TMO} & \std{V.59} diagnostics. & \cmdl[plusT]{+TRDL, +TAL, +TTER, \ldots} & Loop and error rate tests (\std{V.58}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}}

\mainmatter
\section{Command Lines}

Use \cmdr{AT} commands to control the operation and the settings of the
modem.  The modem accepts the commands in command mode.  The modem is in
command mode at these times:
\begin{itemize}
\item After it starts.
\item When a call ends.
\item After a command, but not after a dial command or an answer
  command.
\item Online, after the escape sequence
  (\hyperref[sec:online]{Section~\ref*{sec:online}}).  The call stays
  connected.
\end{itemize}
The modem answers each command line with a result code
(\hyperref[sec:results]{Section~\ref*{sec:results}}).

\begin{itemize}
\item A command line starts with \cmdr{AT}.  It ends with the character
  of \sreg{3} (carriage return) or \sreg{4} (line feed).  The modem
  removes the spaces and the tabs in the line before it reads the line.
\item The character of \sreg{5} (backspace) erases the last character
  that you typed.
\item A line can have a maximum of \textbf{63} characters.  If a line is
  longer, the result is \rc{ERROR}.
\item If echo is on (\cmdl[E]{E1}), the modem sends back the characters
  from the \std{DTE} as you type them.
\item If a line does not start with \cmdr{AT}, it is not a command.  If
  echo is on, the modem erases the line from the screen of the \std{DTE}.
\end{itemize}

The modem does the commands of a line in sequence (\cmdr{ATE1Q0V1}).  If
a basic command has no number, the number is 0 (\cmdl[E]{ATE} is
\cmdl[E]{ATE0}).  An extended command (\cmdr{+}\ldots) accepts
\cmdr{=}\p{values}, \cmdr{?} (its values) and \cmdr{=?} (its ranges).
You can put a \cmdr{;} between two commands.  \std{V.250} puts one after
an extended command if a different command follows it
(\cmdr{AT+IFC=2,2;+MS=V92}).  This modem does not need it.  If you do not
give a value in the \cmdr{=}\p{values} of an extended command, that value
does not change.

The modem keeps some extended parameters (\cmdl[plusILRR]{+ILRR},
\cmdl[plusMR]{+MR}, \cmdl[plusPCW]{+PCW}, \cmdl[minusSEC]{-SEC} and
others) for the setup strings of other modems.  They have no other
effect, except where an entry tells you.  \cmdr{=}\p{values} sets them.
Each value must be in its range, or the result is \rc{ERROR}.  \cmdr{?}
gives \rc{+\p{name}: \p{values}}, \cmdr{=?} gives the ranges, and
\cmdl[Z]{ATZ} sets the \emph{defaults}.

This manual puts the commands in groups by function.  Each group is a
section: echo and result codes, settings and profiles, identification,
dialing and answering, the speaker and the line, modulation, error
control, data compression, the \std{DTE} interface, \std{ISDN}, then fax,
text telephones and voice.  \hyperref[tab:summary]{Table~\ref*{tab:summary}}
lists the commands by name.

If a command fails, the modem stops the line and gives \rc{ERROR}.  If no
command fails, \rc{OK} follows the last command.  \cmdl[A]{A}, \cmdl[D]{D}
and \cmdl[O]{O} end the line and give their result later (\rc{CONNECT},
\rc{NO CARRIER} and other codes).  \cmdl[Z]{Z} ends the line with
\rc{OK}.  The modem does not read the text that follows these commands on
the line.  The exception is the \cmdl[D]{;} of a dial string: after the
modem dials the number, it does the remainder of the line
(\cmdl[D]{ATD}).  A command that gives information (\cmdl[I]{I},
\cmdl[S]{S}\p{n}\cmdr{?}, \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS?} \ldots) shows it on
separate lines before the result.

If the \std{DTE} sends a character while the modem makes a connection
(dialing, answering, the training of the modems), the modem disconnects
the call.  After the \cmdl[D]{;} of a dial string, the modem is in
command state and accepts commands.

\section{Echo and Result Code Settings}\label{sec:echo}

\begin{command}{Aslash}{A/}{Repeat Last Command}
\cmdl[Aslash]{A/} does the last command line again.  You do not type
\cmdr{AT} before it or a line end after it: the modem does the command
when you type the \cmdr{/}.  Its usual function is to dial a busy number
again.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{E}{E\p{n}}{Command Echo}
This command sets the echo of the characters from the \std{DTE} in
command mode (\sreg{13}).
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{E\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
E0 & No echo. \\ \hline
E1 & Echo (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{Q}{Q\p{n}}{Result Code Control}
This command sets the result codes on or off (\sreg{14}).
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{Q\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
Q0 & The modem gives result codes (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
Q1 & The modem does not give result codes. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{V}{V\p{n}}{Result Code Form}
This command sets the form of the result codes: words or numbers
(\sreg{15}).  \hyperref[sec:results]{Section~\ref*{sec:results}} gives
the two forms.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{V\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
V0 & Numbers.  A carriage return goes after each number. \\ \hline
V1 & Words (\emph{default}).  A carriage return and a line feed go
  before each word and after it. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{W}{W\p{n}}{Connect Message Control}
Other modems use \cmdl[W]{W} to select the data that \rc{CONNECT} gives.
The modem accepts this command, but it changes nothing.  On this modem,
\cmdl[ampA]{\&A} and \cmdl[bsV]{\bs V} select this data.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--2.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{X}{X\p{n}}{Result Code Set and Call Progress}
This command sets these items (\sreg{56}):
\begin{itemize}
\item If \rc{CONNECT} gives the rate.
\item If the modem waits for a dial tone before it dials.
\item If the modem gives a result for a busy tone.
\end{itemize}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--4.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{4}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.42\textwidth}|L{0.12\textwidth}|}{X\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{} & \thd{CONNECT} & \thd{Before dialing} & \thd{BUSY}}
X0 & Without the rate. & No wait for a dial tone. & No. \\ \hline
X1 & With the rate. & No wait for a dial tone. & No. \\ \hline
X2 & With the rate. & Waits for a dial tone (below). & No. \\ \hline
X3 & With the rate. & No wait for a dial tone. & Yes. \\ \hline
X4 & With the rate. & Waits for a dial tone (below) (\emph{default}). & Yes. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\cmdl[X]{X2} and \cmdl[X]{X4} wait for 1 s of dial tone.  The tone can be
steady or in a cadence with gaps of 1 s maximum (for example, the tone
of Italy: the modem adds the tone periods).  If no dial tone comes in
\sreg{6} seconds, the result is \rc{NO DIALTONE}.  This time is a minimum
of 2 s.  The modem adds 2 s more while it hears a dial tone in a cadence.

A busy tone is four cycles of the cadence of the busy tone or the
congestion tone of the country.  The modem uses the cadence from the
list of the \std{ITU}, with a tolerance of 15\% or 80~ms.  Examples are
0.48~s on and off or 0.24 in Germany, 0.75~s in India, and 1~s on and 4
off in Egypt.  The modem does not use the ringing tone.  If a country
has neither tone (none of the 192), the modem uses the limits of its
table.  \rc{NO ANSWER} comes with all values of \p{n}.  The \cmdl[D]{W}
and \cmdl[D]{@} of the dial string wait with all values of \p{n}.  A
\std{SIP} call has no dial tone: for \texttt{dmodemd}, use
\cmdl[X]{ATX3}.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampA}{\&A\p{n}}{Connection Lines Before CONNECT}
This command adds lines about the connection before the \rc{CONNECT} of
a data call (\sreg{70}).  \p{n} is the sum of the bits that follow.  With
\cmdl[V]{V0}, the first three lines are numbers and the modem does not
give the fourth line.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--7.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{3}{|C{0.1\textwidth}|L{0.52\textwidth}|L{0.22\textwidth}|}{\&A\p{n} Bits}
  {\thd{Bit} & \thd{Line} & \thd{With V0}}
1 & \rc{Modulation: \p{name}}, the modulation (\cmd{V92}, \cmd{V34},
  \cmd{V32bis}, \ldots). & 1000 plus its number (1092, 1034, 1132, \ldots). \\ \hline
2 & \rc{Protocol: LAPM}, \cmd{MNP} or \cmd{NONE}, the error correction
  (the \std{LAPM} of \std{V.42}, \std{MNP} 2 to 4). & 77, 78, 76. \\ \hline
1 and 2 & \rc{Compression: V44}, \cmd{V42bis}, \cmd{MNP5}, \cmd{ZSTD}
  (Annex F of \std{V.cvm}) or \cmd{NONE}: the compression that \std{V.42} or \std{MNP} agreed. & 68, 67,
  66, 70, 69. \\ \hline
4 & \rc{TxRate: \p{rate}}, the rate at which the modem sends. & None. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
The \emph{default} is 0, no lines.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampE}{\&E\p{n}}{Rate in CONNECT}
The modem keeps the value in \sreg{71}.  The value changes nothing:
\rc{CONNECT} always gives the rate at which the modem receives.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bsV}{\bs V\p{n}}{Protocol Result Code}
This command sets if the \rc{CONNECT} of a data call gives the error
correction and the compression after its rate (\sreg{106}).  With
\cmdl[V]{V0} or \cmdl[X]{X0}, this command changes nothing.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--2.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\bs V\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
\bs V0 & Only the rate: \rc{CONNECT 33600} (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\bs V1, \bs V2 & The rate and the protocol: \rc{CONNECT 33600/LAPM/V42bis},
  \rc{CONNECT 2400/MNP/MNP5}, \rc{CONNECT 28800/NONE}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusMR}{+MR, +ER, +DR}{Modulation, Error Control and Compression Reports}
On other modems, these commands enable the \rc{+MCR:}, \rc{+MRR:},
\rc{+ER:} and \rc{+DR:} reports.  This modem keeps the values.  Each
value is 0 or 1, \emph{default} 0.
\end{command}

\section{Settings and Profiles}\label{sec:profiles}

\begin{command}{S}{S\p{n}}{Read or Set an S-Register}
\cmdl[S]{ATS}\p{n}\cmdr{?} reads S-register \p{n} (0 to 255).  It gives
the value in decimal on a separate line.  \cmdl[S]{ATS}\p{n}\cmdr{=}\p{v}
sets the register to \p{v}.  The registers have 8 bits, thus the modem
uses \p{v} modulo 256.  The two numbers are decimal, also with a 0 at the
start (\cmdl[S]{ATS09?} is \sreg{9}).  \cmdl[S]{ATS103=}\p{n} is
\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N}\p{n}.  \hyperref[sec:sregs]{Section~\ref*{sec:sregs}}
lists the registers.
\begin{syntax}
\item ATS\p{n}?
\item ATS\p{n}=\p{v}
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a number more than 255, a read-only register (\sreg{44}, \sreg{45}), or if \cmdr{?} or \cmdr{=} does not follow the number.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{Z}{Z\p{n}}{Reset}
This command disconnects the call and sets the modem to its condition
at the start.  It sets these items: the S-registers,
\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}, modem-on-hold, the data mode
(\cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=0}), the extended parameters and the loop
tests (\cmdl[ampT]{\&T5}).  The country does not change.  The
S-registers that have no \emph{default} also do not change: \sreg{18},
\sreg{28}, \sreg{47} (\cmdl[ampL]{\&L}), \sreg{48}, \sreg{161} and the
free registers.  There are no stored profiles, thus \p{n} (0 to 3)
changes nothing.  The modem does not read the remainder of the line.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampF}{\&F\p{n}}{Restore Factory Defaults}
The modem accepts this command, with or without a digit, and does
nothing.  Refer to \cmdl[ampD]{\&D}.  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} sets the
\emph{defaults} again.
Two exceptions load the \std{ETC} settings of the 3Com cards
(\hyperref[sec:etc]{Section~\ref*{sec:etc}}): \cmdl[ampF]{\&F5} and
\cmdl[ampF]{\&F6}.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampV}{\&V\p{n}}{View Active Configuration}
\cmdl[ampV]{\&V} and \cmdl[ampV]{\&V0} show the active settings, in lines
of a maximum of \textbf{72} columns.  The list has these items:
\begin{itemize}
\item The settings of the basic commands.
\item The S-registers that the modem uses (\rc{S\p{nn}:\p{vvv}}), but
  not \sreg{18} or \sreg{44} to \sreg{48}.
\item \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} and the other extended settings.
\item The extended parameters.
\end{itemize}
There are no stored profiles: the list ends with \rc{STORED PROFILES:
none}.

\cmdl[ampV]{\&V1} gives the statistics of the last call in the format of
the Rockwell modems.  Each item is on one line: the name, with dots to
column 28, then the value.  It gives the same call as \cmdl[I]{ATI11}:
in online command state, the call that is connected, or else the last
call.  Before the first call, it gives \rc{No Previous Call}.  If a call
did not connect, or if it was a fax call, the modem gives only the first
line.  If the modem does not measure a value, the value is \cmd{N/A}.

\begin{vtable}{2}{|T{0.33\textwidth}|L{0.53\textwidth}|}{AT\&V1 Lines}
  {\thd{Line} & \thd{Value}}
TERMINATION REASON & \cmd{NONE} while the call is connected.  \cmd{LOCAL REQUEST}
  (\cmdl[H]{ATH}, \cmdl[Z]{ATZ}, \std{DTR}), \cmd{KEY ABORT} (a character
  during the connection procedure), \cmd{GSTN CLEARDOWN} (the \std{LAPM}
  \std{DISC} or \std{MNP} \std{LD} of the far end), \cmd{LINK DISCONNECT} (the \std{SIP}
  call ended), \cmd{CARRIER LOSS}, \cmd{RETRAIN FAILURE} (a retrain that
  failed), \cmd{INCOMPATIBLE PROTOCOL} (no error correction where it is
  necessary, errors of \std{LAPM}) or \cmd{NO CONNECT} (no dial tone, busy,
  no answer, training failed). \\ \hline
LAST TX rate, LAST RX rate & The rates now, or at the end, as
  \p{n}\cmd{ BPS}.  Rockwell rounds a \std{PCM} rate (\cmd{53333}). \\ \hline
HIGHEST TX rate, HIGHEST RX rate & The highest rates of the call. \\ \hline
PROTOCOL & \cmd{LAPM}, \cmd{MNP} or \cmd{NONE}. \\ \hline
COMPRESSION & \cmd{V44}, \cmd{V42Bis}, \cmd{MNP5}, \cmd{ZSTD} or \cmd{NONE}. \\ \hline
Line QUALITY & The mean squared error of the receiver, as the Rockwell
  three-digit value (the \std{NMSE} times 128: a lower value is better). \\ \hline
Rx LEVEL & The received power in dBm0 without its sign, three digits (\cmd{015} for $-15$\,dBm0). \\ \hline
Highest Rx State, Highest TX State, EQM Sum & \cmd{N/A} (the pump states of
  Rockwell). \\ \hline
RBS Pattern & The frames of six in which the network uses a bit, as a hex
  mask (\cmd{0x08}).  The \std{DIL} of the analogue modem finds them
  (\std{V.90}, \std{V.92}). \\ \hline
Rate Drop & \cmd{N/A}. \\ \hline
Digital Pad & The pad of the network: \cmd{None}, \cmd{3dB}, \cmd{6dB}, \ldots
  (\std{V.90}, \std{V.92}). \\ \hline
Local Rtrn Count, Remote Rtrn Count & The retrains of this modem and of
  the far end, two digits. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\example{Example output: AT\&V}
\begin{verbatim}
ACTIVE PROFILE:
E1 L3 M1 N1 Q0 T V1 X4 Y0 &A0 &C0 &E0 &H0 &P0 \A3 \N3 \V0 %C3 %E0
S00:000 S01:000 S02:043 S03:013 S04:010 S05:008 S06:002 S07:060 S08:002
...
+MS=92,1,300,56000 +FCLASS=0 +GCI=b5 +PMH=0 +PMHT=5 #CID=0 +IFC=2,2
...
STORED PROFILES: none
\end{verbatim}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampW}{\&W\p{n}}{Store Current Configuration}
There are no stored profiles: \cmdl[ampW]{\&W}, \cmdl[ampW]{\&W0} and
\cmdl[ampW]{\&W1} give \rc{OK} and store nothing.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampY}{\&Y\p{n}}{Profile at Power On}
The modem accepts this command, but it changes nothing: there are no
stored profiles.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bangR}{!R}{Init String of the Settings}
\cmdl[bangR]{!R} shows an init string: the commands that set each setting
that is not at its default.  You can copy this string into the next
session after \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} (there are no stored profiles).  The string
has the settings that the configurators (\cmdl[bangC]{!C}) show:
\begin{itemize}
\item The settings of the basic commands (\cmdl[E]{E}, \cmdl[X]{X},
  \cmdl[bsN]{\bs N}, \ldots).
\item The S-registers that have no command (\sreg{0}, \sreg{6} to
  \sreg{12}, \ldots).
\item \cmdl[plusGCI]{+GCI}.  It is first, because it sets \sreg{8} and
  \sreg{24}.
\item \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} (its four values together),
  \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS}, \cmdl[hashCID]{\#CID}, \cmdl[plusPMH]{+PMH}
  and the extended parameters.
\end{itemize}
A line has a maximum of \textbf{63} characters, as a command line has.
If the string is longer, the modem uses more lines.  Each line starts
with \cmd{AT}.  If no setting has changed, the modem shows only
\cmd{AT}.  The command operates in all states, also during a call.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\example{Example output: AT!R}
\begin{verbatim}
AT+GCI=B4;+MS=34,1,1200,28800;\N0L1S0=2+ES=1,0,1
OK
\end{verbatim}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bangM}{!M}{Settings Model, for Programs}
\cmdl[bangM]{!M} lists the settings that the configurators
(\cmdl[bangC]{!C}) show.  A program can use this list to make its own
settings page (the D-Modem desktop apps do this).  There is one line for
each setting and one line for each choice of a setting.  Tabs separate
the fields.
\begin{itemize}
\item The first line is \cmd{model}, the format (\textbf{1}) and the
  number of settings that follow.
\item A setting line has these fields: \cmd{setting}, its key, its type
  (\cmd{pick}, \cmd{number} or \cmd{text}, the last is a list of
  numbers), its value now, its default, the lowest and highest values
  (only for a number), its command (\cmd{\%s} shows the position of the
  value), its group, its label and a line of help.
\item The choices of a pick follow it.  Each choice line has these
  fields: \cmd{choice}, the value that the command accepts, its name in
  the command line configurator and its label.
\end{itemize}
As \cmdl[bangR]{!R}, this command operates in all states.  The modem
reads the remainder of the line as more commands.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\example{Example output: AT!M (a part; the gaps are tabs)}
\begin{verbatim}
model    1         73
setting  country   pick  B5  B5        +GCI=%s  Line  Country  ...
choice   B5        usa   USA
...
setting  echo      pick  1   1         E%s  Terminal  Command echo ...
choice   0         off   Off
choice   1         on    On
OK
\end{verbatim}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bangC}{!C, !CF, !CT}{Configurators}
The configurators show each setting with its value and its default.
They change the settings with the commands of the settings, thus they
accept the same values as the commands.  They operate only while the
modem is idle and on hook.  At other times, the result is \rc{ERROR}.
The modem does not read the remainder of the command line.  When you
exit the configurator, the result is \rc{OK}.  A \rc{RING} stops the
configurator immediately, with no \rc{OK}.  This also occurs when the
\std{DTE} disconnects.
\begin{itemize}
\item \cmdl[bangC]{!CF} is a full-screen configurator in the style of
  the Turbo Vision of Borland, for an \std{ANSI} terminal.  It has a menu
  bar (\textbf{F10}, Alt and a letter), the groups of settings and their
  settings side by side, dialogs for a value, and a status line.  Use
  these keys:
  \begin{itemize}
  \item The arrows move the cursor.
  \item Tab moves between the groups and the settings.
  \item Enter changes a setting.  Left, Right and Space go through its
    values.  Del sets its default.
  \item \textbf{F2} saves the changes.
  \item \textbf{F3} shows the init string of the changes
    (\cmdl[bangR]{!R}).
  \item \textbf{F5} sets all defaults.
  \item Alt-X or Esc closes the configurator.  It asks if you want to
    save.
  \end{itemize}
  The configurator sends only the cells that change, in \std{UTF-8} or
  \std{CP437} box characters.
\item \cmdl[bangC]{!CT} is a command line in the style of the IOS of
  Cisco.  \cmd{dmodem\#} accepts \cmd{show running-config} (the settings
  that are not at their defaults), \cmd{show settings} [\p{group}],
  \cmd{show init-string}, \cmd{show version}, \cmd{configure terminal}
  and \cmd{exit}.  \cmd{dmodem(config)\#} accepts \p{setting} \p{value},
  \cmd{no} \p{setting} (off, or its default), \cmd{default} \p{setting},
  \cmd{do} \p{command}, and \cmd{exit} or \cmd{end} (or Ctrl-Z).  You can
  type a short form of a word (\cmd{sh run}, \cmd{conf t}).  \cmd{?}
  lists the possible next words.  Tab completes a word.  The Up and Down
  arrows show the lines that you typed before.  A change has an effect
  immediately.
\item \cmdl[bangC]{!C} examines the terminal: it asks for the position of
  the cursor (\cmd{ESC [6n}).  If a terminal answers in 1.5\,s, the
  modem starts \cmdl[bangC]{!CF}.  It also asks the terminal for its size
  and for \std{UTF-8} in the same way.  If the terminal does not answer,
  the modem starts \cmdl[bangC]{!CT}.  \cmdl[bangC]{!CF} also asks.  If
  it gets no answer, it uses 80 by 24 in \std{UTF-8}.
\end{itemize}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{When you exit the configurator.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{The modem is not idle and on hook.}
\end{results}
\example{Example: AT!CT}
\begin{verbatim}
dmodem#conf t
Enter configuration commands, one per line.  End with CNTL/Z.
dmodem(config)#modulation v34
dmodem(config)#no echo
dmodem(config)#end
dmodem#sh run
Building configuration...

Current configuration:
!
modulation v34
echo off
!
end
\end{verbatim}
\end{command}

\section{Identification and Diagnostics}\label{sec:ident}

\begin{command}{I}{I\p{n}}{Identification and Statistics}
This command gives the identity of the modem, its settings and the
statistics of the last call.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--7, 11 or 12.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{I\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
I0 & The product: \cmd{D-Modem}. \\ \hline
I1, I2 & The product and version (\rc{D-Modem, 1.0.0}), then the
  manufacturer, \rc{D-Modem project}. \\ \hline
I3 & The name of the modem, then the type of its line (\texttt{dmodemd}: \cmd{dmodem0},
  \cmd{SIP}). \\ \hline
I4 & \sreg{0} to \cmdr{S23}, eight on each line (\rc{s00=000 s01=000 \ldots}). \\ \hline
I5 & \rc{Stored Profile 0:} (there are no stored profiles). \\ \hline
I6 & The statistics of the last call, as the link diagnostics of
  USRobotics (the same as \cmdl[I]{I11}). \\ \hline
I7 & The country: \rc{Country: USA}. \\ \hline
I11 & The statistics of the last call, in the format of the Conexant modems. \\ \hline
I12 & The carriers of the last \cmd{PEP} call: the frequency of each bin,
  its bits in each direction and its noise (dBm0).  After a different
  call, \rc{No PEP Call}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}

\cmdl[I]{ATI11} lists the statistics of the last call:
\begin{itemize}
\item In online command state, the statistics of the call that is
  connected.
\item After a call (dialed or answered, connected or not), the
  statistics of that call, until the next call.
\item Before the first call, \rc{No Previous Call}.
\end{itemize}
\cmdl[I]{ATI6} gives the same lines.  \cmdl[ampV]{AT\&V1} gives the same
call in the Rockwell format.  \cmdl[hashUD]{AT\#UD} gives the same values
in the Unimodem format.  If the call does not have a value, the modem
does not give its line.  Examples: the rates and the lines that follow
them for a call that did not connect, the frame counts without error
correction, the coding lines without \std{V.34}, the measurements that a
modulation does not make, and the cause while the call is connected.

\begin{vtable}{2}{|T{0.33\textwidth}|L{0.53\textwidth}|}{ATI11 Lines}
  {\thd{Line} & \thd{Value}}
Last Connection & The modulation of the connection (\cmd{V34}, \cmd{V90},
  \cmd{V92}, \cmd{V91}, \cmd{V32bis}, \cmd{PEP}, \ldots), \cmd{Fax}, or \cmd{None}. \\ \hline
Initial Transmit/Receive Carrier Rate & The rates (bit/s) at \rc{CONNECT}. \\ \hline
Final Transmit/Receive Carrier Rate & The rates now, or at the end
  (after renegotiations). \\ \hline
Symbol Rate (Tx/Rx) & The symbols each second in each direction (\std{V.22} to \std{V.92},
  the clear channel). \\ \hline
Carrier Frequency (Hz, Tx/Rx) & The carrier of each direction.  \cmd{PCM} for
  a \std{V.90}, \std{V.91} or \std{V.92} \std{PCM} direction. \\ \hline
Protocol Negotiation Result & \cmd{LAPM}, \cmd{MNP} or \cmd{NONE}. \\ \hline
Data Compression Result & \cmd{V44}, \cmd{V42bis}, \cmd{MNP5},
  \cmd{ZSTD} or \cmd{NONE}. \\ \hline
Trellis Code, Nonlinear Encoding, Precoding, Shaping (Tx/Rx) & The coding
  of \std{V.34} in each direction (\cmd{16S-4D}, \cmd{ON}, \cmd{OFF}.  \cmd{-} for a \std{PCM}
  direction). \\ \hline
Estimated \std{SNR} (dB) & The signal to noise ratio of the receiver (\std{V.34}, the upstream
  of \std{V.90}, \std{V.32bis}, \std{V.29}, \std{V.33}, \std{V.27ter}, \std{V.wb}). \\ \hline
Estimated Noise Level (dBm0) & The noise that the \std{SNR} shows: the
  received power minus the \std{SNR}, as the Lucent modems give it. \\ \hline
Receive/Transmit Signal Power Level (dBm0) & The mean power of the line
  in each direction while the call was connected. \\ \hline
Round Trip Delay (ms) & The value that phase 2 of \std{V.34} (or the start
  of \std{V.cvm}) measured. \\ \hline
Near/Far Echo Level (dBm0) & The echoes of the signal of this modem that
  the canceller removes (\std{V.34}, \std{V.90}, \std{V.92}). \\ \hline
Carrier Frequency Offset (Hz) & The difference between the carrier of
  the far end and the carrier of this modem (\std{V.22} to \std{V.92}). \\ \hline
Timing Offset (ppm) & The difference between the clock of the far end and
  the clock of this modem (\std{V.32bis} to \std{V.92}). \\ \hline
\std{PCM} Encoding & The law of the network, \cmd{A-law} or \cmd{u-law} (\std{V.90},
  \std{V.92}). \\ \hline
Digital Loss (dB), Robbed Bit Frames & The pad of the network and the frames
  of six in which it uses a bit (\cmd{None}, \cmd{6}, \ldots).  The \std{DIL} of
  the analogue modem finds them (\hyperref[sec:analogue]{Section~\ref*{sec:analogue}}). \\ \hline
Characters Sent/Received & The characters of the \std{DTE}. \\ \hline
Transmit Frame Count & The frames of \std{LAPM} or \std{MNP} that the modem sent. \\ \hline
Transmit Frame Error Count & The information frames from these that the
  modem sent again. \\ \hline
Receive Frame Count & The frames that the modem received with no damage. \\ \hline
Receive Frame Error Count & The frames that the modem received with a bad \std{FCS}. \\ \hline
\cmd{PEP} Line Rate (Tx/Rx) & The rate of \cmd{PEP} on the line in each
  direction while a side has the line (bit/s). \\ \hline
\cmd{PEP} Packets Accepted, Received Bad & The packets of \cmd{PEP} that
  the modem accepted, and the packets that it received with a bad \std{CRC}. \\ \hline
\cmd{PEP} Micro Turns (Tx/Rx) & Its short turns, sent and received. \\ \hline
Retrains by Local Modem, Retrains Requested by Remote Modem & The
  retrains of this modem and of the far end. \\ \hline
Rate Renegotiation by Local/Remote Modem & Rate renegotiations (\std{V.34},
  \std{V.90}, \std{V.92}, \std{V.32bis}). \\ \hline
Call Duration & From \rc{CONNECT}, \p{h}\cmd{:}\p{mm}\cmd{:}\p{ss}. \\ \hline
Call Termination Cause & \rc{Local hangup} (\cmdl[H]{ATH},
  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ}, \std{DTR}, a character during the connection procedure), \rc{Remote
  cleardown} (the \std{LAPM} \std{DISC} or \std{MNP} \std{LD} of the far end), \rc{Line dropped}
  (the \std{SIP} call ended), \rc{Carrier lost}, \rc{No dial tone},
  \rc{Busy}, \rc{No answer}, \rc{Training failed}, \rc{Protocol
  failure} (no error correction where it is necessary, errors of \std{LAPM}),
  \rc{No carrier}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}

\example{Example output: ATI11}
\begin{verbatim}
Description                         Status
-----------                         ------
Last Connection                     V34
Initial Transmit Carrier Rate       33600
...
Call Duration                       0:00:10
Call Termination Cause              Local hangup
\end{verbatim}
The digital modem cannot measure the digital loss or the robbed-bit
signalling of the line.  Thus, on the \std{SIP} line of \texttt{dmodemd},
these two lines are not there.  The analogue modem (\texttt{dmodemd -a},
\texttt{--usb}) measures them.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusGMI}{+GMI, +GMM, +GMR}{Identification}
These commands give the manufacturer (\rc{D-Modem project}), the model
(\cmd{D-Modem}) and the revision (\rc{1.0.0}, the version of the build).
With \cmdr{?} after them (\cmdl[plusGMI]{AT+GMI?}), they give the same
data.  With \cmdr{=?} after them (\cmdl[plusGMI]{AT+GMI=?}), they give
no data.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusGSN}{+GSN}{Serial Number}
This command gives the serial number of the modem.  The modem uses the
first value that it has, in this sequence:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The serial number that the line gives.
\item The \texttt{--serial} option of \texttt{dmodemd}.
\item The name of the modem (for example, \rc{dmodem0}).
\end{enumerate}
With \cmdr{?} after it, it gives the same data.  With \cmdr{=?} after
it, it gives no data.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusGOI}{+GOI}{Global Object Identifier}
This command gives the global object identifier (ITU-T X.208) of the
modem, if the line gives one.  D-Modem does not have an identifier of
its own.  Thus, usually the command gives no data.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusGCAP}{+GCAP}{Capabilities}
This command lists the command sets of the modem: \rc{+GCAP:
+FCLASS,+MS}.  With \cmdr{=?} after it, it gives no data.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{hashUD}{\#UD}{Unimodem Diagnostics}
This command gives the statistics of the last call (or of the call that
is connected), with the values of \cmdl[I]{ATI11}.  It uses the format of
the Unimodem Diagnostics Command Reference Specification (version~1.0) of
Microsoft, for the modem log of Windows and the programs that read it.
For each key that has a value, it gives a line \rc{DIAG <2A4D3263
\p{key}=\p{value}>}.  The key and the value are in hexadecimal.  Before
the first call: \rc{0=10}, \rc{1=0} and \rc{60=1}.  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} clears
the log.  If \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} ended a call, the log then has only these
values: \rc{0=10}, \rc{1=0} and \rc{60=52} (the reset of the \std{DTE}).
Some measurements are only in some modulations (the \std{CM} and \std{JM}
of \std{V.8}, the \std{NMSE}, the far echo, the \std{INFO} bits of
\std{V.34}).  For the other modulations, the modem does not give them.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\#UD Keys}
  {\thd{Key} & \thd{Value}}
0 & The format (10). \\ \hline
1 & The setup of the call. \\ \hline
2, 3 & The mode of the call (data or fax) and the mode of the DTE interface (async, or
  the transparent or framed synchronous mode of \std{V.80}). \\ \hline
4, 5 & The \std{CM} and \std{JM} of \std{V.8}, the octets in quotes. \\ \hline
10--12 & The receive and transmit levels, the noise. \\ \hline
13 & The \std{NMSE} (the error of the receiver divided by the smallest
  distance between the points, in hundredths). \\ \hline
14--17 & The loss of the echoes, the delay of the far echo (ms), the round trip
  delay. \\ \hline
18 & The \std{INFO} bits of \std{V.34} (its Table~5). \\ \hline
20, 21 & The modulation (the codes of \std{V.58}). \\ \hline
22--27 & The symbol rates, the carriers, the first rates. \\ \hline
30 & The losses of the carrier (20~ms or more, after which the carrier came back). \\ \hline
31--33 & The renegotiations, the retrains that the modems asked for and accepted. \\ \hline
34, 35 & The last rates. \\ \hline
40, 41 & The error control and its frame size. \\ \hline
42, 43 & The timeouts of the error control and its \std{REJ}s or \std{SREJ}s (\std{MNP}: the
  acknowledgements that ask for frames again). \\ \hline
44, 45 & The compression (the codes of \std{V.58}.  80 \std{MNP~5}, 81 \std{V.44}, 82 zstd) and
  the dictionary size of \std{V.42bis}. \\ \hline
50, 51 & The flow control (\cmdl[ampH]{\&H}). \\ \hline
52--59 & The characters (54, 55: the characters that the modem lost
  because a buffer was full) and the frames. \\ \hline
60 & How the call ended (\p{callCleared} of \std{V.58}.  2 while the call continues). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\example{Example output: AT\#UD}
\begin{verbatim}
DIAG <2A4D3263 0=10>
DIAG <2A4D3263 1=7>
DIAG <2A4D3263 10=0C>
...
\end{verbatim}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusTMO}{+TMO}{V.59 Diagnostics}
This command gives the managed objects of \std{V.59}: the data about the
last call (or about the call that is connected).  The data stays from the
start of the call until the next call starts.  \std{V.250} uses
\cmd{+TMO} to read these objects.  Each object has a name and a
four-digit hexadecimal tag.  The first two digits identify its
high-level object (\cmd{V34Diag} 0C, \cmd{CnxDiag} 2C, \ldots).  The
last two digits identify the object in it.  If an object does not apply
to the last call, it is \rc{NULL}.  You do not have to type the spaces
between the parts.  The modem accepts upper case and lower case.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{After the objects asked for.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a name or tag that the modem does not have, or \cmd{+TMO}
  alone before a different \cmd{+TMO}.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|T{0.3\textwidth}|L{0.56\textwidth}|}{+TMO Forms}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
+TMO & The last \cmd{+TMO} again. \\ \hline
+TMO \p{level}=?, +TMO \p{level}N=? & The objects that are available, as
  tags (\cmd{N}: as names).  0 all, 1 the high-level objects, 2 the
  mid-level objects, 3 the low-level objects.  4 gives \rc{+TMO: (0,1)}
  (names and tags). \\ \hline
+TMO \p{tag} & A four-digit tag: that object, as \rc{\p{tag}\{\p{value}\}}.
  Two digits: all objects of that high-level object. \\ \hline
+TMO \p{name} [All] & An object by name, or all objects of a high-level object
  (\cmd{V34Diag}, or only \cmd{V34}), as \rc{\p{name}\{\p{value}\}}.  If you
  give only the name of an object of a modulation, the modem uses the
  modulation of the last call. \\ \hline
+TMO \p{high} \p{name} & The object of that high-level object
  (\cmd{+TMO V34 rxDataHistory}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{+TMO High-Level Objects}
  {\thd{Tag} & \thd{Objects}}
00 & \cmd{ModemIdentity}: the manufacturer, model, version, the version of
  \std{V.59} (1), the functions of the modem (a bit string, in hexadecimal). \\ \hline
01 & \cmd{ModemCapability}: the mode of each modulation and protocol
  (0 not there, 1 the preferred, 2 enabled, 3 disabled), and the other
  modulations and protocols. \\ \hline
02 & \cmd{ModeSelected}: the modulation of the call. \\ \hline
07 & \cmd{V8Diag}: the \std{CM} and \std{JM} of \std{V.8} (their octets in hexadecimal), its result. \\ \hline
09--23 & The objects of each modulation: its mode, the transmit and receive levels and
  the noise (dBm0), the symbol rates and carriers (the codes of \std{V.59}),
  the rates during the call, the echoes, the round trip delay, and the
  robbed bits, digital loss and \std{G.711} law of \std{V.90} and \std{V.92}. \\ \hline
26 & \cmd{NSMDiag}: a modulation for which \std{V.59} has no object (K56flex, \ldots). \\ \hline
2C & \cmd{CnxDiag}: the carriers, carrier losses, renegotiations,
  retrains, the length of the call, and if this modem made the call. \\ \hline
2E & \cmd{CallProgress}: the last step of the call, the cause of its end (the codes of \std{V.59}). \\ \hline
2F & \cmd{ErrorControl}: the protocol, its frame sizes and windows, its frames and errors, the throughput. \\ \hline
30 & \cmd{Compression}: the agreed compression in each direction, its dictionary and string sizes. \\ \hline
31 & \cmd{DTEDCE}: the flow control, framing, rate, throughput and the characters that the modem lost. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\example{Example: AT+TMO V34 txDataHistory, AT+TMO 2E}
\begin{verbatim}
txDataHistory{33600;31200}
OK
2E01{40}
2E02{90}
OK
\end{verbatim}
\end{command}


\begin{command}{ampT}{\&T\p{n}}{Loop Tests}
This command does the loop tests of \std{V.54}, with the Hayes numbers.
\begin{itemize}
\item Loop 3 operates offline on a leased line (\cmdl[ampL]{\&L}) with a
  4-wire modulation (\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=V29}, \cmd{V33} or \cmd{V27TC}).
  The signal of the modem goes back into its own receiver and the line
  is silent.
\item To get loop 2 at the far end, the modem sends a request in band
  (the \std{PREP}, \std{ACK} and \std{TERM} of \std{V.54}).  Send this
  request in online command state, on a call with no error correction
  (\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N0}).
\item The self-test sends and examines the 511-bit pattern of \std{V.52}.
\end{itemize}
If \sreg{18} is more than 0, a test ends after that number of seconds.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{CONNECT}}{For \cmdl[ampT]{\&T1} and \cmdl[ampT]{\&T6}: the data of the \std{DTE} comes back to it.}
\res{\rc{OK}}{For the other tests.  If \cmdl[ampT]{\&T0} follows \cmdl[ampT]{\&T7} or \cmdl[ampT]{\&T8}, it first gives the bit errors of the self-test, three digits (a maximum of 255).}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For \cmdl[ampT]{\&T2} and for \p{n} more than 8, outside the state that the test must have, or when the far end does not answer \cmdl[ampT]{\&T6} or \cmdl[ampT]{\&T7}.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\&T\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
\&T0 & Ends the test (\cmdl[ampT]{\&T} is the same). \\ \hline
\&T1 & Loop 3, the local analogue loop. \\ \hline
\&T3 & Loop 2 here: the data from the far end goes back to it. \\ \hline
\&T4 & The far end can ask for loop 2. \\ \hline
\&T5 & The far end cannot ask for loop 2 (\emph{default}, also after
  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ}). \\ \hline
\&T6 & Loop 2 at the far end, the remote digital loop. \\ \hline
\&T7 & Loop 2 at the far end with the self-test. \\ \hline
\&T8 & Loop 3 with the self-test. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusT}{+T\ldots}{Loop and Error Rate Tests}
These are the test commands of \std{V.250}, made from the test objects of
\std{V.58}: the loop tests of \cmdl[ampT]{\&T}, an error rate test on
these loops, and their status.  Each command also accepts \cmd{?} (its
value) and \cmd{=?} (its range).
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{When the command is complete.  For \cmd{+TRDL=1}, when the
  far end starts the loop.  For \cmd{+TAL=1}, when loop 3 starts.  These
  two are in online command state (not \rc{CONNECT}: use \cmdl[O]{ATO} for
  the data of the \std{DTE}).  For \cmd{+TRDL=0}, when the \std{TERM} is
  sent.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Outside the state that the test must have, when the far
  end does not start the loop, or for a value that the command does not have.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|T{0.3\textwidth}|L{0.56\textwidth}|}{+T Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
+TERDL=\p{n} & 1: the far end can ask for loop 2 (\cmdl[ampT]{\&T4}).  0: it cannot
  (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
+TLDL=\p{n} & 1: loop 2 here, from online command state (\cmdl[ampT]{\&T3}).  0 ends it. \\ \hline
+TRDL=\p{n} & 1: loop 2 at the far end, from online command state (\cmdl[ampT]{\&T6}).
  0 ends it. \\ \hline
+TAL=\p{n}[,\p{band}] & 1: loop 3, offline on a 4-wire leased line (\cmdl[ampT]{\&T1}).
  0 ends it.  The modem accepts \p{band} but does not use it. \\ \hline
+TTER=\p{type},\p{len},\p{blocks},\p{pattern} & The error rate test on
  the loop that operates (loop 2 at the far end, loop 3), from online
  command state.  It uses the 511-bit pattern of \std{V.52} (\p{pattern}
  1, the only pattern) for \p{blocks} blocks of \p{len} bits (1--65535
  each).  \p{type} 1 bit errors, 2 block errors, 3 the two (the modem
  counts the two).  \cmd{+TTER=0} stops the test.  \cmd{+TTER?} gives the
  type of the test (0 none), \p{len}, the blocks that remain and the
  pattern. \\ \hline
+TNUM? & The bit errors and block errors of the last error rate test, \rc{+TNUM: \p{bits},\p{blocks}}
  (a maximum of 65535). \\ \hline
+TALS? & Loop 3: 0 none, 3 in operation. \\ \hline
+TDLS? & Loop 2 here: 1 none, 3 from this modem (\cmd{+TLDL}, \cmdl[ampT]{\&T3}), 4 from the far end. \\ \hline
+TRDLS? & Loop 2 at the far end: 1 none, 4 in operation. \\ \hline
+TSELF=\p{n} & The self-test (0 full, only offline.  1 partial): the
  pattern generator, the checker and the recognisers of the loop tests
  examine themselves. \\ \hline
+TRES? & Its result: 0 none since \cmdl[Z]{ATZ}, 1 pass, 2 fail. \\ \hline
+TE140, +TE141, +TEPDL, +TEPAL, +TMODE, +TADR & Only 0: no circuits 140
  and 141, no front panel, point to point, no \std{V.54} address. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}


\begin{command}{pctE}{\%E\p{n}}{Line Quality Monitor}
The modem keeps the value in \sreg{39}.  Its modulations retrain and
renegotiate their rate automatically.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--2.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\section{Dialing, Answering and Disconnection}\label{sec:dial}

\begin{command}{D}{D\p{string}}{Dial}
This command sets the modem off hook, dials the number in \p{string},
and then makes the call.
\begin{itemize}
\item The modem dials the digits and \cmd{* \# A B C D} as \std{DTMF}.
  Each digit is \sreg{11} ms long, with \sreg{11} ms between the digits.
\item \cmdl[T]{T} and \cmdl[P]{P} select tone or pulse dialing
  (\sreg{16}) for the remainder of the call.
\item The modem dials pulses on an analogue line (\texttt{-a} and
  \texttt{--usb} of \texttt{dmodemd}).  It uses the break, the make, the
  gap between digits and the digit pattern of the country
  (\cmdl[plusGCI]{+GCI}).  It ignores \cmd{* \# A B C D}.  On a line
  that cannot send pulses, for example \std{SIP}, the modem dials them in
  \std{DTMF}.
\item With \cmdl[X]{X2} or \cmdl[X]{X4}, the modem first waits for a dial
  tone.
\item In \texttt{dmodemd}, the dial string is the number of the \std{SIP}
  call (\hyperref[sec:sip]{Section~\ref*{sec:sip}}).
\end{itemize}

\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.12\textwidth}|L{0.74\textwidth}|}{Dial Modifiers}
  {\thd{Modifier} & \thd{Function}}
0--9 * \# A--D & The modem dials these as \std{DTMF}.  With \cmdl[P]{P}, it dials the digits as pulses. \\ \hline
L & Dial the last number again (first \rc{Dialing \p{number}...}).
  Use it only as the first character of the string.  \rc{ERROR} while
  the modem holds a call after \cmdl[D]{;}. \\ \hline
T & Tone dialing (\std{DTMF}) from this point. \\ \hline
P & Pulse dialing from this point. \\ \hline
, & Pause for \sreg{8} seconds. \\ \hline
W & Wait for a dial tone (1 s of it).  If no dial tone comes in \sreg{7}
  seconds, \rc{NO DIALTONE}. \\ \hline
@ & Wait for the ringing of the far end (a minimum of 0.5 s of
  sound), then for 5 s of silence after it.  If this does not occur in
  \sreg{7} seconds, \rc{NO ANSWER}. \\ \hline
; & End the dial string.  After the modem dials the number, it goes back
  to command state.  It stays off hook, keeps the call and gives
  \rc{OK}.  Then the modem does the remainder of the line. \\ \hline
S=\p{n}, S\p{n} & The stored number \p{n} (0--9, \cmdl[starG]{\&Z\p{n}=}).  Use it
  only as the first character of the string (T or P can come before it).  If
  the slot is empty, \rc{ERROR}.  Later in the string, \rc{ERROR}. \\ \hline
! \$ & Hook flash and bong tone: the modem ignores them (a \std{SIP} call has no hook flash). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}

The modem ignores all other characters.  After a \cmdl[D]{;}, the call
waits, silent, for the \std{DTE}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \cmdl[D]{ATD}\p{more} dials more digits on the call (as
  \std{DTMF}).  A \cmdl[D]{;} holds the call again.
\item \cmdl[D]{ATD} alone or \cmdl[O]{ATO} starts the handshake of the
  calling modem.
\item \cmdl[A]{ATA} starts the handshake of the answering modem.
\item \cmdl[H]{ATH} disconnects the call (\rc{OK}, then \rc{NO CARRIER}).
\end{itemize}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{CONNECT}}{When the call is connected.}
\res{\rc{OK}}{After a \cmdl[D]{;}.}
\res{\rc{BUSY}, \rc{NO ANSWER}, \rc{NO DIALTONE} or \rc{NO CARRIER}}{When the call does not connect.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For an empty stored number, for \cmdl[D]{S=}\p{n} after the start of the string, or for \cmdl[D]{L} with no number to dial.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{P}{P}{Select Pulse Dialing}
This command sets pulse dialing for the next dial (\sreg{16}\cmdr{=0}).
Refer to \cmdl[D]{ATD}.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{T}{T}{Select Tone Dialing}
This command sets tone (\std{DTMF}) dialing for the next dial
(\sreg{16}\cmdr{=1}, the \emph{default}).  Refer to \cmdl[D]{ATD}.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampP}{\&P\p{n}}{Pulse Dial Make/Break Ratio}
The modem keeps the value in \sreg{28}.  The table of the country sets
the pulse times.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--3.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{A}{A}{Answer}
This command sets the modem off hook to answer a call.  After \sreg{92}
seconds, the modem sends the tones of the answering modem.  In
\texttt{dmodemd}, the modem answers the call that rings (\rc{RING}).  If
the modem holds a call after the \cmdl[D]{;} of a dial string,
\cmdl[A]{ATA} answers that call.  Thus,
\cmdl[D]{ATD}\p{number}\cmdl[D]{;}\cmdl[A]{A} dials and then answers (the
call-back of \std{V.250}).
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{CONNECT}}{When the call is connected.}
\res{\rc{NO CARRIER}}{When the call does not connect.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{If there is no call to answer.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{H}{H\p{n}}{Hook Control}
This command sets the modem on hook or off hook.  \cmdl[H]{H} or
\cmdl[H]{H0} disconnects a call.  In voice mode, \cmdl[H]{ATH} releases
the line and goes back to data mode
(\hyperref[sec:voice]{Section~\ref*{sec:voice}}).
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a different number, or when the hook cannot change.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{H\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
H0 & On hook (disconnect). \\ \hline
H1 & Off hook. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{O}{O\p{n}}{Return to On-Line Data Mode}
This command sets the modem online again after the escape sequence
(\hyperref[sec:online]{Section~\ref*{sec:online}}).  If the modem holds a
call after the \cmdl[D]{;} of a dial string, the command starts the
handshake of the calling modem (as \cmdl[D]{ATD}).  \cmdl[O]{O1} and
\cmdl[O]{O3} operate in \std{V.34}, \std{V.90}, \std{V.92}, \std{V.32bis}
and \std{V.32terbo} calls (\cmdl[O]{O3} of \std{V.32} retrains).  In the
other calls, they only go back online.  \cmdl[I]{ATI11} counts a retrain
or a renegotiation from this command as one of the local modem.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{CONNECT}}{When the modem is back online.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{When there is no call, or for \p{n} = 2 or more than 3.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{O\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
O0 & Back online (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
O1 & Back online, then retrain (the \std{V.250} function). \\ \hline
O3 & Back online, then renegotiate the rate (the Rockwell function). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{Y}{Y\p{n}}{Long Space Disconnect}
Other modems use \cmdl[Y]{Y} for long space disconnect.  The modem keeps
the value in \sreg{161} and does nothing more.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--3.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{pctB}{\%B, *B}{Blacklisted Numbers}
These commands list the numbers that the modem must not dial.  The modem
keeps no numbers.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{starG}{*G\p{n}, *H\p{n}=, *HS=, *V, \&Z\p{n}=}{ZyXEL Security and Stored Numbers}
These commands are the type 2 security of ZyXEL (the U-1496 and the Elite
2864).  When the modem answers a call with \cmdr{*G3} to \cmdr{*G5}, it
sends \rc{PassWord:} to the far end after \rc{CONNECT}.  The modem echoes each
character from the far end as \rc{*}.  \sreg{3} ends the password.  The data
does not go through, in the two directions, until the password is correct.
If the password is wrong, the modem sends \rc{Error..., try again} and the
prompt again.  After the third wrong password, the modem disconnects
(\cmdl[I]{ATI6}: \rc{Security check}).
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.18\textwidth}|L{0.68\textwidth}|}{ZyXEL Security}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
*G0 & No security (the \emph{default}). \\ \hline
*G1, *G2 & Type 1 of ZyXEL (only from ZyXEL to ZyXEL): \rc{ERROR}. \\ \hline
*G3 & The password, then \rc{Correct} and the data. \\ \hline
*G4 & The password and \rc{Correct}.  Then the modem disconnects, and after
  2 s it calls the stored number of the slot of the password. \\ \hline
*G5 & The password, then \rc{Call back phone number:}.  The caller types a
  maximum of 26 characters (\sreg{5} erases one) and \sreg{3}.  Then the modem
  disconnects, and after 2 s it calls that number. \\ \hline
*H\p{n}=\p{password} & User password \p{n} (0--9): a maximum of 8 characters,
  case-sensitive, with no comma.  No characters make the slot empty.  Put the
  password in quotes, or the modem uses the remainder of the line.  If a
  supervisory password is set, use \cmdr{*H}\p{n}\cmdr{=}\p{super}\cmdr{,}\p{password}. \\ \hline
*HS=\p{password} & The supervisory password.  If one is set, use
  \cmdr{*HS=}\p{old}\cmdr{,}\p{password}. \\ \hline
*V, *V=\p{super} & The table: \cmdr{*G}\p{n}, then the number, the password
  and the stored number of each slot that is in use.  If a supervisory
  password is set, use \cmdr{*V=}\p{super}. \\ \hline
\&Z\p{n}=\p{number} & Stored number \p{n} (0--9), for \cmdl[D]{ATDS=}\p{n}
  and \cmdr{*G4}.  Put the number in quotes, or the modem uses the remainder of the line. \\ \hline
\&Z\p{n}? & Stored number \p{n}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\cmdr{*H0} to \cmdr{*H2} without \cmdr{=} are still the \std{MNP~10} command
(\cmdl[mnp10]{*H}).  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} keeps the passwords, the stored numbers and
\cmdr{*G}.  It also stops a call back that is pending.  \cmdl[H]{ATH} also stops it.
The modem does not store these settings.  Put them in the init string.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{When the value is valid (and the supervisory password is correct).}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{CK}{CK<\p{key}>}{ZyXEL Encryption Key}
This command sets the key of the ZyXEL encryption (\sreg{102}, the Elite
2864I).  The encryption operates only on \std{X.75} and \std{V.120} calls
on an \std{ISDN} B channel (\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=CLEAR}).  The key is all the
characters after \cmd{<}, up to \cmd{>} or the end of the line.  The
characters are case-sensitive.  The modem removes the spaces from the
command line, thus a key cannot have spaces.  The modem makes two 8-octet
registers from the key, as the Elite does.  The two modems must have the
same key when \sreg{102} bit 2 is 1, or when one of the two modems has
bit 2.  Without \cmd{<}, the command gives \rc{ERROR}.  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} keeps
the key.  The modem does not store it.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{With \cmd{<}.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Without \cmd{<}.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{hashCID}{\#CID, +VCID}{Caller ID}
This command sets caller ID on or off.  When caller ID is on, the modem
tells the \std{DTE} who calls.  After the first \rc{RING}, it sends the
date and time, the number of the caller and, if available, the name of
the caller.  Each item is on a separate line.  \cmdl[hashCID]{+VCID} is
a different name for the same command.

Caller ID operates on a \std{SIP} call and on a telephone line.  If the
caller does not show the number or the name, the modem sends \rc{P}
(private) in its position.  If the number or the name is not known, the
modem sends \rc{O} (out of area).  Value 2 sends all of the caller ID
message in hexadecimal.  Use this value for programs that decode the
message themselves.

\begin{syntax}
\item AT\#CID=\p{n} (or AT+VCID=\p{n})
\item AT\#CID?
\item AT\#CID=? (gives \rc{0,1,2})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--2.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\#CID=\p{n} Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Function}}
0 & Off (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
1 & The fields: \rc{DATE = }, \rc{TIME = }, \rc{NMBR = }, \rc{NAME = },
  a line each. \\ \hline
2 & All of the message in hexadecimal: \rc{MESG = }. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\example{Example output: AT\#CID=1, then a call}
\begin{verbatim}
RING

DATE = 1030
TIME = 1043
NMBR = +15551234
NAME = Alice Smith

RING
\end{verbatim}
\par\needspace{4\baselineskip}Technical data:
\begin{itemize}
\item \rc{DATE} is the month and day (MMDD).  \rc{TIME} is the hour and
  minute (HHMM).
\item In voice mode (\cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=8}), the lines come as
  the complex event report of \std{V.253}, between \cmd{<DLE>X} and
  \cmd{<DLE>.}.  In the other modes, they come after an empty line.
\item On a telephone line, the modem listens for caller ID after the
  first ring (the procedure of North America, and of \std{ETSI} after a
  short ring).  The modem can hear some lines while it is on hook
  (\std{ALSA}, a pipe, the pico-serial Line).  On these lines, it also
  listens before the ring (the procedure of \std{ETSI} after its
  alerting tone, the procedure of the UK, \std{DTMF} caller ID).
\item It accepts Bell 202 \std{FSK} and \std{V.23} \std{FSK} (\std{ETSI}
  EN 300 659-1, BT SIN 227).  It also accepts \std{DTMF} caller ID:
  \rc{A} and the number, or \rc{B} (or \rc{D}) and the reason that the
  number is not there (\cmd{10} private, as \rc{P}.  Other reasons
  \rc{O}), up to \rc{C}, \cmd{\#} or a pause.
\item In voice mode, when the modem has the line, it also accepts
  Type~II caller ID: the caller of a waiting call.  After the \std{CPE}
  alerting signal (2130 and 2750~Hz), it answers with \std{DTMF} \rc{A}
  (the procedure of GR-30, a \std{CPE} without \std{ADSI}).  Then it gives
  the \std{FSK} message that follows as a packet.  During this time, the
  audio of the line to the \std{DTE} is silent.
\item A \std{SIP} call (\texttt{dmodemd} \cmd{-L}) gets its caller ID
  from the INVITE: the P-Asserted-Identity of the caller, or else its
  From.  The user part of the \std{URI} is \rc{NMBR} and the display name
  is \rc{NAME}.  The date and time come from the host.  If the number or
  the name is not shown (the \cmd{id}, \cmd{user} or \cmd{header} of the
  Privacy header, or an anonymous caller), the modem sends \rc{P}.  With
  value 2, the modem sends the \std{MDMF} message that it made from these
  items.
\end{itemize}
\end{command}

\section{Speaker, Line and Country}\label{sec:line}

\begin{command}{L}{L\p{n}}{Speaker Volume}
This command sets the volume of the speaker (\sreg{30}).  A \std{SIP} line
has no speaker: the modem only keeps the setting.  A SmartUSB56 with
\texttt{dmodemd --usb-buzzer} operates its buzzer while the speaker is
on, at all volumes except \cmdl[L]{L0}.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--3.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{L\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
L0 & Off. \\ \hline
L1 & Low volume. \\ \hline
L2 & Medium volume. \\ \hline
L3 & High volume (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{M}{M\p{n}}{Speaker Control}
This command sets when the speaker is on (\sreg{29}).
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--3.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{M\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
M0 & Speaker always off. \\ \hline
M1 & Speaker on until the call is connected (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
M2 & Speaker always on. \\ \hline
M3 & As \cmdl[M]{M1} (on other modems: not during dialing). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampJ}{\&J\p{n}}{Auxiliary Relay}
The modem accepts this command, but it changes nothing: there is no
relay.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampL}{\&L\p{n}}{Leased Line}
This command sets the type of the line (\sreg{47}): a dialed line, or a
leased pair or circuit with no exchange.  A leased line has no dial
tone, no ringing and no hook.
\begin{itemize}
\item On a leased line, \cmdl[D]{ATD} (with a number or with no number)
  makes the call immediately.  The modem only listens for the answer
  tone.
\item \cmdl[A]{ATA} answers.
\item If \sreg{0} is more than 0, the modem answers automatically, with
  no ring, when it is idle.
\item If the line ends a call (\rc{NO CARRIER}), the call starts again
  automatically after 2~s.  The modem dials or answers as before.
  \cmdl[H]{ATH}, \std{DTR}, \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} or \cmdl[ampL]{\&L0} stop this
  until the next command line.
\end{itemize}

All voice band modulations of \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} operate on
\cmdl[ampL]{\&L1}.  On \cmdl[ampL]{\&L2}, the 4-wire modulations
(\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=V29}, \cmd{V33}, \cmd{V27TC}) operate as duplex pumps
on a continuous carrier.  Each end starts immediately with its own
synchronizing signal.  The other modulations operate as on
\cmdl[ampL]{\&L1}.

With \sreg{48}\cmdr{=1}, on a call with no error correction, the modem
does the simulated carrier control of \std{V.13}.  Between the bursts of
data from the \std{DTE}, the line carries the idle pattern of \std{V.13}.
The far end uses this pattern as carrier detect OFF.  Each burst sets it
to ON again.

A line at 48000 Hz or more (\cmd{-r 96000} of \texttt{dmodemd}) is
always leased.  Its mode is \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=VWB}.  Its start and its
retrains take a maximum of \sreg{7} seconds.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--2.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\&L\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
\&L0 & A dialed line (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\&L1 & A 2-wire leased line. \\ \hline
\&L2 & A 4-wire leased line. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusGCI}{+GCI}{Country of Installation}
This command sets the country with its \std{T.35} code in hexadecimal
(\rc{+GCI:b5} is the USA).  The country sets its dial pause (\sreg{8})
and hook flash (\sreg{24}) times, its pulse dialing and its tones.

The \cmd{-c} option of \texttt{dmodemd} sets the country at the start
(\cmd{--countrylist} lists the countries).  You can use a country of the
table of the modem.  You can also use one of the 192 countries of the
list of the tones of national networks of the \std{ITU} (after
\std{E.180}).  Use its name from that list in capital letters, or its
name in the table (\cmd{-c FIJI}, \cmd{-c UNITED\_KINGDOM}: \cmd{UK}).
The countries of the table have their own dialing settings.  The other
countries use the settings of the USA (\cmdl[I]{ATI7}: \rc{Country: FIJI
(USA line)}).  All countries have their own busy and congestion tones.
\cmdl[Z]{ATZ} does not change the country.
\begin{syntax}
\item +GCI=\p{h}
\item +GCI? (gives \rc{+GCI:b5})
\item +GCI=? (lists the codes)
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a code that is not in the table.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\section{Modulation}\label{sec:modulation}

\begin{command}{plusMS}{+MS}{Modulation Selection}
This command sets these items:
\begin{itemize}
\item The modulation that the modem uses to call or to answer.
\item If the modem can use a slower modulation (\p{automode} 1, as
  \cmdl[N]{N1}.  \sreg{31}).  With \p{automode} 0, the \std{V.8}
  CM and JM of the modem give only the modulation of \p{mod}, on all
  lines: no \std{V.32bis}, \std{V.22bis}, \std{V.23} or \std{V.21}.
  K56flex (56) and x2 (57) then do not offer \std{V.90}.
\item The rates at which the modem can connect, \p{min} to \p{max}
  bit/s.
\end{itemize}
\p{mod} is a number or its \std{V.250} name, in upper case or lower case
(\cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS=V34} is \cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS=34}).  If you do not
give a parameter, it keeps its value: \cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS=,0} only sets
automode off.  In \std{V.34}, \p{max} is the limit of the rates in the two
directions (the modem sends it in its \std{MP} sequences).
\begin{syntax}
\item +MS=[\p{mod}][,[\p{automode}][,[\p{min}][,[\p{max}]]]]
\item +MS? (gives \rc{92,1,300,56000}.  \cmd{200,1,300,56000} at 48000
  Hz or more)
\item +MS=?\\
  gives \rc{(21,22,23,26,126,226,27,29,229,122,32,132,232,168,}\allowbreak
\rc{128,61,33,34,103,18,45,110,5,12,212,201,202,208,}\allowbreak
\rc{56,90,91,92,150,200,255,64),(0,1),}\allowbreak
\rc{(300-56000),}\allowbreak
\rc{(300-56000)}:
  the modulations of the program
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a modulation that the modem does not have, \p{automode} more than 1, \p{min} more than \p{max}, or a rate outside \std{300} to \std{56000}.}
\end{results}
