\paragraph{The 4-Wire Modulations.}  \std{V.29}, \std{V.33}, \std{V.27ter},
\std{V.26} and Bell 201, 202 and 208 are the modulations for a 4-wire
leased line (\cmdl[ampL]{\&L2}).  Each end sends on its own pair, on a
carrier that stays on.
\begin{itemize}
\item \std{V.26} and Bell 201 send their data with no scrambler, as those
  modems do.  They use the phase changes of alternative A or B of
  \std{V.26}.
\item Bell 208 sends the 4800 bit/s signal of \std{V.27ter}.  \std{V.27}
  took this modulation from Bell 208.
\item Bell 202 is 1200~Hz mark and 2200~Hz space at 1200 bit/s in each
  direction.
\item The \std{DPSK} modulations start with the training of \std{V.27ter}
  as their synchronizing signal.  A different D-Modem must have this
  signal.  If an old modem of these types does not send this signal, the
  modem does not find it.
\end{itemize}
\p{max} sets the rate at which each end sends.  \p{min} sets the lowest
rate that it can use.  A receiver finds the rate of the far end
automatically.  If an end is faster than the far end, it decreases to
the rate of the far end.  If an end loses its equalization two times in
20~s at its own rate, it decreases to the next lower rate.  If a receiver
loses its equalization, it sends its synchronizing signal again.  Then
it gets the signal of the far end again (\std{V.29} 10, \std{V.33} 9).

\paragraph{Point of Sale.}\label{sec:pos}  Card terminals and vending
machines dial their host.  They use two short start procedures that no
Recommendation gives.  Thus, a transaction of some hundred octets takes
some seconds, not ten.
\begin{itemize}
\item \emph{Fast connect} (\sreg{49}=1) makes the handshake of
  \std{V.22}, Bell 212A and \std{V.22bis} shorter.  The answering modem
  sends a short answer tone (\sreg{50}, 400~ms by default).  The calling
  modem answers it immediately with its scrambled ones (with
  \std{V.22bis}, its S1 when it hears the unscrambled ones).  Each side
  starts the data 150~ms after 50~ms of the signal of the other side.
  The connection is complete in about 0.9~s from the answer
  (\std{V.22bis}, 1.4~s), not 5~s.  \sreg{49}=3 sends HDLC flags,
  scrambled, in place of the scrambled ones at 1200 bit/s.  Some hosts
  must have these flags.  The modem accepts the two types from the far
  end.
\item \emph{V.29 FastPOS} (\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=V29FP}) is \std{V.29} half
  duplex.  The calling modem sends a steady 980~Hz calling tone.  The
  answering modem sends a 2225~Hz answer tone (\sreg{50}).  Then the two
  modems give \rc{CONNECT}.  After this, the side whose DTE has data
  sends it in a burst, with the \std{V.29} training first.  The other
  side listens.  The turn changes when the data stops (32 idle bits:
  marks or HDLC flags).  The DTEs take turns, as the SDLC of a terminal
  and its host does.  V.29 has no rate signal: the two ends must have
  the same \p{max}.
\end{itemize}
The two types are for synchronous DTEs.  Use the framed sub-mode of
\std{V.80} (\cmdl[plusES]{+ES=6,,8}, with the FCS:
\cmdl[plusESA]{+ESA=0,0,0,,1}) or \cmd{\bs N0} and the framing of the DTE.
The two ends must have the same setting.  If a modem with the usual
handshake gets such a call, the call connects late or does not connect.
Refer to \texttt{docs/dsp/fastpos.md}.

\paragraph{V.90, \std{V.91} and \std{V.92}.}  \std{V.90} and \std{V.92}
are available as the \emph{digital} modem (the side of the server, as on
a \std{G.711} VoIP line), on an 8000 Hz \std{G.711} line.  Analogue
\std{V.90} and \std{V.92} modems that call can connect at up to
\std{56000} bit/s downstream.  When the modem answers, 90 and 92 also
offer \std{V.91}, which two digital modems use, and \std{V.34} for the
other modems.  On a line at a
different sample rate, they are \std{V.34}.  On a digital line, they are
also available as the \emph{analogue} modem (refer to
\hyperref[sec:digitalclient]{the client on a digital line}).  With automode, \std{V.8}
and the \std{V.34} modem change to \std{V.32bis} (and its \std{V.22bis}
and \std{V.22}) for a modem without \std{V.34}.  For a modem without
\std{V.8}, the answering modem tries \std{V.23} and Bell 103.

\paragraph{On an Analogue Line.}\label{sec:analogue}  On an analogue
telephone line (\texttt{-a} and \texttt{--usb} of \texttt{dmodemd}), the
modem is the analogue end of the call.  \std{V.90} and \std{V.92} operate
as the analogue modem with a \std{V.90} or \std{V.92} server (at 9600~Hz.
Refer to \texttt{docs/hardware.md}).  \std{V.91} and \cmd{CLEAR} must
have a digital line, thus they operate as \std{V.34}.

\paragraph{The Client on a Digital Line.}\label{sec:digitalclient}  On a
digital line (\std{SIP}, or the line of a telephone system), the modem
can also be the analogue end of the call, with no modem hardware.  It
calls the 56k server of a provider with \std{V.92}, \std{V.90} (90 and
92), K56flex (56) or x2 (57).  When the modem calls, its \std{V.8} CM
gives only the mode of \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}: the two ends of \std{V.90}
or \std{V.92} without \std{V.91}, or \std{V.91} with \cmd{+MS=V91}.  When it
answers, it offers the two ends and \std{V.91}, and the CM selects one:
\begin{itemize}
\item Against a server, the modem is the analogue modem.
\item Against an analogue modem, the modem is the digital modem.
\item Against another D-Modem that calls with \cmd{+MS=V91}, the two
  modems use \std{V.91}.
\item If the two modems offer the two ends, the calling modem is the
  analogue modem.
\end{itemize}
\sreg{117} selects the end on a digital line.
The path must be \std{G.711} from end to end, with no transcoding.  The
quick connect of \std{V.92} is not used as the analogue modem on a
digital line.  Refer to \texttt{docs/dsp/sip-client.md}.

\paragraph{V.cvm.}  \cmd{VCVM} is the proposed Recommendation of
\cmd{docs/v\_cvm.pdf}.  It is for calls whose speech path uses a mobile
codec (\std{AMR}, AMR-WB).  These calls cannot carry \std{V.34} or
\std{V.32}.  The call starts with \std{V.8}, with \std{V.22} and
\std{V.21} as the fallback.  Then the modem probes the codec and loads a
mode for it.  The start takes 15 to 21~s.  Make sure that \sreg{7} is
sufficiently long for it.  The connection uses \std{LAPM}.  The modem
also accepts \cmd{V\_CVM}, its old name.

\paragraph{V.wb.}  \cmd{VWB} is the mode of D-Modem for a dry copper pair
between two D-Modem modems (the WB stick of pico-serial, a codec at 96
kHz).  It uses discrete multitone.  The calling modem uses the lower
tones and the answering modem uses the upper tones.  The modem loads
each tone from its measured \std{SNR}.  Refer to \cmd{docs/dsp/vwb.md}.
It operates only at 48000 Hz or more.  There, it is the only mode and the
\emph{default} (\cmdl[Z]{ATZ} sets it again), as a leased line
(\cmdl[ampL]{\&L1}).  The rates of \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} do not limit it.

\paragraph{Clear Channel.}  \cmd{CLEAR} is a clear \std{64} kbit/s digital
channel, for example the data call of an \std{ISDN} B-channel (a \std{SIP}
call in CLEARMODE, \std{RFC} 4040, or a call of a \std{PBX} with an
unrestricted digital bearer).  There is no modulation.  Each octet of the
channel is 8 bits of the link.  \rc{CONNECT 64000} in the two directions.
It starts immediately on \cmdl[D]{ATD} and \cmdl[A]{ATA}, with no call
progress and no answer tone.  The first bit of each octet is its most
significant bit (\std{I.460}, \std{RFC} 4040).  Some lines tell the
modem that the call is clear (the \std{SIP} of \texttt{dmodemd} for a
CLEARMODE call, \cmd{app\_dmodem} for an \std{ISDN} data call).  On these
lines, the modem uses this mode, whatever \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} says.
\cmdl[plusB2]{+B2} sets the protocol on the channel, \std{X.75} by
\emph{default}, or the rate adaption of \std{V.110} with
\cmdl[plusCBST]{+CBST}.  Refer to \cmd{docs/isdn.md}.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusA8E}{+A8E}{V.8 and V.8 bis Controls}
This command gives the controls of \std{V.251} for \std{V.8} and \std{V.8 bis}.
\begin{itemize}
\item \p{v8o} and \p{v8a} are 1: the modem does \std{V.8} itself when it
  calls and when it answers (\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} sets if it does).
\item \p{v8cf} is 0: the \std{DTE} has no call function.
\item \p{v8b} 1 starts a data call with \std{V.8 bis}.  When the modem
  answers, it sends MRe before its answer tone.  If the caller answers
  MRe, the two modems agree on the data mode (CL, MS, ACK).  Then they
  continue with \std{V.8}.  When the modem calls, it answers the MRe of
  the far end in the same way.  If a caller does not have \std{V.8
  bis}, the modem answers with \std{V.8} after 3~s.
\end{itemize}
This applies only to the modulations that start with \std{V.8}
(\std{V.34}, \std{V.90}, \std{V.91}, \std{V.92}, \std{V.cvm}).  It does
not apply to a leased line or a clear channel.  Refer to
\cmd{docs/dsp/v8bis.md}.
\begin{syntax}
\item +A8E=[\p{v8o}][,[\p{v8a}][,[\p{v8cf}][,[\p{v8b}]]]]
\item +A8E? (gives \rc{+A8E: 1,1,0,0})
\item +A8E=? (gives \rc{+A8E: (1),(1),(0),(0,1)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range: \p{v8b} 2 (the \std{V.8 bis}
  of the \std{DTE}, \cmdr{+A8M}), \p{cfrange} and \p{protrange}.}
\end{results}
The \emph{default} \p{v8b} of \std{V.251} is 1.  The \emph{default} of
D-Modem is 0, because an answer with \std{V.8 bis} makes a caller without
it wait about 3.5~s.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusDSVD}{+DSVD}{Voice and Data Together}
This command is from D-Modem.  \p{on} 1 offers the digital simultaneous
voice and data (\std{DSVD}) of \std{V.70} in \std{V.8 bis}
(\p{v8b} 1 of \cmdl[plusA8E]{+A8E}).  The modem selects it if the other
modem also offers it.  The call then continues with \std{V.8} and its
modulation.  The link is the multiplexer of \std{V.76}.  The data of the
\std{DTE} goes on its data channel, which the messages of \std{V.75}
open.  The data channel has error correction and no compression.  The
protocol is then \cmd{V.76} (\cmdl[I]{ATI11}, the \cmd{Protocol:}
report).  Some modems have a voice path (the handset of a SmartUSB56, or
the \cmd{--dsvd-audio} command of dmodemd).  On these modems, a voice
channel opens next to the data.  The speech is coded with \std{G.729}
Annex A.  If the two ends send the audio header of \std{V.75}, silence
is coded with Annex B (comfort noise, and the data gets the line).  If
the far end does not have \std{DSVD}, the call is a usual data call.
Refer to \cmd{docs/v70.md}.
\begin{syntax}
\item +DSVD=[\p{on}]
\item +DSVD? (gives \rc{+DSVD: 0})
\item +DSVD=? (gives \rc{+DSVD: (0,1)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusASVD}{+ASVD}{Analogue Voice and Data Together}
This command is from D-Modem.  \p{on} 1 offers the analogue simultaneous
voice and data (\std{ASVD}) of \std{V.61} in \std{V.8 bis}
(\p{v8b} 1 of \cmdl[plusA8E]{+A8E}).  The modem selects it if the other
modem also offers it.  The call then continues with the start procedure
of \std{V.61}, not with \std{V.8}.  One carrier of \std{3000} (or
\std{2800}) symbols each second carries \std{4800}~bit/s of data, with
the speech around it.  While neither end speaks, it carries only data,
at up to \std{14400}~bit/s.  The data of the \std{DTE} has the error
correction of \std{V.42}, as on all calls.  The speech uses the same
voice path as \cmdl[plusDSVD]{+DSVD} (the handset of a SmartUSB56, or
the \cmd{--dsvd-audio} command of dmodemd).  If there is no voice path,
each frame has only data.  If the two ends also offer
\cmdl[plusDSVD]{+DSVD}, the modem selects \std{DSVD}.  If the far end
does not have \std{ASVD}, the call is a usual data call.
\cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS=V61} uses \std{V.61} without \std{V.8 bis}.  Refer
to \cmd{docs/dsp/v61.md}.
\begin{syntax}
\item +ASVD=[\p{on}]
\item +ASVD? (gives \rc{+ASVD: 0})
\item +ASVD=? (gives \rc{+ASVD: (0,1)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{B}{B\p{n}}{Communication Standard Setting}
\cmdl[B]{ATB} with no number sets \std{V.22} at \std{1200} bit/s only,
without automode.  It is the same as \cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS=22,0,1200,1200}.
Other modems use \cmdl[B]{B0} to \cmdl[B]{B3}, \cmdl[B]{B15} and
\cmdl[B]{B16} to select the \std{ITU} or the Bell modulation at
\std{1200} and \std{300} bit/s.  The modem accepts these commands, but
they change nothing: the negotiation of the call and \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}
select the modulation.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If there is no \p{n}, or \p{n} = 0--3, 15 or 16.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{B\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
B & \std{V.22} at \std{1200} bit/s, no automode. \\ \hline
B0, B1 & Accepted (other modems: \std{V.22} or Bell 212A at \std{1200} bit/s). \\ \hline
B2, B3 & Accepted (other modems: the reverse channel of \std{V.23}). \\ \hline
B15, B16 & Accepted (other modems: \std{V.21} or Bell 103 at \std{300} bit/s). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{N}{N\p{n}}{Automode}
This command sets if the modems can find their best common modulation,
from the modulation that \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} selected and lower
(\sreg{31}).  It is the same as the second parameter of
\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}.  With \cmd{N0}, the \std{V.8} CM and JM give only
the modulation of \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}, on all lines.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{N\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
N0 & Only the modulation that \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} selected. \\ \hline
N1 & Automode: that modulation or the best common modulation below it (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{C}{C\p{n}}{Carrier Control}
The modem accepts \cmdl[C]{C1}, so that software that sends it operates
correctly.  The modem does not have \cmdl[C]{C0}.  On some other modems,
\cmdl[C]{C0} sends no carrier (receive only).
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise (\cmdl[C]{C} alone is \cmdl[C]{C0}).}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{C\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
C0 & Carrier always off (not available). \\ \hline
C1 & Usual carrier operation (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampB}{\&B\p{n}}{V.32 Auto Retrain}
Other modems use \cmdl[ampB]{\&B} for the retrain of \std{V.32} (on
USRobotics modems: a fixed \std{DTE} rate).  The modem accepts this
command, but it changes nothing: its modulations retrain automatically.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--2.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampG}{\&G\p{n}}{V.22bis Guard Tone}
The modem accepts this command, but it changes nothing: the modem sends
no guard tone.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--2.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampN}{\&N\p{n}}{Line Rate}
On USRobotics modems, this command selects the rate of the line.  This
modem accepts it, but it changes nothing.  \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} sets the
rates.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--14.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\subsection{V.cvm}

\begin{command}{plusCVM}{+CVM}{V.cvm Settings}
This command is for \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=VCVM}.  It has these parameters:
\begin{itemize}
\item \p{modes}: the permitted modes, a sum of the values that follow.
\item \p{rate}: the rate of the signal interface, 8000 or 16000.
\item \p{report}: 0 or 1.  1 asks for \rc{+CVMR} at each renegotiation.
\end{itemize}
D-Modem keeps \p{rate} and \p{report}, but it operates only at 8000~Hz,
without the wideband modes.  It sends no \rc{+CVMR}.  During and after a
\std{V.cvm} call, \cmdl[plusCVM]{+CVM?} gives a second line with the
values of that call:
\rc{+CVM: \p{raw tx},}\allowbreak\rc{\p{raw rx},}\allowbreak
\rc{\p{coded tx},}\allowbreak\rc{\p{coded rx},}\allowbreak\rc{\p{symbol ms},}\allowbreak
\rc{\p{tones tx},\p{tones rx},}\allowbreak\rc{\p{code rate},}\allowbreak
\rc{\p{renegotiations},\p{erasures}}
(\rc{+CVM: 1600,1600,}\allowbreak\rc{1400,1400,}\allowbreak\rc{20.0,16,16,}\allowbreak\rc{7/8,0,0}.
The tones are 0 in the parametric mode).  The \rc{CONNECT} of the call
has its coded receive rate and \cmd{/CVM}: \rc{CONNECT 1200/CVM}, with
\cmdl[bsV]{\bs V1} \rc{CONNECT 1200/CVM/LAPM}.  \sreg{44} and \sreg{45}
contain the number of loaded tones and the number of renegotiations.
\begin{syntax}
\item +CVM=[\p{modes}][,[\p{rate}][,[\p{report}]]]
\item +CVM? (gives \rc{+CVM: 63,8000,0})
\item +CVM=? (gives \rc{+CVM: (0-63),(8000,16000),(0,1)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+CVM Modes}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Mode}}
1 & The robust mode. \\ \hline
2 & Narrowband. \\ \hline
4 & Wideband (not available). \\ \hline
8 & 8-DPSK. \\ \hline
16 & The amplitude rings. \\ \hline
32 & The parametric mode. \\ \hline
63 & All modes (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\subsection{V.92 Call Waiting and Modem-on-Hold}

\begin{command}{plusPCW}{+PCW, +PIG}{V.92 Call Waiting and \texorpdfstring{\texttt{PCM}}{PCM} Upstream}
\cmdl[plusPCW]{+PCW} (0 to 2, \emph{default} 2) is the call waiting of
\std{V.92}.  This modem does not detect call waiting.
\cmdl[plusPCW]{+PIG} (0 or 1, \emph{default} 1) is the \std{PCM}
upstream of \std{V.92}, a setting of an analogue modem.  The modem keeps
the two values.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusPMH}{+PMH}{Modem-on-Hold Enable}
This command sets if the far end can put the call on hold.
\begin{syntax}
\item +PMH=\p{n}
\item +PMH? (gives \rc{+PMH: 0})
\item +PMH=? (gives \rc{+PMH: (0-1)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+PMH Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Meaning}}
0 & The far end can put the call on hold (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
1 & The far end cannot put the call on hold. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusPMHT}{+PMHT}{Modem-on-Hold Timer}
This command sets the maximum time of a hold that the modem accepts: the
code of Table~33 of \std{V.92}, 0 to 13.  0 accepts no hold.
\begin{syntax}
\item +PMHT=\p{n}
\item +PMHT? (gives \rc{+PMHT: 5})
\item +PMHT=? (gives \rc{+PMHT: (0-13)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--13.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusPMHR}{+PMHR}{Modem-on-Hold Request}
In online command mode on a \std{V.92} connection, this command asks
the far end to go on hold for \p{s} seconds (1 to 960.  If there is no
\p{s}, 10).
\begin{syntax}
\item +PMHR[=\p{s}]
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Outside online command mode of a \std{V.92} call, or for \p{s} outside 1 to 960.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\section{Error Control}\label{sec:errctl}

\begin{command}{plusES}{+ES}{Error Control Selection}
This command is the error control selection of \std{V.250}.  It sets these
items:
\begin{itemize}
\item \p{orig}: the error control that the modem tries first when it
  calls.
\item \p{orig\_fb}: the error control that can follow if the first one
  fails.
\item \p{ans\_fb}: the error control that the modem accepts when it
  answers.
\end{itemize}
\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N} sets this command.  This command sets \sreg{103} to the
\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N} that has the same values (if no \cmdl[bsN]{\bs N} has
them, \sreg{103} does not change).  The Synchronous Mode and the Frame
Tunnelling Mode of \std{V.80} (5 and 7, 7 and 9) are not available.
\begin{syntax}
\item +ES=[\p{orig}][,[\p{orig\_fb}][,[\p{ans\_fb}]]]
\item +ES? (gives \rc{+ES: 3,0,2})
\item +ES=? (gives \rc{+ES: (0-4,6),(0-4),(0-6,8)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{3}{|C{0.14\textwidth}|C{0.07\textwidth}|L{0.6\textwidth}|}{+ES Values}
  {\thd{Parameter} & \thd{Value} & \thd{Meaning}}
\p{orig} & 0, 1 & No error correction. \\ \hline
 & 2 & \std{V.42} (\std{LAPM}) without the detection phase. \\ \hline
 & 3 & \std{V.42} with the detection phase (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
 & 4 & \std{MNP} first (no detection phase, no \std{LAPM}). \\ \hline
 & 6 & The Synchronous Access Mode of \std{V.80} (\hyperref[sec:v80]{Section~\ref*{sec:v80}}). \\ \hline
\p{orig\_fb} & 0, 1 & None (\emph{default} 0). \\ \hline
 & 2 & \std{V.42} or \std{MNP}, or disconnect. \\ \hline
 & 3 & Only \std{V.42}. \\ \hline
 & 4 & Only \std{MNP} (and first). \\ \hline
\p{ans\_fb} & 0, 1 & No error correction. \\ \hline
 & 2, 3 & \std{V.42}, \std{MNP} or none (\emph{default} 2). \\ \hline
 & 4 & \std{V.42} or \std{MNP}, or disconnect. \\ \hline
 & 5 & Only \std{V.42}. \\ \hline
 & 6 & Only \std{MNP}. \\ \hline
 & 8 & The Synchronous Access Mode of \std{V.80}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampQ}{\&Q\p{n}}{Error Control Mode}
Other modems use this command to select the error correction, as
\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N}.  \cmdl[ampQ]{\&Q} alone is \cmdl[ampQ]{\&Q0}.  The
synchronous modes are not available.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0, 5, 6, 8 or 9.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\&Q\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
\&Q0, \&Q6 & No error correction (\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N0}). \\ \hline
\&Q5, \&Q8, \&Q9 & \std{V.42}, then \std{MNP}, then none (\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N3}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bsN}{\bs N\p{n}}{Error Control Mode}
This command sets the error correction (\sreg{103}), as on the Rockwell
modems.  Each value sets \cmdl[plusES]{+ES} to the equivalent value of
\std{V.250}.  \cmdl[S]{ATS103=}\p{n} is \cmdl[bsN]{\bs N}\p{n}.

The detection phase of \std{V.42} selects \std{LAPM} or no error
correction.  If the answering modem sends no \std{V.42} answer, the
calling modem sends the \std{LR} of \std{MNP}.  If the answering modem
gets an \std{LR} (from a modem with only \std{MNP}, or a modem with this
setting), it starts \std{MNP}.  With only \std{MNP}, the calling modem
starts with its \std{LR}.  \std{MNP~5} compression operates with
\std{MNP} (\cmdl[pctC]{\%C}).  \cmdl[bsA]{\bs A} sets the block size of
\std{MNP}.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--5.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{3}{|C{0.12\textwidth}|L{0.55\textwidth}|C{0.14\textwidth}|}{\bs N\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function} & \thd{+ES}}
\bs N0 & Normal: no error correction. & 1,0,1 \\ \hline
\bs N1 & Direct: no error correction. & 0,0,0 \\ \hline
\bs N2 & Reliable: \std{V.42} (\std{LAPM}) or \std{MNP}, or else disconnect. & 3,2,4 \\ \hline
\bs N3 & Auto-reliable: \std{V.42}, \std{MNP}, or none (\emph{default}). & 3,0,2 \\ \hline
\bs N4 & Only \std{V.42} (\std{LAPM}), or else disconnect. & 3,3,5 \\ \hline
\bs N5 & Only \std{MNP}, or else disconnect. & 4,4,6 \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampM}{\&M\p{n}}{Asynchronous Communications Mode}
The modem accepts this command, but it changes nothing (other modems:
asynchronous mode.  USRobotics modems: error correction, on this modem
\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N}).
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0, 4 or 5.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bsA}{\bs A\p{n}}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{MNP}}{MNP} Block Size}
This command sets the block size of \std{MNP}, the longest information
field that it offers (\sreg{105}).  As on other modems, an \std{LR} offers
64 and the 256-octet data phase optimization.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--3.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\bs A\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
\bs A0 & 64 octets. \\ \hline
\bs A1 & 128 octets. \\ \hline
\bs A2 & 192 octets. \\ \hline
\bs A3 & 256 octets (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{minusSEC}{-SEC}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{MNP}}{MNP} 10EC}
This command is the \std{MNP~10EC} of Rockwell, and its transmit level
(\cmd{1,12}).  The modem keeps the values, \emph{default} 0.
\cmdl[minusSEC]{-SEC?} gives only the values, without the name.
\cmdr{=?} gives \cmd{(0,1),(0-30)}.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{mnp10}{-C, -K, -Q, )M, @M, *H, :T19}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{MNP}}{MNP} 10}
These commands control the \std{MNP~10} of Microcom.  The modem offers
\std{MNP~10} in all \std{MNP} calls (S113 bit 0, \emph{default} on).  The
packets of the link become larger and smaller with the quality of the
line.  The call changes between modulations when the line becomes better
or worse.  If the far end is a D-Modem (S113 bit 1, \emph{default} on),
the call can change between all modulations that the two modems have,
from \std{V.34} and \std{V.32bis} to \std{V.21}.  With other
\std{MNP~10} modems, the call changes only in \std{V.34}, and in
\std{V.32bis}, \std{V.22bis} and \std{V.22}.
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.2\textwidth}|L{0.66\textwidth}|}{\std{MNP~10} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
\cmdl[mnp10]{-K0} & A \std{V.42} call stays \std{V.42} (S107, \emph{default}). \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{-K1}, \cmdl[mnp10]{-K2} & If the far end offers \std{MNP~10} in its \std{XID}, a \std{V.42} call changes to \std{MNP~10}, in \std{HDLC} frames (it loses \std{V.42bis}). \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{-Q0} & No rate below 4800 bit/s (S108). \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{-Q1} & No rate below 2400 bit/s. \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{-Q2} & Down to the lowest rate (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{)M0} & Not cellular (S109, \emph{default}). \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{)M1}, \cmdl[mnp10]{)M2} & Cellular: the transmit level changes with the reports of the far end, and the fallbacks are faster. \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{@M\p{n}} & The start value of the cellular transmit level (S110): \cmd{@M0} 8~dB down, \cmd{@M1} 20~dB, \cmd{@M10} to \cmd{@M30} 0 to 20~dB. \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{*H0} & The call starts at the highest rate (S111, \emph{default}). \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{*H1}, \cmdl[mnp10]{*H2} & The calling modem starts at \std{V.22} (1200 bit/s) or \std{V.32bis} at 4800, and increases the rate. \\ \hline
\cmdl[mnp10]{:T19\p{n}} & 1 (\emph{default}): at 1200 bit/s, disconnect after 120~s with no data from the far end (S112). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
The \std{LR} of the caller is 38 octets or less, because some modems
refuse a longer \std{LR}.  Thus, some \std{MNP~10} parameters are only
in the \std{LR} when they fit.  With \std{V.42bis} offered (S114=1),
the \std{LR} does not offer \std{MNP~10}.  S113 bit 2 (\emph{default}
off) sends the long \std{LR}: all the \std{MNP~10} parameters and the
mark of the originator (parameter 0x11), with no limit.  Set it only
for a modem that must have them.
The modem accepts \cmdl[mnp10]{-C}, but it changes nothing.  The modem
does not have the modulations of \std{MNP~10EC}.  A modem with
\std{MNP~10EC} connects with \std{V.42} or with \std{MNP~10}.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{For \cmdl[mnp10]{-C0}, \cmdl[mnp10]{-C1}, \cmdl[mnp10]{-K0} to \cmdl[mnp10]{-K2}, \cmdl[mnp10]{-Q0} to \cmdl[mnp10]{-Q2}, \cmdl[mnp10]{)M0} to \cmdl[mnp10]{)M2}, \cmdl[mnp10]{@M0} to \cmdl[mnp10]{@M30}, \cmdl[mnp10]{*H0} to \cmdl[mnp10]{*H2}, \cmdl[mnp10]{:T190}, \cmdl[mnp10]{:T191}.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\subsection{ETC (Enhanced Throughput Cellular)}\label{sec:etc}

\std{ETC} is the cellular mode of AT\&T Paradyne.  The 3Com Megahertz PC
Cards (3CXM556 and others) have it.  The firmware of these cards uses
\std{V.32bis} or lower, error control, a transmit filter, and other
timers.  D-Modem uses the parts of this firmware that are known: S116,
\cmdl[ampF]{\&F5}, \cmdl[ampF]{\&F6} and \cmdl[plusEF]{+EF}.

\begin{command}{etc}{\&F5, \&F6, S116}{ETC Settings}
S116 holds the \std{ETC} flags, with the bits of the 3Com firmware
(\emph{default} 0, off).  D-Modem uses these bits:
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.2\textwidth}|L{0.66\textwidth}|}{S116 Bits}
  {\thd{Bit} & \thd{Function}}
0 to 2 & If one of them is set, \std{ETC} is on: \rc{/ETC} follows the error correction in \rc{CONNECT} (with \cmdl[bsV]{\bs V1}), and \cmd{\#UD} gives 82 for error control (key 40). \\ \hline
1 & The cellular side: the caller sends a calling tone of 1500~Hz (0.7~s on, 1.5~s off). \\ \hline
4 & The landline side: if bits 0 to 2 are not set, the answerer listens for the 1500~Hz tone in the first 10~s.  If it finds the tone, \std{ETC} is on for the call.  The transmit level is 6.6~dB lower. \\ \hline
5 & The transmit filter of \cmdl[plusEF]{+EF} is on. \\ \hline
any & If S116 is not 0, \std{V.42} offers frames of 64 octets (N401), and T401 is 3~s plus the time of three frames. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.2\textwidth}|L{0.66\textwidth}|}{ETC Profiles}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
\cmdl[ampF]{\&F5} & The cellular side (\texttt{ETC\_CELL}): S116 = 238 (0xEE), \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=V32B} (\std{V.32bis} and lower), \cmdl[bsN]{\bs N2} (error control is necessary), S10 = 40 (4~s), S7 = 90. \\ \hline
\cmdl[ampF]{\&F6} & The landline side (\texttt{ETC\_LAND}): S116 = 176 (0xB0), \cmdl[bsN]{\bs N3} (error control if possible).  The modulations do not change. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
With \cmdl[ampF]{\&F6}, \std{ETC} is on only for a call from an
\std{ETC} caller.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusEF}{+EF=\p{n}}{ETC Transmit Filter}
The set of coefficients of the 6-tap transmit filter (0 to 10,
\emph{default} 5), as the 3Com firmware has them.  The filter operates when
bit 5 of S116 is set.  Set 0 has a flat response.  Sets 1 to 6 decrease
the high frequencies, to compensate for the pre-emphasis of a cellular
voice channel.  Set 5 changes the gain from +3.5~dB at 300~Hz to
$-$7.6~dB at 3600~Hz.  D-Modem sets the gain of each set to 0~dB at
1800~Hz.  \cmd{+EF?} gives the set, and \cmd{+EF=?} gives \cmd{(0-10)}.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} is 0 to 10.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\subsection{V.80 Synchronous Access}\label{sec:v80}

With \cmdl[plusES]{+ES=6} (calling) or \cmdl[plusES]{+ES=,,8}
(answering), the call has no error correction and no compression.  After
\rc{CONNECT}, the modem moves the octets of the \std{DTE} as a synchronous
bit stream.  The in-band commands of \std{V.80} follow EM (0x19) in the
two directions (Table~9/V.80).

The modem starts in the \emph{transparent} sub-mode.  The octets of the
\std{DTE} go out with no change, least significant bit first.  The bits of
the line come back as octets.  When the \std{DTE} has no data, the modem
sends \p{syn1} and tells the \std{DTE} \cmd{<EM><under>}.  With the
\p{trans\_idle} 1 or 2 of \cmdl[plusESA]{+ESA}, the receiver first
searches for the \std{SYN}.  It gives the \std{DTE} no data before the
\std{SYN}.

\cmd{<EM><flag>} from the \std{DTE} starts the \emph{framed} sub-mode:
\begin{itemize}
\item The data goes in \std{HDLC} frames.  The \cmd{<EM><flag>} of the
  \std{DTE} ends each frame.  The modem adds the zero insertion, the
  flags and the \std{FCS}.
\item The modem gives back the octets of each received frame (without
  the \std{FCS}, which it examined).  It adds \cmd{<EM><flag>} for a good
  frame or \cmd{<EM><err>} for a bad frame.
\item It gives an \cmd{<EM><err>} when its search finds a flag, and
  \cmd{<EM><mark>} for an abort or marks.
\item \cmd{<EM><mark>} from the \std{DTE} goes back to transparent.
\end{itemize}
The modem shields EM and 0x99 in the data in the two directions
(\cmd{<EM><t1>}, \cmd{<EM><t2>}).  It also shields DC1 and DC3 with
DC1/DC3 flow control (\cmdl[plusIFC]{+IFC}).  From the \std{DTE}, the
modem accepts all codes T1 to T20.  The half duplex of \std{V.34} is not
available.

\begin{vtable}{3}{|T{0.28\textwidth}|C{0.1\textwidth}|L{0.45\textwidth}|}{V.80 In-Band Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{From} & \thd{Meaning}}
<EM><ecs> & \std{DTE} & Online command state (in place of \cmdr{+++}, which is
  data).  The modem answers \cmd{<EM><ecs>}, then \rc{OK}.  \cmdl[O]{ATO} goes back. \\ \hline
<EM><eot> & \std{DTE} & Disconnect: \cmd{<EM><eot>}, then \rc{NO CARRIER}. \\ \hline
<EM><rate><tx><rx> & \std{DTE} & The highest rates to send and to receive
  (the \p{max} of \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}), for the next retrain or renegotiation (\cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS}
  sets the two again). \\ \hline
<EM><hunt> & \std{DTE} & Start the search of the receiver again (framed, or transparent
  with \p{trans\_idle} 1 or 2).  The modem discards the data that waits for the \std{DTE}. \\ \hline
<EM><resume> & \std{DTE} & Continue after an underrun (the
  \p{framed\_un\_ov} of \cmdl[plusESA]{+ESA}). \\ \hline
<EM><rtn>, <EM><rrn> & \std{DTE} & Retrain, or renegotiate the rate, as
  \cmdl[O]{ATO1} and \cmdl[O]{ATO3}. \\ \hline
<EM><eot> & Modem & The carrier of the far end stopped (before \rc{NO CARRIER}). \\ \hline
<EM><rtn>, <EM><rrn> & Modem & A retrain or renegotiation that the \std{DTE}
  did not ask for (from the far end, or from this modem after a loss of signal). \\ \hline
<EM><rate><tx><rx> & Modem & The rates, after each of these, or when they
  change during the call. \\ \hline
<EM><rover> & Modem & The modem lost received octets (the \std{DTE} read no data
  for 2048 octets). \\ \hline
<EM><tover> & Modem & The modem lost octets of the \std{DTE} (the buffer was full). \\ \hline
<EM><under> & Modem & The \std{DTE} has no more data. \\ \hline
<EM><bnum> & Modem & The count of the transmit buffer, with \cmdl[plusITF]{+ITF}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}

\begin{command}{plusESA}{+ESA}{Synchronous Access Mode Settings}
These are the eight settings of the Synchronous Access Mode.  If you do
not give a setting, it does not change.
\begin{syntax}
\item +ESA=[\p{trans\_idle}][,\p{framed\_idle}...][,\p{syn2}]
\item +ESA? (gives \rc{+ESA: 0,0,0,0,0,0,255,255})
\item +ESA=? (gives \rc{+ESA: (0-2),(0,1),(0,1),(0),}\allowbreak
  \rc{(0-2),(0),(0-255),(0-255)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{3}{|T{0.2\textwidth}|C{0.07\textwidth}|L{0.56\textwidth}|}{+ESA Parameters}
  {\thd{Parameter} & \thd{Value} & \thd{Meaning}}
trans\_idle & 0 & The modem sends \p{syn1} when the \std{DTE} has no data.
  The receiver gives the octets as they come (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
 & 1 & The same, but the receiver searches for \p{syn1}. \\ \hline
 & 2 & The modem sends \p{syn1} and \p{syn2} in turn.  The receiver
  searches for the two (\p{syn1} first).  Each search gives the \std{DTE}
  the octets from the \std{SYN}, aligned on it. \\ \hline
framed\_idle & 0 & Flags between frames (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
 & 1 & Marks between frames. \\ \hline
framed\_un\_ov & 0 & The \std{DTE} has no more data in a frame: an abort (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
 & 1 & A flag (no \std{FCS}).  For the two values, the modem then
  discards the octets of the \std{DTE} until its \cmd{<EM><resume>}.
  \cmd{<EM><unum>} and their count answer it (for the two:
  \cmd{<EM><under>}). \\ \hline
hd\_auto & 0 & Only 0. \\ \hline
crc\_type & 0 & No \std{FCS} (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
 & 1 & FCS-16. \\ \hline
 & 2 & FCS-32 (\std{V.42}'s). \\ \hline
nrzi\_en & 0 & Only 0. \\ \hline
syn1, syn2 & 0--255 & The \std{SYN} octets (\emph{default} 255). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusITF}{+ITF}{Transmit Buffer Thresholds}
This command controls the transmit buffer of the Synchronous Access Mode.
If the number of octets of the \std{DTE} in the buffer (with the EM
codes) is more than \p{off}, the modem does not accept more octets.  The
modem keeps \p{on}.  If \p{report\_period} is not 0, the modem gives
\cmd{<EM><bnum>} with the number of octets every \p{report\_period} times
10~ms.
\begin{syntax}
\item +ITF=[\p{off}][,[\p{on}][,[\p{report\_period}]]]
\item +ITF? (gives \rc{+ITF: 383,128,0})
\item +ITF=? (gives \rc{+ITF: (1-4095),(0-4094),(0-500)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range, or if \p{off} is not more than \p{on}.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\section{Data Compression}\label{sec:compression}

\begin{command}{ampK}{\&K\p{n}}{Data Compression}
\cmdl[ampK]{\&K0} to \cmdl[ampK]{\&K3} set data compression as on the
USRobotics modems.  They are the same as \cmdl[pctC]{\%C} (\sreg{104}).
The Rockwell and Conexant modems use \cmdl[ampK]{\&K} for flow control.
The modem accepts their \cmdl[ampK]{\&K4} to \cmdl[ampK]{\&K6}, but these
have no effect.  Their \cmdl[ampK]{\&K3} (\std{RTS}/CTS) keeps the
compression of \std{V.42} on.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--6.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\&K\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
\&K0 & No compression (\cmdl[pctC]{\%C0}). \\ \hline
\&K1, \&K2 & \std{V.44}, \std{V.42bis} or \std{MNP~5} (\cmdl[pctC]{\%C3}). \\ \hline
\&K3 & \std{V.44} or \std{V.42bis}, no \std{MNP~5} (\cmdl[pctC]{\%C2}). \\ \hline
\&K4--\&K6 & Accepted (the flow control of other modems). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{pctC}{\%C\p{n}}{Data Compression Control}
This command sets data compression (\sreg{104}).  Data compression must
have error correction (\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N}).  \std{V.42} offers \std{V.44}
and \std{V.42bis}, and the far end selects one.
\cmdl[plusDS]{+DS}, \cmdl[plusDS44]{+DS44} and \cmdl[plusDSZ]{+DSZ} give
the detailed settings.  \cmdl[ampK]{\&K} sets the same item.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--3.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\%C\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
\%C0 & No compression. \\ \hline
\%C1 & \std{MNP~5} (with \std{MNP}). \\ \hline
\%C2 & \std{V.44} or \std{V.42bis} (with the \std{LAPM} of \std{V.42}).  Also \std{V.42bis} with \std{MNP}, if the far end offers it (\sreg{114}). \\ \hline
\%C3 & One of the two, as applicable to the error correction of the call (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusDS}{+DS}{V.42bis Data Compression}
This command controls \std{V.42bis} (\std{V.250} 6.6.1), in the
compression that \cmdl[pctC]{\%C} sets.
\begin{syntax}
\item +DS=[\p{direction}][,[\p{neg}][,[\p{dict}][,[\p{string}]]]]
\item +DS? (gives \rc{+DS: 3,0,2048,34})
\item +DS=? (gives \rc{+DS: (0-3),(0,1),(512-65535),(6-250)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{3}{|T{0.16\textwidth}|C{0.13\textwidth}|L{0.54\textwidth}|}{+DS Parameters}
  {\thd{Parameter} & \thd{Values} & \thd{Meaning}}
direction & 0--3 & The directions of the \std{DTE}: 0 none, 1 the data that
  it sends, 2 the data that it receives, 3 the two (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
neg & 0, 1 & 1: disconnect (\rc{NO CARRIER}) if the link starts without
  compression in those directions.  \emph{Default} 0. \\ \hline
dict & 512--65535 & The largest dictionary that the \std{XID} of \std{V.42}
  offers.  A maximum of 2048, the \emph{default}. \\ \hline
string & 6--250 & The longest string that the modem offers.  A maximum of 34, the \emph{default}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusDS44}{+DS44}{V.44 Data Compression}
This command controls \std{V.44} (\std{V.250} 6.6.2), in the same way.
\cmdl[plusDS]{AT+DS=0} with \cmdl[plusDS44]{AT+DS44=3} offers only
\std{V.44}.  \cmdl[plusDS44]{AT+DS44=0} offers only \std{V.42bis} (and
zstd, if \cmdl[plusDSZ]{+DSZ=0} is not set).  If the two are 0, there is
no compression, as \cmdl[pctC]{AT\%C0}.  zstd uses the directions of the
two.
\begin{syntax}
\item +DS44=[\p{direction}][,[\p{neg}][,[\p{capability}][,...]]]
\item +DS44? (gives \rc{+DS44: 3,0,0,4096,4096,255,255,12288,12288})
\item +DS44=? (gives the ranges below)
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{3}{|T{0.22\textwidth}|C{0.13\textwidth}|L{0.48\textwidth}|}{+DS44 Parameters}
  {\thd{Parameter} & \thd{Values} & \thd{Meaning}}
direction & 0--3 & As for \cmdl[plusDS]{+DS} (\emph{default} 3). \\ \hline
neg & 0, 1 & 1: disconnect if the link starts without \std{V.44}, or zstd
  in its place, in those directions. \\ \hline
capability & 0, 1 & The modem keeps the value, but it always uses the
  stream method. \\ \hline
codewords tx, rx & 256--65535 & The largest codewords, in each direction.
  A maximum of 4096. \\ \hline
strings tx, rx & 32--255 & The longest strings.  A maximum of 255. \\ \hline
history tx, rx & 512--65535 & The history, in each direction.  A maximum of 12288. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
For each pair, the modem uses the smaller value in the two directions.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusDSZ}{+DSZ}{zstd Data Compression}
This command is from D-Modem.  \p{on} 1 offers zstd (Zstandard, Annex F
of \texttt{docs/v\_cvm.pdf}) in the \std{XID} of \std{V.42}, next to
\std{V.42bis} and \std{V.44}, when compression is on (\cmdl[pctC]{\%C}).
\p{dict} 1 offers zstd with the dictionary of \std{V.cvm}.  A call uses
zstd only if the far end also offers it (a different D-Modem).  Then
\rc{CONNECT} ends in \cmd{/ZSTD}, and the compression result is
\cmd{ZSTD} (70 with \cmdl[V]{V0}).  With other modems, the call continues
with \std{V.44} or \std{V.42bis}.  \cmd{0} offers only \std{V.44} and
\std{V.42bis}.
\begin{syntax}
\item +DSZ=[\p{on}][,[\p{dict}]]
\item +DSZ? (gives \rc{+DSZ: 1,1})
\item +DSZ=? (gives \rc{+DSZ: (0,1),(0,1)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusX2UP}{+X2UP}{x2 Upgrade Host}
D-Modem's own: the host side of the USRobotics remote feature upgrade
(\texttt{docs/x2-upgrade.md}).  After \std{LAPM} connects, \p{mode} 1 reads
the serial number and the feature records of a Courier, and \p{mode} 2
also enables the features in \p{enable} and disables the features in
\p{disable}.  These are sums of Terbo (4), V.FC (8) and x2 (16).  The
defaults are 16 and 0, thus \cmd{AT+X2UP=2} enables x2.  The modem then
sends one line to the \std{DTE}:
\rc{+X2UP: }\p{result}\rc{,"}\p{serial}\rc{",}\p{before}\rc{,}\p{after}.
\p{result} is \cmd{ENABLED}, \cmd{DISABLED}, \cmd{CHANGED},
\cmd{ALREADY}, \cmd{REFUSED}, \cmd{QUERIED} or \cmd{NO REPLY};
\p{before} and \p{after} are record 2 in decimal, as the Courier's
\cmd{ATY14} shows it.  \cmd{0} does nothing.
\begin{syntax}
\item +X2UP=[\p{mode}][,\p{enable}][,\p{disable}]
\item +X2UP? (gives \rc{+X2UP: 0,16,0})
\item +X2UP=? (gives \rc{+X2UP: (0-2),(0,4,8,12,16,20,24,28),(0,4,8,12,16,20,24,28)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range, or for a feature in
\p{enable} and in \p{disable}.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusDEBC}{+DEBC}{EBCDIC DTE}
This command is from D-Modem (\texttt{docs/ebcdic.md}).  It sets the
character set of the \std{DTE}.  With \p{mode} 1, the first two
characters of each command line identify the character set:
\cmd{AT} in EBCDIC is 0xC1 0xE3.  Then the modem sends the echo, the
information text and the result codes of that line in the same
character set, and it also sends the unsolicited result codes (for
example \rc{RING} and \rc{CONNECT}) in that character set.  In EBCDIC,
the escape character (S2) is also in EBCDIC: \cmd{+} is 0x4E.
\p{mode} 0 accepts only ASCII.  \p{mode} 2 accepts only EBCDIC.
\p{data} 1 also changes the data of a call: the modem changes the
EBCDIC of the \std{DTE} to ISO~8859-1 for the far end, and the
ISO~8859-1 of the far end to EBCDIC.  With \p{data} 0, the modem does
not change the data.  \p{cp} is the IBM code page.  The default is 37
(USA and Canada).  The result of the command line that sets
\cmd{+DEBC} is in the character set of that command line.
\cmdl[Z]{Z} sets \cmd{+DEBC=1,0,37}.
\begin{syntax}
\item +DEBC=[\p{mode}][,[\p{data}][,\p{cp}]]
\item +DEBC? (gives \rc{+DEBC: 1,0,37})
\item +DEBC=? (gives \rc{+DEBC: (0-2),(0,1),}\allowbreak
\rc{(37,273,275,277,278,280,}\allowbreak
\rc{284,285,297,500,871,1047)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{DTE}}{DTE} Interface and Flow Control}\label{sec:dte}

\begin{command}{plusIFC}{+IFC, +ICF, +IPR}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{DTE}}{DTE} Interface}
\cmdl[plusIFC]{+IFC} sets the flow control in each direction (DC1/DC3 in
the Synchronous Access Mode of \std{V.80},
\hyperref[sec:v80]{Section~\ref*{sec:v80}}).  \cmdl[plusIFC]{+ICF} sets the
character framing of the \std{DTE}.  \cmdl[plusIFC]{+IPR} sets the rate of
the \std{DTE} (0 automatic).
\begin{vtable}{3}{|T{0.1\textwidth}|C{0.13\textwidth}|L{0.6\textwidth}|}{\std{DTE} Interface Parameters}
  {\thd{Name} & \thd{Default} & \thd{Range}}
+IFC & 2,2 & (0-3),(0-2) \\ \hline
+ICF & 3,3 & (1-6),(0-3) \\ \hline
+IPR & 0 & 0, 300, 1200, 2400, 4800, 9600, 19200, 38400, 57600, 115200,
  230400, 460800, 921600 \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampH}{\&H\p{n}}{Flow Control}
The modem keeps the value in \sreg{62}.  The pty controls the flow, thus
the value changes only the report of \cmdl[hashUD]{\#UD}.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--3.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\&H\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function (as in the report)}}
\&H0 & None (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\&H1 & \std{RTS}/CTS. \\ \hline
\&H2 & \std{XON}/XOFF. \\ \hline
\&H3 & Both. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampI}{\&I\p{n}}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{XON}}{XON}/XOFF Flow Control}
This is the software flow control of USRobotics.  The modem accepts this
command, but it changes nothing.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--5.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bsQ}{\bs Q\p{n}}{Flow Control}
The modem accepts this command, but it changes nothing: the pty controls
the flow.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--3.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bsG}{\bs G\p{n}}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{XON}}{XON}/XOFF Pass Through}
The modem accepts this command, but it changes nothing.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampC}{\&C\p{n}}{Data Carrier Detect (\texorpdfstring{\texttt{DCD}}{DCD}) Control}
The modem keeps the value in \sreg{60}.  The value changes nothing: a pty
has no carrier line.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.16\textwidth}|L{0.7\textwidth}|}{\&C\p{n} Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Function}}
\&C0 & \std{DCD} always on (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
\&C1 & \std{DCD} follows the carrier (on other modems). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{ampD}{\&D\p{n}, \&F\p{n}, \&R\p{n}, \&S\p{n}}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{DTR}}{DTR}, Factory Defaults, \texorpdfstring{\texttt{RTS}}{RTS}, \texorpdfstring{\texttt{DSR}}{DSR}}
The modem accepts these commands, with or without a digit, and does
nothing.  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} sets the \emph{defaults}.  When the \std{DTE}
sets \std{DTR} off, \texttt{dmodemd} disconnects the call, whatever
\cmdl[ampD]{\&D} says (\hyperref[sec:online]{Section~\ref*{sec:online}}).
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{F}{F\p{n}}{On-Line Data Character Echo}
The modem accepts \cmdl[F]{F1}, so that software that sends it operates
correctly.  On other modems, \cmdl[F]{F0} echoes the online data.  This
modem never echoes the online data.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise (\cmdl[F]{F} alone is \cmdl[F]{F0}).}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bsJ}{\bs J\p{n}}{Rate Adjust}
On other modems, this command makes the rate of the \std{DTE} follow the
rate of the line.  This modem accepts it, but it changes nothing.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusILRR}{+ILRR, +IDSR, +ILSD}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{DTE}}{DTE} Rate Report, \texorpdfstring{\texttt{DSR}}{DSR}, Long Space}
These commands are the rate report of the \std{DTE}, \std{DSR} and long
space disconnect.  The modem keeps the values.  Each value is 0 or 1,
\emph{default} 0.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bsK}{\bs K\p{n}}{Break Control}
On other modems, this command sets the function of a break.  This modem
accepts it, but it changes nothing.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--5.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bsR}{\bs R\p{n}}{Ring Indicator After Answer}
The modem accepts this command, but it changes nothing.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{bsT}{\bs T\p{n}}{Inactivity Timer}
The modem accepts this command, but it changes nothing.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--255.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{ISDN}}{ISDN} and Cellular}\label{sec:isdn}

\begin{command}{plusB2}{+B2}{B-Channel Protocol}
This command is from D-Modem.  It uses the numbers of \std{CAPI} 2.0 for
layer 2.  It sets the protocol on a clear \std{64} kbit/s channel
(\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=CLEAR}, or a line that tells the modem that its call
is clear).  Other modes do not use it.  \cmdl[ampV]{\&V} lists it.
\begin{syntax}
\item +B2=\p{n}
\item +B2? (gives \rc{+B2: 0})
\item +B2=? (gives \rc{+B2: (0,5,7,9)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0, 5, 7 or 9.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+B2 Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Protocol}}
0 & \std{X.75} (\std{LAPB} in \std{HDLC}, basic mode, a window of 7, the
  calling modem at address B).  This is the protocol of \std{ISDN}
  terminal adapters, BBSes and mailers.  The characters of the \std{DTE}
  go in I frames of up to 256 octets.  \rc{CONNECT 64000}, with
  \cmdl[bsV]{\bs V1} \rc{CONNECT 64000/X.75}.  \cmdl[H]{ATH} sends
  \std{DISC} (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
5 & Synchronous \std{PPP} (\std{RFC} 1618).  The \std{DTE} uses the
  asynchronous framing of \std{PPP} (\std{RFC} 1662, as pppd does on a
  serial port).  Each frame goes to the line in \std{HDLC} without its
  escapes, and comes back with escapes.  \cmd{/PPP} with
  \cmdl[bsV]{\bs V1}. \\ \hline
7 & The link of the modem (\std{V.42}, \std{MNP} or plain characters, as
  \cmdl[bsN]{\bs N} sets), for the clear channel of an earlier D-Modem. \\ \hline
9 & \std{V.120} (the frames of \std{Q.921} for logical link 256 in
  extended mode, the information of each I frame after the header octet
  \cmd{0x83} of \std{V.120}).  This is the protocol of North American
  terminal adapters.  \cmd{/V.120} with \cmdl[bsV]{\bs V1}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusCBST}{+CBST}{Bearer Service Type (V.110)}
This command is the bearer service of 27.007: the rate adaption of
\std{V.110} on a clear channel (\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=CLEAR}).
\begin{itemize}
\item \p{name} \cmd{0} (the \emph{default}) is asynchronous: the
  characters of the \std{DTE} go through the link of the modem
  (\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N}), whatever \cmdl[plusB2]{+B2} says.
\item \p{name} \cmd{1} is synchronous: the protocol of
  \cmdl[plusB2]{+B2} in \std{HDLC} at the user rate.
\item \p{ce} has no function here.
\end{itemize}
\rc{CONNECT} gives the user rate (with \cmdl[bsV]{\bs V1} and no
\std{V.42}, \cmd{/V.110}).  \cmdl[ampV]{\&V} lists it.  Refer to
\cmd{docs/isdn.md}.
\begin{syntax}
\item +CBST=[\p{speed}][,[\p{name}][,[\p{ce}]]]
\item +CBST? (gives \rc{+CBST: 0,0,0})
\item +CBST=?
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{For a \p{speed} below, \p{name} 0 or 1, \p{ce} 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+CBST Speeds}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{User rate}}
0 & None (\emph{default}).  An answering modem uses the rate of the frames of the caller
  (\std{600} to \std{38400}), if they come. \\ \hline
65, 66, 68, 70 & \std{300}, \std{1200}, \std{2400}, \std{4800} bit/s (intermediate rate 8k). \\ \hline
71 & \std{9600} bit/s (16k). \\ \hline
75, 79 & \std{14400}, \std{19200} bit/s (32k). \\ \hline
80, 81 & \std{28800}, \std{38400} bit/s (64k). \\ \hline
83 & \std{56000} bit/s (7 bits in each octet, no frames). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusCRC}{+CRC}{Cellular Result Codes}
This command is from 27.007.  With \cmd{1}, the modem gives a different
result in place of \rc{RING} for each ring of a call:
\begin{itemize}
\item \rc{+CRING: SYNC} for a call on a clear \std{64} kbit/s channel (an
  \std{ISDN} data call: the CLEARMODE of \std{SIP}, the digital bearer of a
  \std{PBX}, the unrestricted digital call of an mISDN card).
\item \rc{+CRING: VOICE} for all other calls.
\end{itemize}
These results are always words, whatever \cmdl[V]{V} says.  A program that
answers automatically (dmodem-capid, \std{CAPI} on a D-Modem) uses them to
identify the two types of call.  \cmdl[ampV]{\&V} lists the setting.
\begin{syntax}
\item +\std{CRC}=\p{n}
\item +\std{CRC}? (gives \rc{+CRC: 0})
\item +\std{CRC}=? (gives \rc{+CRC: (0,1)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 (\rc{RING}, the \emph{default}) or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\section{Result Codes}\label{sec:results}

The modem answers each command line with a result code, as a word
(\cmdl[V]{V1}) or a number (\cmdl[V]{V0}).  Each result code is on a
separate line.  \cmdl[Q]{Q1} sets the result codes off.

The modem uses the line format of ITU-T V.250.  This format does not
change when echo is off (\cmdl[E]{E0}):
\begin{itemize}
\item \cmdl[V]{V1}: a carriage return and a line feed (\sreg{3} and
  \sreg{4}) go before each word result code and after it.  The same
  pair goes before the first line of a response and after each line of
  it.  Thus, \cmdr{ATE0} and then \cmdr{AT} give
  \texttt{<CR><LF>OK<CR><LF>}.
\item \cmdl[V]{V0}: a carriage return (\sreg{3}) goes after each
  number.  Thus, \cmdr{AT} gives \texttt{0<CR>}.  Each line of a
  response ends with a carriage return and a line feed.
\end{itemize}

\begin{vtable}{3}{|L{0.26\textwidth}|C{0.1\textwidth}|L{0.48\textwidth}|}{Result Codes}
  {\thd{Result code} & \thd{Number} & \thd{Description}}
\rc{OK} & 0 & The modem did the command line. \\ \hline
\rc{CONNECT} & 1 & The call is connected (\cmdl[X]{X0}, fax and voice), or the data of a fax
  command starts. \\ \hline
\rc{CONNECT} \p{rate} & Below & A data call is connected at \p{rate}. \\ \hline
\rc{RING} & 2 & A call rings (\texttt{dmodemd}: every 3 s, \hyperref[sec:sip]{Section~\ref*{sec:sip}}). \\ \hline
\rc{NO CARRIER} & 3 & No call connected, or the call ended. \\ \hline
\rc{ERROR} & 4 & A command failed, or a line was too long. \\ \hline
\rc{NO DIALTONE} & 6 & No dial tone (\cmdl[X]{X2}, \cmdl[X]{X4}). \\ \hline
\rc{BUSY} & 7 & A busy tone (\cmdl[X]{X3}, \cmdl[X]{X4}). \\ \hline
\rc{NO ANSWER} & 8 & No answer in \sreg{7} seconds after the modem dialed. \\ \hline
\rc{FAX} & 88 & Adaptive answer (\cmdl[plusFAA]{+FAA=1}): a fax call.
  \std{Class 1} follows. \\ \hline
\rc{DATA} & 89 & Adaptive answer: a data call.  Its \rc{CONNECT} follows. \\ \hline
\rc{VCON} & 90 & Voice mode, set with \cmdl[hashCLS]{\#CLS=8}: the modem has
  the line for voice (\cmdl[A]{A}, the answer of \cmdl[D]{D}, \cmdl[hashVLS]{\#VLS=1}).
  In this condition, \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=8} gives \rc{OK}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}

The \rc{CONNECT} of a data call gives the rate at which the modem
receives, in bit/s (\rc{CONNECT 33600}).  With \cmdl[V]{V0}, it gives
the number of that rate (23).  With \cmdl[X]{X0}, in fax mode and in voice
mode, it is only \rc{CONNECT} (1).  \cmdl[bsV]{\bs V1} adds the error
correction and the compression (\rc{CONNECT 33600/LAPM/V42bis}).
\cmdl[ampA]{\&A} adds lines about the connection before it.  For a
\std{PEP} call, it is \rc{CONNECT FAST}, as on a Telebit.  Its number is
the number of the rate at which the modem receives.  The table that
follows gives the number of each rate.  A rate that is not in the table
is 1.

  {\thd{Rate} & \thd{n} & \thd{Rate} & \thd{n} & \thd{Rate} & \thd{n} & \thd{Rate} & \thd{n}}

1200 & 5 & 26666 & 82 & 40000 & 25 & 54000 & 33 \\ \hline

\section{S-Registers}\label{sec:sregs}

The S-registers contain the settings of the modem, 8 bits each.
\cmdl[S]{ATS} reads and sets them: \cmdl[S]{ATS}\p{n}\cmdr{?} gives the
value of register \p{n}, and \cmdl[S]{ATS}\p{n}\cmdr{=}\p{v} sets it.
Registers 0 to 255 are available.  The registers that this section does
not list are 0 and free.  The modem keeps the values that you write in
them.  In this section, ``kept'' means that you can set and read the
value, but it has no other effect.

\begin{vtable}{5}{|C{0.07\textwidth}|L{0.39\textwidth}|C{0.14\textwidth}|L{0.1\textwidth}|C{0.09\textwidth}|}{S-Register Summary}
  {\thd{Reg.} & \thd{Description} & \thd{Range} & \thd{Unit} & \thd{Default}}
\hyperlink{sreg:0}{S0} & Rings before the modem answers. & 0--255 & rings & 0 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:1}{S1} & Ring counter. & 0--255 & rings & 0 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:2}{S2} & Escape character. & 0--255 & \std{ASCII} & 43 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:3}{S3} & Command line end. & 0--255 & \std{ASCII} & 13 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:4}{S4} & Line feed. & 0--255 & \std{ASCII} & 10 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:5}{S5} & Backspace. & 0--255 & \std{ASCII} & 8 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:6}{S6} & Wait for dial tone. & 0--255 & s & 2 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:7}{S7} & Wait for the call to connect. & 0--255 & s & 60 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:8}{S8} & Pause of the comma. & 0--255 & s & 2 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:9}{S9} & Carrier detect time (kept). & 0--255 & 0.1 s & 6 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:10}{S10} & Carrier loss before disconnection. & 0--255 & 0.1 s & 7 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:11}{S11} & \std{DTMF} tone and gap. & 0--255 & ms & 100 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:12}{S12} & Escape guard time (kept). & 0--255 & 20 ms & 50 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:13}{S13} & Echo (\cmdl[E]{E}). & 0, 1 & -- & 1 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:14}{S14} & Quiet (\cmdl[Q]{Q}). & 0, 1 & -- & 0 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:15}{S15} & Verbose (\cmdl[V]{V}). & 0, 1 & -- & 1 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:16}{S16} & Tone or pulse dialing. & 0, 1 & -- & 1 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:18}{S18} & Loop test time. & 0--255 & s & 0 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:24}{S24} & Hook flash time. & 0--255 & 10 ms & 20 \\ \hline
\hyperlink{sreg:28}{S28} & Pulse ratio (kept). & 0--3 & -- & 0 \\ \hline

% (a new page: the first definition sits on the page's foot by a few
% points, and its measured height then moves it from run to run)
\clearpage
\subsection{S-Register Definitions}

The range is the range of the function of the register.  \cmdl[S]{ATS}
accepts all values from 0 to 255 for all registers, but not for \sreg{44}
and \sreg{45}, which are read only.  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} sets the registers to
their \emph{defaults}, but \sreg{18}, \sreg{28}, \sreg{47}, \sreg{48}
and \sreg{161} keep their values.

\sregdef{0}{Auto-Answer Ring Number}
The number of rings that the modem counts before it answers a call
automatically.  0 disables auto-answer: the modem then answers only with
\cmdl[A]{ATA}.  On a leased line (\cmdl[ampL]{\&L}), a value more than 0
makes the modem answer, with no ring, when it is idle.
\facts{0--255}{0}{rings}

\sregdef{1}{Ring Counter}
The number of rings until now.  The modem compares it with the value of
\sreg{0}.
\facts{0--255}{0}{rings}

\sregdef{2}{Escape Character}
The \std{ASCII} value of the character of the escape sequence, \cmdr{+++}
(\hyperref[sec:online]{Section~\ref*{sec:online}}).
\facts{0--255}{43 (\cmd{+})}{\std{ASCII}}

\sregdef{3}{Command Line Termination Character}
The character that ends a command line and the result codes.
\facts{0--255}{13 (carriage return)}{\std{ASCII}}

\sregdef{4}{Response Formatting Character}
The line feed after the result codes.  A command line also ends at this
character.
\facts{0--255}{10 (line feed)}{\std{ASCII}}

\sregdef{5}{Command Line Editing Character}
The backspace: it erases the last character that you typed.  If echo is
on, the modem sends back the backspace, a space and the backspace again.
\facts{0--255}{8 (backspace)}{\std{ASCII}}

\sregdef{6}{Wait Time for Dial Tone}
With \cmdl[X]{X2} or \cmdl[X]{X4}, the time that the modem waits for a
dial tone before \rc{NO DIALTONE}.  The time is a minimum of 2 s, whatever
the value.  The modem adds 2 s more while it hears a dial tone in a
cadence.
\facts{0--255}{2}{seconds}

\sregdef{7}{Connection Completion Time-Out}
The time that the modem waits for the call to connect after it dials or
answers: the answer and the training.  The \cmdl[D]{W} and \cmdl[D]{@} of
the dial string also wait for this time.  The \cmdl[plusVRN]{+VRN} of
voice mode does not wait longer than this time.  The start of \std{V.cvm}
takes 15 to 21~s, thus make sure that \sreg{7} is sufficiently long.
\facts{0--255}{60}{seconds}

\sregdef{8}{Pause Time for Comma Dial Modifier}
The pause of a \cmd{,} in the dial string.  The country
(\cmdl[plusGCI]{+GCI}) sets it.
\facts{0--255}{2 (the country's)}{seconds}

\sregdef{9}{Carrier Detect Response Time}
Kept: the modulations detect the carrier automatically.
\facts{0--255}{6}{0.1 s}

\sregdef{10}{Automatic Disconnect Delay}
The time that the carrier can be absent before the modem disconnects with
\rc{NO CARRIER}.
\facts{0--255}{7}{0.1 s}

\sregdef{11}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{DTMF}}{DTMF} Dialing Speed}
The length of each \std{DTMF} digit that the modem dials, and of the gap
after it.  If the value is less than 40, the modem uses 95.
\facts{0--255}{100}{milliseconds}

\sregdef{12}{Escape Guard Time}
Kept: the guard time of the escape sequence is always 0.5 s.
\facts{0--255}{50}{0.02 s}

\sregdef{13}{Echo}
The setting of \cmdl[E]{E}.
\facts{0, 1}{1}{none}

\sregdef{14}{Quiet}
The setting of \cmdl[Q]{Q}.
\facts{0, 1}{0}{none}

\sregdef{15}{Verbose}
The setting of \cmdl[V]{V}.
\facts{0, 1}{1}{none}

\sregdef{16}{Tone or Pulse Dialing}
1 tone (\cmdl[T]{T}), 0 pulse (\cmdl[P]{P}).
\facts{0, 1}{1}{none}

\sregdef{18}{Loop Test Timer}
A loop test (\cmdl[ampT]{\&T}) ends after this number of seconds.  If the
value is 0, the test does not end.  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} does not change it.
\facts{0--255}{0}{seconds}

\sregdef{24}{Hook Flash Time}
The length of a hook flash.  The country sets it.
\facts{0--255}{20 (the country's)}{0.01 s}

\sregdef{28}{Pulse Dial Ratio}
The setting of \cmdl[ampP]{\&P}, kept: the table of the country sets the
pulse times.  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} does not change it.
\facts{0--3}{0}{none}

\sregdef{29}{Speaker Control}
The setting of \cmdl[M]{M}.
\facts{0--3}{1}{none}

\sregdef{30}{Speaker Volume}
The setting of \cmdl[L]{L}.
\facts{0--3}{3}{none}

\sregdef{31}{Automode}
The setting of \cmdl[N]{N}, and the second parameter of
\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}.  0: the \std{V.8} CM and JM give only the
modulation of \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}, with no slower modulation.
\facts{0, 1}{1}{none}

\sregdef{32}{Modulation}
The modulation of \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}, by its number
(\hyperref[tab:ms]{Table~\ref*{tab:ms}}).
\facts{the numbers of \hyperref[tab:ms]{Table~\ref*{tab:ms}}}{92 (200 at 48000 Hz or more)}{none}

\sregdef{39}{Line Quality Monitor}
The setting of \cmdl[pctE]{\%E}, kept.
\facts{0--2}{0}{none}

\sregdef{44}{V.cvm Tones Loaded}
The number of tones that the modem loaded in the receive direction of the
last \std{V.cvm} call (\cmdl[plusCVM]{+CVM}).  Read only.
\facts{0--255}{0}{tones}

\sregdef{45}{V.cvm Renegotiations}
The number of renegotiations of the last \std{V.cvm} call.  Read only.
\facts{0--255}{0}{none}

\sregdef{46}{Hayes Protocol Selection}
Not 0: the \std{XID} of a \std{V.42} call has the private sets of the Hayes
V-series modems (\std{AFT} and \std{HMP}).  The value has the coding of the
Hayes S46: 136 \std{LAP-M}, 138 \std{LAP-M} with compression (bit 1).  When
the two modems agree on the compression and not on \std{V.42bis}, the call
uses Hayes \std{ADC} in the two directions, and \rc{CONNECT} shows
\rc{COMPRESSION: ADC} (68).  When the two modems send the sets and one of
them does not agree on the compression, the call has no compression.
0: no Hayes sets.
\facts{0--255}{0}{none}

\sregdef{47}{Leased Line}
The setting of \cmdl[ampL]{\&L}: 0 a dialed line, 1 a 2-wire leased line,
2 a 4-wire leased line.  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} does not change it.
\facts{0--2}{0}{none}

\sregdef{48}{Simulated Carrier Control}
1: the simulated carrier control of \std{V.13} on a leased line, on a call
with no error correction (\cmdl[ampL]{\&L}).  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} does not
change it.
\facts{0, 1}{0}{none}

\sregdef{49}{Point of Sale Fast Connect}
1: the short handshake of \std{V.22}, Bell 212A and \std{V.22bis} that
point of sale terminals and their hosts use.  3: the same, with HDLC flags
in place of the scrambled ones at 1200 bit/s
(\hyperref[sec:pos]{Section~\ref*{sec:pos}}).  0: the handshakes of the
Recommendations.
\facts{0, 1, 3}{0}{none}

\sregdef{50}{Fast Answer Tone}
The length of the answer tone when \sreg{49} is set, and of the answer
tone of \std{V.29} FastPOS.  0 is 400~ms.
\facts{0--255}{0}{10 ms}

\sregdef{56}{Result Code Set}
The setting of \cmdl[X]{X}.
\facts{0--4}{4}{none}

\sregdef{60}{Data Carrier Detect}
The setting of \cmdl[ampC]{\&C}, kept.
\facts{0, 1}{0}{none}

\sregdef{62}{Flow Control}
The setting of \cmdl[ampH]{\&H}, kept.
\facts{0--3}{0}{none}

\sregdef{70}{Connection Lines}
The setting of \cmdl[ampA]{\&A}.
\facts{0--7}{0}{bits}

\sregdef{71}{Rate in CONNECT}
The setting of \cmdl[ampE]{\&E}, kept.
\facts{0, 1}{0}{none}

\sregdef{92}{Answer Delay}
The time that the modem waits after it goes off hook to answer, before
it sends its answer tone.  If the value is 0, the modem waits 10 ms.  On
other modems, \sreg{92} is a fax transmit level: on this modem,
\cmdl[S]{ATS92=10} makes the modem wait 10 s.
\facts{0--255}{2}{seconds}

\sregdef{103}{Error Correction}
The setting of \cmdl[bsN]{\bs N}.  A value in the register sets
\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N}.
\facts{0--5}{3}{none}

\sregdef{104}{Data Compression}
The setting of \cmdl[pctC]{\%C}, and of \cmdl[ampK]{\&K}.
\facts{0--3}{3}{none}

\sregdef{105}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{MNP}}{MNP} Block Size}
The setting of \cmdl[bsA]{\bs A}.
\facts{0--3}{3}{none}

\sregdef{106}{Protocol in CONNECT}
The setting of \cmdl[bsV]{\bs V}.
\facts{0--2}{0}{none}

\sregdef{114}{V.42bis in MNP}
1: when the modem calls with \std{MNP}, its \std{LR} offers \std{V.42bis}
(with \cmdl[pctC]{\%C2} or \cmdl[pctC]{\%C3}) in place of \std{MNP~5}.
0: the \std{LR} offers \std{MNP~5} only.  When the modem answers, it
always accepts \std{V.42bis} from the far end.  Some modems do not
complete a call with this \std{LR}: thus the default is 0.
\facts{0, 1}{0}{none}

\sregdef{102}{ZyXEL Encryption}
The encryption of the Elite 2864I on \std{X.75} and \std{V.120}
(\cmdl[CK]{ATCK}).  Bit 0: the modem offers the encryption.  Bit 1:
triple \std{DES} (the two modems must set it).  Bit 2: no key exchange,
use the \cmdl[CK]{ATCK} key.  Bit 3: if the far end does not agree, the
modem disconnects.  After the link is up, each modem sends an \std{XID}
frame with the ZyXEL parameters.  The data waits until the far end's
parameters come.  If the far end sends no \std{XID} reply, the modem
continues without encryption after 4 tries.  Refer to
\texttt{docs/zyxel-des.md}.
\facts{0--15}{0}{none}

\sregdef{115}{ZyXEL Peer Marker}
1: in a \std{V.32bis} or ZyX call (\cmdl[MS]{+MS}), the modem sends a
marker in the start-up.  If the far end also sends its marker, the
two modems change to ZyX.  If not, the call continues as \std{V.32bis}.
0: the modem sends no marker and does not change to ZyX.  Use the same
value on the two modems.  The answerer marker is not the ZyXEL signal,
thus the marker finds only a D-Modem.
\facts{0, 1}{0}{none}
\sregdef{116}{ETC Flags}
The \std{ETC} flags, with the bits of the 3Com firmware: 238 (0xEE) for
the cellular side, 176 (0xB0) for the landline side, 0 for off.  Refer to
\cmdl[etc]{\&F5, \&F6}.
\facts{0--255}{0}{none}

\sregdef{117}{PCM End on a Digital Line}
The end of \std{V.90} and \std{V.92} that the modem offers in \std{V.8}
on a digital line (refer to
\hyperref[sec:digitalclient]{the client on a digital line}).  0: automatic.
The modem offers the two ends, and \std{V.8} selects one.  When it
answers, it also offers \std{V.91}.  1: the digital modem (the server)
only.  2: the analogue modem (the client) only.  With 1 and 2, the modem
does not offer \std{V.91}.  \cmd{+MS=V91} ignores this register.  On an
analogue line, the modem is always the analogue modem.
\facts{0--2}{0}{none}

\sregdef{161}{Long Space Disconnect}
The setting of \cmdl[Y]{Y}, kept.  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} does not change it.
\facts{0--3}{0}{none}

\section{Fax Commands}\label{sec:fax}

With \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{AT+FCLASS=1}, the modem accepts the \std{Class 1}
commands of \std{T.31}.  The \std{DTE} does the functions of the fax
machine.  (The \std{PBX} modules and libdmodem also have their own
\std{T.30} engine.)  When the modem calls, it sends \std{CNG} after it
dials, until the flags of the answering modem come in its first
\cmdl[plusFTH]{+FRH=3} (\std{T.30} sends it until the \std{DIS}).

\begin{command}{plusFCLASS}{+FCLASS}{Service Class}
This command sets the modem as a data modem, a \std{Class 1}, 1.0,
\std{Class 2} or 2.0 fax modem, a ZyXEL fax modem, or a voice modem.
\cmdl[Z]{ATZ} sets 0.
\begin{syntax}
\item +FCLASS=\p{n}
\item +FCLASS? (gives the class)
\item +FCLASS=? (gives \rc{0,1,1.0,2,2.0,6,8})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0, 1, 1.0, 2, 2.0, 6 or 8.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+FCLASS Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Class}}
0 & Data (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
1 & Fax \std{Class 1} (\std{T.31}). \\ \hline
1.0 & Fax \std{Class 1.0}: \std{Class 1} with \std{V.34} half-duplex fax
  (\std{T.31} Annex B, \cmdl[plusF34]{+F34}). \\ \hline
2 & Fax \std{Class 2} (\hyperref[sec:class2]{Section~\ref*{sec:class2}}). \\ \hline
2.0 & Fax \std{Class 2.0} (\std{T.32}, \hyperref[sec:class2]{Section~\ref*{sec:class2}}). \\ \hline
6 & ZyXEL fax mode (\hyperref[sec:zfax]{Section~\ref*{sec:zfax}}). \\ \hline
8 & Voice (\std{V.253}, \hyperref[sec:voice]{Section~\ref*{sec:voice}}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusFAA}{+FAA}{Adaptive Answer}
With \cmdl[plusFAA]{+FAA=1}, the modem answers a call (\cmdl[A]{ATA},
\sreg{0}) in data mode or fax mode as a data call.  Its answer tone, the
ANSam of \std{V.8}, is also the \std{CED} for a fax machine.  Then the
modem finds if the caller is a fax machine or a data modem:
\begin{itemize}
\item A fax call has one of these signals:
  \begin{itemize}
  \item The \std{CNG} of the caller (1100~Hz, 0.5~s of each 3.5~s), or a
    short burst that stops when the answer tone starts (a fax machine
    that stops its \std{CNG} when it hears the answer tone).
  \item The \std{V.8} \std{CM} of a fax call (a call function of
    facsimile).  \std{V.34} fax machines and some \std{Class 1} modems
    send it on ANSam.
  \item The far end changes to \std{T.38} (the \std{SIP} calls of
    \texttt{dmodemd}).
  \item The caller stays silent through all answer signals of the
    automode sequence (ANSam, then the answer signals of \std{V.32},
    \std{V.22}, \std{V.21}, \std{V.23} and Bell 103, about 20~s, in the
    T1 of 35~s of \std{T.30}).
  \end{itemize}
  The modem then gives \rc{FAX}, \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS} becomes 1,
  and the modem continues as a \std{Class 1} answering modem.  It sends
  the remainder of the 3~s of \std{CED}, then \rc{CONNECT}, as after
  \cmdl[plusFTH]{AT+FTH=3}, for the first frames of the \std{DTE}.
\item A data call has the handshake of its modem.  The modem gives
  \rc{DATA} when the call connects, then its \rc{CONNECT}.
  \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS} becomes 0.
\end{itemize}
If a call ends with neither result, \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS} does not
change.  A \std{V.22}, \std{V.21} or Bell 103 caller stays silent until it
hears its own answer signal.  It answers its step of the sequence:
\rc{DATA}.  For voice mode, refer to
\hyperref[sec:voice]{Section~\ref*{sec:voice}}: a calling tone on the call
changes it to fax or data.  The Rockwell modems also give \rc{FAX} and
\rc{DATA}, as 33 and 35 with \cmdl[V]{V0}.  On this modem, those numbers
are the numbers of the CONNECTs of rates, thus the modem uses 88 and 89.
\begin{syntax}
\item +FAA=\p{n}
\item +FAA? (gives \rc{+FAA: 0})
\item +FAA=? (gives \rc{+FAA: (0,1)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+FAA Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Meaning}}
0 & Answer as \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS} says (\emph{default}.  \cmdl[Z]{Z} sets it). \\ \hline
1 & Adaptive answer: fax or data. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusFMI}{+FMI, +FMM, +FMR}{Fax Identification}
These commands give the manufacturer, the model and the revision, as
\cmdl[plusGMI]{+GMI}, \cmdl[plusGMI]{+GMM} and \cmdl[plusGMI]{+GMR} give
them, in all modes.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusFTS}{+FTS, +FRS}{Transmit Silence, Receive Silence}
\cmdl[plusFTS]{+FTS} sends silence for \p{n} times 10 ms.
\cmdl[plusFTS]{+FRS} waits for silence of \p{n} times 10 ms.
\begin{syntax}
\item +FTS=\p{n}
\item +FRS=\p{n}
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{When the command is complete.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Outside fax mode, or with no \p{n}.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusFTM}{+FTM, +FRM}{Transmit Data, Receive Data}
These commands send or receive data at modulation \p{m}: \rc{CONNECT},
then the data with \cmd{<DLE>} doubled.  \cmd{<DLE><ETX>} ends the data.
\begin{syntax}
\item +FTM=\p{m}, +FRM=\p{m}
\item +FTM=?, +FRM=? (give
  \cmd{24,48,72,73,74,96,\allowbreak 97,98,121,122,145,146})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{CONNECT}}{Then \rc{OK} after the data.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Outside fax mode, or for a modulation that the modem does not have.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.2\textwidth}|L{0.66\textwidth}|}{+FTM and +FRM Modulations}
  {\thd{\p{m}} & \thd{Modulation}}
24, 48 & \std{V.27ter}, \std{2400} and \std{4800} bit/s. \\ \hline
72, 96 & \std{V.29}, \std{7200} and \std{9600} bit/s. \\ \hline
73, 97, 121, 145 & \std{V.17}, \std{7200} to \std{14400} bit/s, long training. \\ \hline
74, 98, 122, 146 & \std{V.17}, \std{7200} to \std{14400} bit/s, short training. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusFTH}{+FTH, +FRH}{Transmit \texorpdfstring{\texttt{HDLC}}{HDLC}, Receive \texorpdfstring{\texttt{HDLC}}{HDLC}}
These commands send or receive \std{HDLC} frames at \std{V.21} channel 2
(\std{300} bit/s).  This is the only modulation for the frames.
\begin{syntax}
\item +FTH=3, +FRH=3
\item +FTH=?, +FRH=? (give \cmd{3})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{CONNECT}}{Then \rc{OK} or \rc{ERROR} after each frame.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Outside fax mode.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusFPR}{+FPR}{Fax \texorpdfstring{\texttt{DTE}}{DTE} Rate}
This is the rate of the fax \std{DTE}.  The modem keeps the value, but
it has no effect.  0 (\emph{default}), 1, 2, 4, 8, 10 or 18.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusF34}{+F34}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{V.34}}{V.34} Fax Rates}
In \std{Class 1.0} (\cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=1.0}), a fax call starts with
\std{V.8} and \std{V.34} half-duplex (\std{T.30} Annex F), not with \std{CNG}
or \std{CED}.  Fax software, for example HylaFAX, expects this with
\cmd{AT+F34}.  If the two ends have it, the modem gives
\rc{+F34:\p{p},\p{c}} (the rate of the primary channel in 2400 bit/s, and
of the control channel: 1 for 1200 bit/s), then \rc{CONNECT}.  After
this, the call is the \std{V.34} data mode of \std{T.31}, with no more
commands:
\begin{itemize}
\item The \std{DTE} sends its frames without their FCS, and
  \cmd{<DLE><ETX>} ends each frame.  The modem sends its frames with
  their FCS, in the same way.
\item \cmd{<DLE><0x6B>} from the \std{DTE} (the sender) asks for the
  primary channel.  At the two ends, the modem gives
  \cmd{<DLE><0x6B><DLE><r>} when the channel is available (\p{r} = 0x6F
  + the rate in 2400 bit/s).  Then the frames of the \std{ECM} block
  follow.
\item \cmd{<DLE><0x6D>} from the \std{DTE} ends the block.  At the two
  ends, the modem gives \cmd{<DLE><0x6D><DLE><0x6E>} when the control
  channel (1200 bit/s) is available again.
\item \cmd{<DLE><EOT>} from the \std{DTE} ends the call (\rc{OK}).  The
  modem gives it when the line stops (\rc{NO CARRIER}).
\end{itemize}
With a fax machine that has no \std{V.8}, the call continues as
\std{Class 1}.  The first \rc{CONNECT} of the calling modem is for the
frames of the answering modem, as after \cmdl[plusFTH]{+FRH=3}.  The first
\rc{CONNECT} of the answering modem is for its own frames, as after
\cmdl[plusFTH]{+FTH=3} (its ANSam was the \std{CED}).  \std{V.34} fax must
have \std{ECM}, and the modem does not poll on it.  It is not available
on \std{T.38}, or after adaptive answer.  \cmdl[Z]{ATZ} sets the
defaults.
\begin{syntax}
\item +F34=[\p{maxp}][,[\p{minp}][,[\p{prefc}][,[\p{maxp2}][,\p{minp2}]]]]
\item +F34? (gives \rc{14,1,0,14,1})
\item +F34=? (gives \rc{(0-14),(0-14),(0-2),(0-14),(0-14)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If each value is in its range.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.12\textwidth}|L{0.74\textwidth}|}{+F34 Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Meaning}}
\p{maxp} & The highest rate of the primary channel, in 2400 bit/s (14:
  33600, \emph{default}).  0: no \std{V.34}, \std{Class 1} as before. \\ \hline
\p{minp} & Its lowest rate (1: 2400, \emph{default}). \\ \hline
\p{prefc} & The preferred rate of the control channel (0 any, \emph{default}.  1
  1200.  2 2400).  The modem keeps the value, but it always uses 1200. \\ \hline
\p{maxp2}, \p{minp2} & For the other direction.  The modem keeps the values. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\subsection{Class 2 and 2.0}\label{sec:class2}

With \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{AT+FCLASS=2.0} (\std{T.32}) or
\cmdl[plusFCLASS]{AT+FCLASS=2} (the older \std{Class 2} that fax programs
know), the modem does \std{T.30} itself.  The \std{DTE} sends and receives
the pages in \std{T.4} MH with \std{EOL}s, with \cmd{<DLE>} doubled.
\cmd{<DLE><ETX>} ends each page.  The \std{DTE} controls the call with the
commands that follow.
\begin{itemize}
\item \std{Class 2} has the older names: \cmdr{+FLID}, \cmdr{+FDCC},
  \cmdr{+FDIS}, \cmdr{+FDCS}, \cmdr{+FBOR}, \cmdr{+FK}, \cmdr{+FPTS} and
  \cmdr{+FHNG}.  The two names operate in the two classes.  Its reports
  are \rc{+FCON}, \rc{+FCSI}, \rc{+FTSI}, \rc{+FDIS}, \rc{+FDCS},
  \rc{+FPTS} and \rc{+FHNG}.
\item The rates are the rates of \std{V.17}, \std{V.29} and
  \std{V.27ter}.  If \cmdl[plusFCC]{+FIS} has error correction mode, the
  modem does the error correction itself (the data of the \std{DTE} does
  not change).
\item The pages are MH, whatever the far end uses (MR or \std{ECM}:
  the modem codes them again), A4 width.
\item A call changes to \std{T.38} as a \std{Class 1} call does (with the
  same commands and results).
\item \cmdl[plusFAA]{+FAA} (adaptive answer) is for \std{Class 1} only.
  The \std{Class 1} commands \cmdl[plusFTH]{+FTH}, \cmdl[plusFTH]{+FRH},
  \cmdl[plusFTM]{+FTM}, \cmdl[plusFTM]{+FRM}, \cmdl[plusFTS]{+FTS} and
  \cmdl[plusFTS]{+FRS} are \rc{ERROR} in \std{Class 2} mode.
\end{itemize}
