\example{Example session: send two pages, then receive them}
\begin{verbatim}
ATD<number>       +FCO           the DIS came
                  +FCI:"<id>"
                  +FIS:1,5,0,2,0,0,0,0
                  OK
AT+FDT            +FCS:1,5,0,2,0,0,0,0
                  CONNECT        DCS, training, CFR
<page><DLE><ETX>  OK
AT+FET=0          +FPS:1,0,0,0,0
                  OK             MPS, MCF
AT+FDT            CONNECT
<page><DLE><ETX>  OK
AT+FET=2          +FPS:1,0,0,0,0
                  +FHS:00
                  OK             EOP, MCF, DCN

ATA               +FCO           the CFR went out
                  +FTI:"<id>"
                  +FCS:1,5,0,2,0,0,0,0
                  OK
AT+FDR            CONNECT
<DC2>             <page><DLE><ETX>
                  +FPS:1,<lines>,0,0,0
                  +FET:0
                  OK
AT+FDR            ... +FET:2, OK
AT+FDR            +FHS:00
                  OK
\end{verbatim}

In \std{Class 2}, an \std{XON} follows the \rc{CONNECT} for a send.  The
page of \cmdl[plusFDT]{+FDR} waits for the \cmd{<DC2>} of the \std{DTE}
(a maximum of one second).  The modem accepts and confirms the pages when
they come, and they wait for \cmdl[plusFDT]{+FDR}.  When the call ends,
the modem gives the hangup status and the result of the command that is
in progress (\rc{NO CARRIER} for \cmdl[D]{ATD} and \cmdl[A]{ATA}).  Then
it goes on hook.

\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+FHS Hangup Status}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Meaning}}
00 & The call is complete. \\ \hline
02 & \cmdl[plusFDT]{+FKS} ended the call. \\ \hline
11 & No fax machine answered. \\ \hline
2x & Send, phase B (the \std{DIS}, the training). \\ \hline
4x, 5x & Send a page, or the exchange after the page. \\ \hline
7x, 9x & Receive. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\std{Class 2.0} gives the status in hexadecimal digits (\std{T.32} Table 23).
\std{Class 2} gives it in decimal.

\begin{command}{plusFLI}{+FLI, +FPI}{Local ID, Polling ID}
The identity of this modem in \std{TSI} and \std{CSI} (\cmdl[plusFLI]{+FLI},
\cmdr{+FLID} in \std{Class 2}), and in \std{CIG} when this modem polls
(\cmdl[plusFLI]{+FPI}, \rc{+FCIG} in \std{Class 2}.  If empty, the modem
uses the identity of \cmdl[plusFLI]{+FLI}).  The identity is a maximum of
20 printable characters.  The modem keeps the spaces in the quotes.
\begin{syntax}
\item +FLI="\p{id}", for example \cmdl[plusFLI]{AT+FLI="Fax ID with Spaces"}
\item +FPI="\p{id}"
\item +FLI?, +FPI? (give the identity in quotes)
\item +FLI=?, +FPI=? (give \cmd{(20)(32-127)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For more than 20 characters.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusFLP}{+FLP, +FSP}{Document to Poll, Request to Poll}
\begin{itemize}
\item \cmdl[plusFLP]{+FLP=1} (\cmdr{+FSPL} in \std{Class 2}): this modem
  has a document that a different modem can poll.  The \std{DIS} tells
  this.  If a caller sends a \std{DTC}, \cmdl[A]{ATA} gives \rc{+FCO},
  \rc{+FPI:"\p{cig}"} and \rc{+FTC:\p{dtc}} (\std{Class 2}: \rc{+FCIG},
  \rc{+FDTC}) and \rc{OK}.  Then \cmdl[plusFDT]{+FDT} sends the document.
\item \cmdl[plusFLP]{+FSP=1}: this modem polls.  The \std{DIS} offers a
  document (\cmdl[D]{ATD} gives \rc{+FPO}, \std{Class 2} \rc{+FPOLL}).
  After this \std{DIS}, \cmdl[plusFDT]{+FDR} sends the \std{CIG} and
  \std{DTC}.  Then it receives as the answering modem does (\rc{+FTI},
  \cmdl[plusFCC]{+FCS}, \rc{CONNECT} and the page).
\end{itemize}
\begin{syntax}
\item +FLP=\p{n}, +FSP=\p{n}
\item +FLP?, +FSP?
\item +FLP=?, +FSP=? (give \cmd{(0,1)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.  The \cmdl[plusFDT]{+FDR} of the caller is \rc{ERROR} without \cmdl[plusFLP]{+FSP=1}.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusFCC}{+FCC, +FIS, +FCS}{Capabilities, Session Parameters}
\cmdl[plusFCC]{+FCC} sets the capabilities (\cmdr{+FDCC} in \std{Class 2}).
It also sets \cmdl[plusFCC]{+FIS}.  \cmdl[plusFCC]{+FIS} sets the
parameters of the session (\cmdr{+FDIS} in \std{Class 2}).  The modem
reads them again at each \cmdl[plusFDT]{+FDT}.  \cmdl[plusFCC]{+FCS?}
gives the parameters of the last session.  At the start, the values are
\cmd{1,5,0,2,0,0,0,0}.
\begin{syntax}
\item +FCC=\p{vr},\p{br},\p{wd},\p{ln},\p{df},\p{ec},\p{bf},\p{st}
\item +FIS=\p{vr},\p{br},\ldots
\item +FCC?, +FIS?, +\std{FCS}?
\item +FCC=?, +FIS=? (give
  \cmd{(0,1),(0-13),(0),(0-2),(0),(0-2),(0),(0-7)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If every value is in its range.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.1\textwidth}|L{0.76\textwidth}|}{+FCC Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Meaning}}
\p{vr} & 0 normal, 1 fine resolution. \\ \hline
\p{br} & 0 to 5: \std{2400}, \std{4800}, \std{7200}, \std{9600}, \std{12000}, \std{14400} bit/s.  6 to 13: also \std{V.34} half-duplex (\std{T.30} Annex F), \std{16800} to \std{33600} bit/s, only with error correction mode.  \std{V.8} offers it at the start of the call.  If the remote modem does not have it, it uses \std{V.17} and the other modulations.  A caller that polls sets \cmdl[plusFLP]{+FSP=1} before it dials, so that \std{V.8} tells this.  \cmdl[plusFCC]{+FCS} then gives the \std{V.34} rate. \\ \hline
\p{ec} & 0 none, 1 or 2 error correction mode. \\ \hline
others & The modem accepts them in their ranges. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusFBO}{+FBO, +FCR, +FPS, +FHS}{Bit Order, Receive, Page Status, Hangup Status}
\cmdl[plusFBO]{+FBO} (\cmdr{+FBOR} in \std{Class 2}): if bit 0 is set, the
first bit of the page data is the top bit of each octet (0, the
\emph{default}: the lowest bit).  The modem keeps \cmdl[plusFBO]{+FCR}
and \cmdl[plusFBO]{+FPS}.  \cmdl[plusFBO]{+FHS?} gives the last hangup
status.  Fax programs send other \cmdr{+F} settings in \std{Class 2}
(\cmdr{+FNR}, \cmdr{+FIE}, \cmdr{+FCT} and other settings).  The modem
keeps them, but they have no effect.
\begin{syntax}
\item +FBO=\p{n}, +FCR=\p{n}, +FPS=\p{n}
\item +FHS?
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusFDT}{+FDT, +FDR, +FET, +FKS}{Send, Receive, End Page, End Call}
\cmdl[plusFDT]{+FDT} sends a page.  \cmdl[plusFDT]{+FDR} receives a page.
\cmdl[plusFDT]{+FET} gives the command after the page that the modem
sent.  \cmdl[plusFDT]{+FKS} (\cmdr{+FK} in \std{Class 2}) ends the call
with a \std{DCN} (\rc{+FHS:02}), as in the example above.
\begin{syntax}
\item +FDT, +FDR, +FKS
\item +FET=\p{n}
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{CONNECT}}{Then the page and \rc{OK}; or the reports, then \rc{OK}.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{With no \std{Class 2} call.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+FET Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Meaning}}
0 & \std{MPS}: another page follows. \\ \hline
1 & \std{EOM}: another document follows. \\ \hline
2 & \std{EOP}: the last page. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\subsection{ZyXEL Fax Mode (Class 6)}\label{sec:zfax}

With \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{AT+FCLASS=6}, the modem operates as the ZyXEL
U-1496 and Elite modems do with their \emph{extended fax AT commands}.
Old ZyXEL fax programs (ZFAX, the \texttt{rcvfax} of a BBS) use this
mode.  The modem does \std{T.30} itself.  There are no \cmdr{+F}
commands to control the call:
\begin{itemize}
\item \cmdl[D]{ATD} makes a fax call.  The modem sends the \std{DCS} for
  \std{V.17} at 14400~bit/s at most, with no error correction mode.
  When the \std{CFR} comes, the modem gives
  \rc{CONNECT FAX/S\p{rate}V\p{n}T0R0L2C7P\p{id}}.  Then the \std{DTE}
  sends the pages.
\item \cmdl[A]{ATA} (or \sreg{0}) answers.  The modem answers as a data
  modem first, as the ZyXEL modems do.  If the caller is a data modem,
  the call is a data call (\rc{CONNECT \p{rate}}).  If the caller is a
  fax machine (\cmdl[plusFAA]{+FAA} tells how the modem finds it), the
  modem gives \rc{CONNECT FAX/\ldots} when the \std{CFR} goes out.
  Then the modem sends the pages to the \std{DTE}.
\item At the end of the fax call, the modem gives
  \rc{DISCONNECT0P\p{id}} if the fax machine at the far end confirmed the last
  page.  Otherwise it gives \rc{DISCONNECT1P\p{id}}.  The modem is then
  in command mode.  If the call ends before \rc{CONNECT FAX}, the modem
  gives \rc{NO CARRIER}.
\item \cmdl[V]{ATV0} and \cmdl[Q]{ATQ1} have no effect on \rc{CONNECT
  FAX} and \rc{DISCONNECT}.
\end{itemize}
The fields of \rc{CONNECT FAX}:
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.12\textwidth}|L{0.74\textwidth}|}{CONNECT FAX Fields}
  {\thd{Field} & \thd{Meaning}}
S\p{rate} & The first four digits of the rate: 1440, 1200, 9600, 7200,
  4800 or 2400. \\ \hline
V\p{n} & 0 normal resolution, 1 fine resolution. \\ \hline
T0 & The data is one-dimensional (MH). \\ \hline
R0 & 1728 pels for each row. \\ \hline
L2 & Any page length. \\ \hline
C7 & No minimum row time: the data has no fill. \\ \hline
P\p{id} & The identity of the fax machine at the far end (its \std{CSI} or
  \std{TSI}), 20 characters, with spaces at the end. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
The page data:
\begin{itemize}
\item The data is \std{T.4} MH with \std{EOL}s.  The first bit of each
  octet is the lowest bit.  There are no \cmd{<DLE>} codes.
\item An \std{RTC} (six \std{EOL}s) ends each page.  When the modem
  sends, it finds the \std{RTC} in the data of the \std{DTE}.  If more
  data follows, the modem sends \std{MPS} and the next page.
\item The ZyXEL modems end the document when the \std{DTE} sets RTS
  off.  D-Modem has no RTS signal.  Thus the document ends when the
  \std{DTE} sends no data for 2~s.  If the \std{DTE} sends no data for
  30~s after \rc{CONNECT FAX}, the modem stops the call.
\item When the modem receives, it sends each page to the \std{DTE} when
  the page is complete.
\end{itemize}

\example{Example session: send two pages}
\begin{verbatim}
AT+FCLASS=6#P5551111     OK
ATD<number>              CONNECT FAX/S1440V1T0R0L2C7P5552222
<page><RTC><page><RTC>   (then no data for 2 s)
                         DISCONNECT0P5552222
\end{verbatim}

\begin{command}{hashF}{\#F, \#B\p{n}, \#V\p{n}, \#T\p{n}, \#R\p{n}, \#L\p{n}, \#C\p{n}, \#P\p{id}, \&N32}{ZyXEL Fax Settings}
These are the ZyXEL fax settings.
\begin{itemize}
\item \cmdl[hashF]{\#F} and \cmdl[hashF]{\&N32} set a \std{V.17} fax
  call on the ZyXEL modems.  In \std{Class 6} all calls are fax calls,
  thus the modem accepts them with no effect.
\item \cmdl[hashF]{\#B0} (\emph{default}): the modem sends the pages to
  the \std{DTE} after \rc{CONNECT FAX/\ldots}.
\item \cmdl[hashF]{\#B1} is for a BBS.  The modem gives \rc{CONNECT FAX}
  and \rc{ZyXEL} on two lines.  Then it waits for a \cmd{<DC2>} (hex 12)
  from the \std{DTE}.  After the \cmd{<DC2>}, it sends
  \cmd{<CR><LF>/S\p{rate}V\p{n}T0R0L2C7P\p{id}<CR><LF>} (with 18
  characters of the identity, as the ZyXEL modems do) and the first
  page.  Each subsequent page needs one more \cmd{<DC2>}.
\item \cmdl[hashF]{\#B2} sets polling for the next call.  After
  \cmdl[D]{D}, the modem sends its identity (\std{CIG}) and a \std{DTC}
  in place of the \std{DCS}.  Then it receives the document of the
  remote, as with \cmdl[hashF]{\#B0} or \cmdl[hashF]{\#B1}.  If the
  remote has no document to be polled, the modem gives
  \rc{NO CARRIER}.  The setting is for one call only.
\item \cmdl[hashF]{\#B3} measures the ring cadence.  The modem must
  be idle.  It gives \rc{Start to measure ring cadence...} and
  \rc{Hit any key to stop!}.  Then, at each start and end of a ring
  burst, it gives one line: \rc{OFF} when a burst starts, \rc{ON} when
  a burst ends, and the time that ended, in steps of 10~ms (for example
  \rc{ON~~~~~~~1980~ms}).  The modem gives no \rc{RING} during the
  measurement.  A character from the \std{DTE} stops it, and the modem
  gives \rc{OK}.  Only a line with its own ring signal (a sound card or
  a USB modem) has ring bursts; a \std{SIP} call does not.
\item \cmdl[hashF]{\#V0} sets normal resolution, \cmdl[hashF]{\#V1}
  (\emph{default}) fine resolution, if the fax machine at the far end has it.
\item The modem keeps \cmdl[hashF]{\#T}, \cmdl[hashF]{\#R},
  \cmdl[hashF]{\#L} and \cmdl[hashF]{\#C}, but they have no effect: the
  data is always MH, 1728 pels, any length, with no fill.
\item \cmdl[hashF]{\#P\p{id}} sets the identity of the modem (its
  \std{CSI} and \std{TSI}).  It is the remainder of the command line,
  or a string in quotes.  The \std{CSI} and the \std{TSI} have the first
  20 characters.
\end{itemize}
\begin{syntax}
\item \#F, \&N32
\item \#B\p{n}, \#V\p{n}, \#T\p{n}, \#R\p{n}, \#L\p{n}, \#C\p{n}
\item \#P\p{id}
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} is in its range (\#B 0--3, \#V and \#T 0--1, \#R
  and \#L 0--2, \#C 0--7) and \p{id} has 25 characters or fewer.  For
  \#B3, after a character from the \std{DTE}.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise, or \#B3 when the modem is not idle.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\section{Text Telephone Commands}\label{sec:text}

The modem is a text telephone in the modes of \std{V.18}
(\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} or \cmdl[plusMV18S]{+MV18S} selects one mode).  These
are the modes:
\begin{itemize}
\item The 5-bit (Baudot) mode of the \std{TTYs}, at \std{45.45}~bit/s on
  1400 and 1800~Hz (TIA-825-A).  With \cmdl[plusMBAUDOT]{+MBAUDOT}, also
  the telex and \std{RTTY} variants (\std{50}~bit/s, the figures of ITA2,
  other tones).
\item \std{DTMF} (each character is one to four \std{DTMF} digits,
  Annex~B).
\item \std{EDT} (channel~1 of \std{V.21} at \std{110}~bit/s, 7 bits, even
  parity, 2 stop bits).
\item Bell 103 (\std{300}~bit/s), the Minitel and Prestel terminals of
  \std{V.23} (\std{1200}/\std{75}~bit/s) and the \std{V.21} text
  telephones of the Nordic countries and the UK (\std{300}~bit/s).  These
  three modes use 7 bits, even parity and a stop bit.
\item The mode of \std{V.18} between two \std{V.18} modems (\std{V.21}, 8
  bits and no parity, the \std{T.140} of the \std{DTE}).
\end{itemize}
The 5-bit mode, \std{DTMF} and \std{EDT} are half duplex.  The modem
sends 150~ms of mark, the characters, and 300~ms of mark after the last
character (\std{DTMF}: 75~ms for each digit, 55~ms between digits).
During this time, the modem does not hear the line.  It does not start
characters while the carrier of the far end is on.

The other modes are duplex on a continuous carrier.  When the modem
calls, it sends in the calling channel of \std{V.21} and Bell 103, and in
the 75~bit/s channel of \std{V.23}, as a Minitel does.  When it answers,
it sends in the other channels.  The \rc{CONNECT} gives the received rate
(\rc{CONNECT 10} for \std{DTMF}, about its characters each second).  No
mode disconnects because the carrier is absent.

The \std{DTE} sends and receives \std{ASCII}:
\begin{itemize}
\item In the 5-bit mode, lower case letters go as capitals.  The modem
  sends the shift codes.  It discards the characters that the code does
  not have.
\item \std{DTMF} has no CR.  The modem discards the CR of the \std{DTE}.
  It gives CR LF to the \std{DTE} for each new line.
\item The modes with parity send even parity and ignore the parity that
  they receive.
\end{itemize}
A text telephone call has no answer tone.  When the modem dials, it goes
into its mode at the end of the dialing.  When it answers, it goes into
its mode immediately.

\cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS=V18} (or \cmdl[plusMV18S]{AT+MV18S=1}) finds the mode
of the far end.
\begin{itemize}
\item When the modem calls, it sends the calling signals of \std{V.18}:
  1~s of silence, then three times the CI of \std{V.8} coded for a
  textphone (400~ms) and 2~s of silence, then the XCI of \std{V.23} (3~s).
  Then it starts again.
\item When the modem answers, it listens for 3~s.
\item When it calls and when it answers, the modem listens for a 5-bit
  character, \std{DTMF}, an \std{EDT} character, \std{V.21} characters, a
  \std{V.21}, Bell 103 or \std{V.23} carrier, and the \std{V.18} signals
  of the far end.
\item If an answering \std{V.18} modem hears CI or XCI, it sends the
  answer tone (2100~Hz) for a maximum of 3~s.  The calling modem sends
  TXP 0.5~s after the tone starts, while the tone continues.  When the
  answering modem hears TXP, it sends three TXP.  Then the two modems are
  in the mode of \std{V.18}.
\item If a calling modem hears the answer tone of a data modem, it
  continues with the carrier of that modem (\std{V.21}, \std{V.23} or
  Bell 103).
\end{itemize}
If the answering modem hears nothing, it probes the line in this
sequence:
\begin{enumerate}
\item The answer message (\cmdl[plusMV18AM]{+MV18AM}) and a new line in
  the 5-bit mode, then in \std{EDT}, then in \std{DTMF}.  The modem
  listens for 2~s after each one.
\item 3~s each of its own \std{V.21}, Bell 103 and \std{V.23} mark.  The
  modem listens for the answer to each.
\item The modem starts the sequence again until the time of \sreg{7}
  ends.
\end{enumerate}
When the modem finds the mode of the far end when it has no carrier, it
answers in that mode.  It waits until the far end is silent, so
that a different automode modem (if this was its probe) also finds the
mode.  The modem sends the characters that it received during the search
to the \std{DTE} after the \rc{CONNECT}.  The CM of \std{V.8} and
\std{V.8 bis} (clause~6/\std{V.18}) are not available.  The answering
modem sends the answer tone of \std{V.25}, not ANSam, so that a
\std{V.18} calling modem continues with TXP.

\begin{command}{plusMV18S}{+MV18S}{V.18 Selection}
This command selects the text telephone mode (as \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}
does.  0 sets \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=92} if \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} had a text
mode).  It also sets if the modem probes a silent far end, and if it
sends the answer message when it answers.  \cmdl[Z]{Z} sets
\cmd{0,0,0,0,1}.
\begin{syntax}
\item +MV18S=[\p{mode}][,[\p{dflt}][,[\p{fbk}][,[\p{msg}][,[\p{probe}]]]]]
\item +MV18S? (gives \rc{+MV18S: 0,0,0,0,1})
\item +MV18S=? (gives \rc{+MV18S: (0-7),(0-7),(0,1),(0,1),(0-2)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+MV18S Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Meaning}}
\p{mode} & 0 off.  1 automode (\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=18}).  2 5-bit (45).  3 \std{DTMF}
  (118).  4 \std{EDT} (110).  5 \std{V.21} (121).  6 \std{V.23} (123).  7 Bell 103 (203):
  their text telephones. \\ \hline
\p{dflt} & The \emph{default} answer mode.  The modem keeps the value. \\ \hline
\p{fbk} & The modem keeps the value. \\ \hline
\p{msg} & 1: the modem sends the answer message when it answers in one of
  the modes 2 to 7. \\ \hline
\p{probe} & 0: automode only listens.  1 or 2: automode probes. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusMV18R}{+MV18R}{V.18 Report}
With \cmdl[plusMV18R]{+MV18R=1}, automode gives the mode that it found
before the \rc{CONNECT}, as \rc{+MV18R: \p{mode}} with the numbers of
\cmdl[plusMV18S]{+MV18S} (\rc{+MV18R: 2} for a \std{TTY}.  \rc{+MV18R: 1}
for the mode of \std{V.18}, with a different \std{V.18} modem).
\begin{syntax}
\item +MV18R=\p{n}
\item +MV18R? (gives \rc{+MV18R: 0})
\item +MV18R=? (gives \rc{+MV18R: (0,1)})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0 or 1.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusMV18AM}{+MV18AM, +MV18P}{Answer Message, Probe Order}
\cmdl[plusMV18AM]{+MV18AM} sets the answer message, a maximum of 63
printable characters.  The modem keeps the spaces in the quotes
(\cmdl[plusMV18AM]{AT+MV18AM="GA"}).  The modem sends the message in the
probes, and when it answers with the \p{msg} of \cmdl[plusMV18S]{+MV18S}.
The modem keeps the value of \cmdl[plusMV18AM]{+MV18P} (2 to 7), but the
probes always go in the sequence above.
\begin{syntax}
\item +MV18AM="\p{text}", +MV18AM?, +MV18AM=?\\
  =? gives \rc{+MV18AM: (63)(32-126)}
\item +MV18P=\p{n}, +MV18P? (gives \rc{+MV18P: 2}), +MV18P=?
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a message that is too long or a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusMBAUDOT}{+MBAUDOT}{5-bit Mode}
This command is from D-Modem.  It sets the rate, the tones and the
figures of the 5-bit mode, for telex and \std{RTTY}:
\cmdl[plusMBAUDOT]{AT+MBAUDOT=50,2125,2295,1} is \std{50} baud ITA2 on
the 170~Hz shift of \std{RTTY}.  The two ends must have the same
settings.  Automode listens on these tones.  \cmdl[Z]{Z} sets the values
of the US \std{TTY}: \cmd{45,1400,1800,0}.
\begin{syntax}
\item +MBAUDOT=[\p{rate}][,[\p{mark}][,[\p{space}][,[\p{code}]]]]
\item +MBAUDOT? (gives \rc{+MBAUDOT: 45,1400,1800,0})
\item +MBAUDOT=?\\
  gives \rc{+MBAUDOT: (45,50,75,100),(300-3400),(300-3400),(0,1)}
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a value outside its range, or if the mark and the space are the same.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+MBAUDOT Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Meaning}}
\p{rate} & \std{45} (\std{45.45}), \std{50}, \std{75} or \std{100} bit/s. \\ \hline
\p{mark} & The mark (1) tone, Hz. \\ \hline
\p{space} & The space (0) tone, Hz. \\ \hline
\p{code} & The figures: 0 the figures of the US \std{TTY} (\cmd{\$} on D, \cmd{"} on Z,
  \cmd{;} on V, unshift on space), 1 the figures of ITA2 (telex). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\section{Alarm Reports: Contact ID}\label{sec:contactid}

With \cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS=CONTACTID}, the modem uses Contact ID (\std{SIA}
\std{DC-05}).  Alarm panels use this \std{DTMF} format to send events to
the receiver of a central station.  This mode has no commands of its own.
\cmd{ATA} (or \cmd{S0}) makes the modem the receiver.  \cmd{ATD} makes
the modem the panel.  The call is in data mode from the handshake, with
\rc{CONNECT 40} on the two sides.  Neither side disconnects because the
carrier is absent.  \cmd{ATH} or the far end ends the call.

A message has 16 digits:
\begin{itemize}
\item The account (4).
\item The message type (18, or 98).
\item The qualifier (1 a new event or an opening, 3 a restore or a
  closing, 6 a previous event that is still there).
\item The event code (3).
\item The partition (2).
\item The zone or user (3).
\item The checksum.  It makes the sum of the values of the digits a
  multiple of 15 (0 counts as 10.  B to F are 11 to 15, sent as
  \cmd{*}, \cmd{\#}, \cmd{A}, \cmd{B} and \cmd{C}).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{The Receiver.}  When the modem answers, it sends the handshake
(1400~Hz, then 2300~Hz, 100~ms each).  It sends the handshake again after
3~s with no message (four times in total).  If a message has a good
checksum, the modem sends it to the \std{DTE} as a line of its 16 digits
(\rc{1234181131010158}).  Then it sends the kissoff (1400~Hz for
900~ms).  If a message has a bad checksum, the modem sends nothing, and
the panel sends the message again.  The modem discards the characters
from the \std{DTE}.

\paragraph{The Panel.}  When the modem dials, it waits for the handshake.
Then it sends each line from the \std{DTE} (\cmd{1234 18 1131 01 015}).
A line has 16 digits, or 15 digits and the modem adds the checksum.  The
modem ignores spaces and dashes.  A message starts 250~ms after the
handshake or the last kissoff, with 55~ms for each digit and 55~ms
between digits.  If no kissoff comes in 1.25~s, the modem sends the
message again (four times in total).  The modem gives one of these
results for each line: \rc{ACK} (kissoff received), \rc{NAK} (four times
without a kissoff), or \rc{ERROR} (no message).

\section{Text Messages: Fixed-Line SMS}\label{sec:sms}

With \cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS=SMS}, the modem uses fixed-line \std{SMS}
(\std{ETSI} \std{ES} \std{201} \std{912}, protocol~1).  Some home
telephones use this procedure to send and receive text messages through
the message centre of their telephone company.  This mode has no
commands of its own.  \cmd{ATA} (or \cmd{S0}) makes the modem the
receiving side.  \cmd{ATD} makes the modem the sending side.  The call is
in data mode from the start, with \rc{CONNECT 1200} on the two sides.
The line uses the 1200~bit/s channel of \std{V.23} (1300 and 2100~Hz) in
the two directions, one side at a time.

\paragraph{The Receiving Side.}  When the modem answers, it asks the caller for
its messages.  It receives each message and gives it to the \std{DTE}:
\begin{itemize}
\item A delivery (from a message centre to a telephone) as \rc{+CMT:
  "\p{from}",,"\p{time}"}.
\item A submission (from a telephone to its message centre) as
  \rc{+SUBMIT: "\p{to}"}.
\end{itemize}
A line of the text (\std{UTF-8}.  8-bit data in hex) follows each one.
All other messages come as \rc{+PDU: \p{hex}}.  The call ends when the
caller tells that it has no more messages.  The modem discards the
characters from the \std{DTE}.

\begin{verbatim}
+CMT: "+441234567890",,"26/10/31,09:05:07+00"
Your code is 1234
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{The Sending Side.}  When the modem dials, it waits for the request for
messages.  Then it sends each line from the \std{DTE}:

\begin{vtable}{2}{|T{0.22\textwidth}|L{0.64\textwidth}|}{Lines a Sending Modem Takes}
  {\thd{Line} & \thd{Sends}}
\p{number},\p{text} & A submission of \p{text} to \p{number} (a
  message from a telephone). \\ \hline
\textless\p{number},\p{text} & A delivery of \p{text} from \p{number} (a
  message from a message centre), with the time now (\std{UTC}).  If
  \p{number} has letters, it is a name. \\ \hline
:\p{hex} & The message (the \std{TPDU} of \std{3GPP} \std{TS} \std{23.040}) with no
  change. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}

A \p{number} is digits (and \cmd{*}, \cmd{\#}), with \cmd{+} first for an
international number.  The text goes in the 7-bit alphabet of \std{GSM},
up to 160 characters.  If the text has a character that is not in that
alphabet, it goes in \std{UCS2}, up to 70 characters.  The modem gives
one of these results for each line: \rc{OK} (accepted), \rc{+CMS ERROR:
\p{cause}} (refused, with the cause from the far end), or \rc{ERROR} (not
a message, or no answer after three attempts).  If no line comes for
3~s, the modem tells the far end that it has no more messages and
disconnects.

\section{Voice Commands}\label{sec:voice}

With \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{AT+FCLASS=8}, the modem accepts the voice commands
of \std{V.253}.  In the other modes, they are \rc{ERROR}, but
\cmdl[hashCID]{+VCID} is not.  Each \cmdr{+V} parameter accepts \cmdr{?}
(its values) and \cmdr{=?} (their ranges).

\begin{command}{vcmds}{+VIP}{Initialize Voice Parameters}
This command sets the voice settings to their values at the start.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVNH}{+VNH}{Automatic Hangup Control}
0 or 1.  The modem accepts this command, but it has no effect: if the
handshake of a held call fails, the modem disconnects the call.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVGT}{+VGT, +VGR}{Transmit and Receive Gain}
The gain when the modem sends (\cmdl[plusVGT]{+VGT}) and receives
(\cmdl[plusVGT]{+VGR}), 0 to 128.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{If \p{n} = 0--128.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{Otherwise.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVIT}{+VIT}{Inactivity Timer}
The inactivity timer, 0 to 255.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVSD}{+VSD}{Silence Detection}
The sensitivity and the period of silence detection, each 0 to 255.
\begin{syntax}
\item +VSD=\p{s},\p{t}
\end{syntax}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVSM}{+VSM}{Voice Compression Method}
This command sets the format of the samples that \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VTX},
\cmdl[plusVTX]{+VRX} and \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VTR} carry: format \p{m} at \p{r}
samples/s (if there is no \p{r}: 8000, or the rate of the format).  The
modem accepts \p{s} and \p{e} for silence compression (0 to 255) and
keeps them, but the modem does not compress silence.
\begin{syntax}
\item +VSM=\p{m}[,\p{r}[,\p{s}[,\p{e}]]]
\item +VSM? (gives \cmdl[plusVSM]{+VSM=1,8000,0,0} at the start)
\item +VSM=? (lists the formats, one on each line)
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a different format, or a rate that the format does not use.  The setting does not change.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.12\textwidth}|L{0.74\textwidth}|}{+VSM Formats}
  {\thd{\p{m}} & \thd{Format}}
0, 1 & 8-bit linear, signed and unsigned (offset by 128), at 7200, 8000
  or 11025 (\std{V.253}'s numbers). \\ \hline
4, 5 & \std{G.711} u-law and A-law, at 8000. \\ \hline
128 & 8-bit linear unsigned, as 1 (from this point, the numbers are from Conexant). \\ \hline
129 & 16-bit linear signed, the low octet first, at 7200, 8000 or 11025. \\ \hline
130, 131 & A-law and u-law, as 5 and 4. \\ \hline
132 & IMA \std{ADPCM}, 4 bits for each sample, two in each octet with the
  first in the low nibble (as in a \std{WAV} file), at 7200, 8000 or
  11025.  The coder starts again at each \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VTX},
  \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VRX}, \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VTR}. \\ \hline
133 & \std{G.726} \std{ADPCM} at 32~kbit/s (\std{G.721}), 4 bits for each
  sample, two in each octet with the first in the low nibble, at 8000:
  the \std{ADPCM} of US Robotics (\cmdl[hashVBS]{\#VSM=129}).  The coder
  starts again as for 132.  The number is from D-Modem. \\ \hline
134, 135, 136 & The \std{ADPCM} of the ZyXEL U-1496 and Elite 2864, with 2, 3 or 4 bits for
  each sample, at 9600 (the rate of ZyXEL), 8000 or 7200.  The first sample
  is in the most significant bits of an octet.  A sample can go across two
  octets.  These are the file formats of the ZyXEL \cmd{vcnvt} program and of the
  \cmd{zyxel} formats of \cmd{vgetty} (the ZyXEL \cmdl[plusVSM]{+VSM=2}, 3 or 30, and 4).  The coder
  starts again as for 132.  The numbers are from D-Modem. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVLS}{+VLS}{Analog Source/Destination Selection}
This command connects the voice engine to the line or releases the line.
It can also connect the voice engine to a handset.  When you change
between 1, 14 and 15, the voice engine continues to operate, and the
line stays connected or released.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{Always.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a device that the modem does not have.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.08\textwidth}|L{0.78\textwidth}|}{+VLS Values}
  {\thd{Value} & \thd{Device}}
0 & Release the line, on hook (\emph{default}). \\ \hline
1 & Connect to the line for voice (on \std{SIP}: answer the call that rings). \\ \hline
14 & Only a handset of the modem, with the line on hook.
  \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VRX} records its microphone and \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VTX}
  plays to its earpiece.  The modem gives the \std{DTMF} from the
  handset. \\ \hline
15 & The line with the handset on the call.  The \std{DTE} hears the line
  and the handset.  The line gets the samples of the \std{DTE} and the
  handset. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\cmdl[plusVLS]{AT+VLS=?} gives the list of the devices of \std{V.253}.
For each device, it gives the events that the device reports in the
transmit, receive and command states, as hex bit fields (bit \p{n} for
the event \p{n} of \std{V.253}, event 0 the leftmost of 32):
\example{Example output: AT+VLS=?}
\begin{verbatim}
0,"",00000000,00000000,90000000
1,"T",0B043060,0B043060,0B043060
\end{verbatim}
On hook (0), the events are caller ID (with \cmdl[hashCID]{+VCID}) and
\rc{RING}.  On the line (1), the events are \std{DTMF}, the two calling
tones, the loop current interruption, a ringing tone, a busy tone and the
two answer tones, in all states.  Some lines can detect the phone on the
jack of the modem (a SmartUSB56 with \texttt{dmodemd --usb-handset}).
These lines add event 8 to the two devices, in all states
(\texttt{00800000}, \texttt{0B843060}, \ldots).  A modem with its own
handset (a SmartUSB56 with \texttt{dmodemd --usb-voice-codec}) lists two
more devices:
\example{Example output: AT+VLS=?, the handset's lines}
\begin{verbatim}
14,"H",08000000,08000000,08000000
15,"HT",0B043060,0B043060,0B043060
\end{verbatim}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVEM}{+VEM}{Event Reporting and Masking}
0 or 1: the event reports of \std{V.253} in the speakerphone state.  The
modem does not have this state.  The modem keeps the value.  It is 0 at
the start and with \cmdl[vcmds]{+VIP}.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{vD}{D}{Voice Dial}
In voice mode, \cmdl[D]{ATD}\p{number} dials.  It gives \rc{OK} when the
far end answers, \rc{BUSY}, \mbox{NO ANSWER} after \sreg{7}, or
\rc{ERROR} when the call fails for a different cause.  On all lines, an
answer tone is the answer and a busy tone is \rc{BUSY} (on \std{SIP},
also in its early media).  On \std{SIP}, the answer is also the
200~\rc{OK}, on a line that does not tell the end of the ringing tone
(\cmdl[plusVRA]{+VRA}), or \cmdl[plusVRN]{+VRN} without a ringing tone.
On an analogue line, the modem first waits 2 s for the dial tone.  When
the modem has the line, it dials the digits as \std{DTMF}, \sreg{11}~ms
each.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{vH}{H}{Voice Hang Up}
In voice mode, \cmdl[H]{ATH} releases the line and goes back to data
mode, \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=0}.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVRA}{+VRA}{Ringback Goes Away Timer}
The far end has answered when the ringing tone is absent for this time
after its last ring.  The unit is 100~ms, 0 to 255 (0: when the first
ring ends).  The value is 50 at the start.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVRN}{+VRN}{Ringback Never Appeared Timer}
The far end has answered when no ring comes for this time after the
modem dials.  The unit is seconds, 0 to 255 (not more than \sreg{7}).
The value is 10 at the start.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVTX}{+VTX, +VRX, +VTR}{Voice Transmit, Receive, Duplex}
These commands send, receive, or send and receive at the same time:
\rc{CONNECT}, then samples in the format and rate of \cmdl[plusVSM]{+VSM},
with \cmd{<DLE>} doubled.  If there is a gap in the samples from the
\std{DTE}, the modem plays silence.
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{CONNECT}}{Then \rc{OK} when it ends.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{If the modem does not have the line or a handset (\cmdl[plusVLS]{+VLS}).}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|C{0.12\textwidth}|L{0.74\textwidth}|}{Voice Data Commands}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Ends at}}
+VTX & \cmd{<DLE><ETX>}, \cmd{<DLE>!} or \cmd{<DLE><CAN>} from the \std{DTE},
  then \rc{OK}. \\ \hline
+VRX & A character from the \std{DTE}.  The modem ends its samples with
  \cmd{<DLE><ETX>}, then \rc{OK}. \\ \hline
+VTR & Only \cmd{<DLE>\textasciicircum} from the \std{DTE}, then
  \cmd{<DLE><ETX>} and \rc{OK}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVTD}{+VTD}{Beep Tone Duration}
The duration of a \cmdl[plusVTS]{+VTS} element that has no duration.  The
unit is 10~ms, 0 to 6000 (0: 1~s).  The value is 100 at the start.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{plusVTS}{+VTS}{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{DTMF}}{DTMF} and Tone Generation}
This command plays the elements of \p{string} in sequence.  Commas
separate the elements.  The command gives \rc{OK} after the last element.
The unit of the durations is 10~ms, 0 to 6000.  A field can be empty.  An
empty frequency is 0, an empty digit is none (silence), and an empty
duration is the duration of \cmdl[plusVTD]{+VTD}.  Thus, \cmd{\{5\}},
\cmd{[440]} and \cmd{[]} have the duration of \cmdl[plusVTD]{+VTD}, and
\cmd{\{\}} is silence for the same time.  The modem puts 50~ms of silence
between two digits that follow each other: \cmdl[plusVTS]{+VTS=1,2,3}
dials 123.  The line does the hook flash: on \std{SIP}, an \std{RFC} 4733
event (16), and the audio is silent during the event.
\begin{syntax}
\item +VTS=\p{string}
\item +VTS? (gives the tone settings)
\item +VTS=? (gives \cmdl[plusVTS]{+VTS=0-4000,0-4000,0-6000})
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}}{After the last element.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{If the modem cannot play one element (the modem plays nothing): a flash on a line that has no flash (a \std{SIP} call whose far end accepts no telephone-events), or a value outside its range.}
\end{results}
\begin{vtable}{2}{|T{0.18\textwidth}|L{0.68\textwidth}|}{+VTS Elements}
  {\thd{Element} & \thd{Plays}}
\p{s} & The \std{DTMF} digit \p{s} (0--9, *, \#, A--D), or \cmdr{!} a hook
  flash, for \cmdl[plusVTD]{+VTD}. \\ \hline
\{\p{s},\p{d}\} & The \std{DTMF} digit or hook flash \p{s} for \p{d}. \\ \hline
[\p{f1},\p{f2},\p{d}] & A tone of \p{f1} and \p{f2} Hz (0 to 4000; 0 is
  none) for \p{d}. \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
\end{command}

\subsection{Voice Events}

While the modem has the line (\cmdl[plusVLS]{+VLS=1}), it gives the
events that it hears, as \std{V.253} specifies, as \cmd{<DLE>} and a
character.  The modem gives \std{DTMF} digits from the far end 51~ms
after they start, and again every 70~ms while they continue.  It gives
them in the voice command state and also in the samples of
\cmdl[plusVTX]{+VRX}.  The audio of these samples is silence from the
report of a digit until the digit ends.  The modem does not give its own
\cmdl[plusVTS]{+VTS} tones.  The modem gives the calling tones when a
burst ends, with a minimum of 4~s between them.

\begin{vtable}{2}{|T{0.2\textwidth}|L{0.66\textwidth}|}{Voice Events}
  {\thd{Report} & \thd{Event}}
<DLE>0--9 * \# A--D & A \std{DTMF} digit from the far end. \\ \hline
<DLE>\textasciitilde & The \std{DTMF} digit ended. \\ \hline
<DLE>r & A ring of a ringing tone (400 to 480~Hz, or 440 and 480~Hz, or
  400 and 450~Hz, on for a minimum of 0.6~s.  The two bursts of the UK
  are one ring.  The ring ends after 0.3~s without the tone). \\ \hline
<DLE>b & A busy or congestion tone (four cycles of 0.2 to 0.8~s on and
  off). \\ \hline
<DLE>a & An answer tone of 2100~Hz (a fax or data answer), 0.3~s after
  it starts. \\ \hline
<DLE>f & An answer tone of 2225~Hz (a data answer), 0.3~s after it starts. \\ \hline
<DLE>c & The \std{CNG} of a calling fax machine (1100~Hz, bursts of 0.36 to
  0.8~s). \\ \hline
<DLE>e & The \std{V.25} tone of a calling data modem (1300~Hz). \\ \hline
<DLE>l & Loop current interruption: the far end ended the call. \\ \hline
<DLE>H, <DLE>h & The phone on the jack of the modem is off hook, or on
  hook again. \\ \hline
<DLE>X \ldots <DLE>. & Caller ID (\cmdl[hashCID]{+VCID}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}

When the far end ends a voice call, the modem gives \cmd{<DLE>l} (the
loop current interruption of \std{V.253}, ``usually a remote hangup'').
Then it goes on hook (\cmdl[plusVLS]{+VLS=0}).  \std{V.253} has no
\rc{NO CARRIER} in voice mode.  The command that is in progress ends:
\begin{itemize}
\item \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VRX} ends its samples with \cmd{<DLE><ETX>}, then
  \rc{OK}.
\item \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VTX} and \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VTR} end when the \std{DTE}
  ends them (the modem discards the samples until then), then \rc{OK}.
  For \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VTR}, the \rc{OK} comes after \cmd{<DLE><ETX>}.
\item The tones of \cmdl[plusVTS]{+VTS} end with \rc{ERROR}.
\end{itemize}
In the command state, the modem gives only \cmd{<DLE>l}.

\subsection{From Voice to Fax or Data}

A voice call can change to a fax call or a data call (\std{V.253} 5.3.2
and its example \std{I.6}).  In the voice command state, off hook,
\cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=1} (or \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{0}) keeps the call.
The voice engine stops, and the line stays off hook and silent.  Then
\cmdl[A]{A} answers on the line as a fax modem (\std{Class 1}: \std{CED},
then \rc{CONNECT} for the \std{DIS}) or as a data modem.  Or \cmdl[D]{D}
calls on the line (call progress waits for the answer tone of the far
end).  \cmdl[H]{H}, \cmdl[Z]{Z} or a disconnection from the far end ends
the held call.  For example, a fax machine calls while the greeting
plays:
\example{Example session: from voice to fax}
\begin{verbatim}
AT+VTX                 CONNECT      the greeting's samples follow
                       <DLE>c       the caller's CNG
<DLE><ETX>             OK
AT+FCLASS=1            OK
ATA                    CONNECT      class 1, as after AT+FTH=3
\end{verbatim}
With \cmdl[plusFAA]{+FAA=1}, the modem does this automatically, in the
voice command state.  (If a \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VTX}, \cmdl[plusVTX]{+VRX},
\cmdl[plusVTX]{+VTR} or \cmdl[plusVTS]{+VTS} is in progress, it ends
first.)  After \cmd{<DLE>c}, the modem gives \rc{FAX},
\cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=1} and the \rc{CONNECT} of the fax answer.
After \cmd{<DLE>e}, it sets \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=0} and does the
adaptive data answer (\rc{DATA}, then its \rc{CONNECT}).

\subsection{Rockwell's and US Robotics' Voice Commands}\label{sec:hashvoice}

Before \std{V.253}, the Rockwell and US Robotics modems had their own voice
commands, with \cmdr{\#} in place of \cmdr{+}.  The Unimodem/V of
Windows~95, the \cmd{Rockwell} and \cmd{US\_Robotics} drivers of vgetty
and many answering machine programs of that time use them.  The modem
also accepts them.  The voice mode that \cmdl[hashCLS]{\#CLS=8} sets is
the same voice mode as \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=8}, with these
differences:
\begin{itemize}
\item When the modem has the line, it gives \rc{VCON}, not \rc{OK}.
\item \cmdl[A]{A} answers a call for voice (as \cmdl[plusVLS]{+VLS=1}
  does).
\item The durations of \cmdl[hashV]{\#VTS} are in 100~ms.
\end{itemize}
\cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=8} removes these differences.  The \cmd{<DLE>}
events and the ends of \cmdl[hashV]{\#VTX} and \cmdl[hashV]{\#VRX} are
the same as in the section above.

\begin{command}{hashCLS}{\#CLS}{Data, Fax or Voice}
As \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS}: 0 data, 1 \std{Class 1} fax, 2 \std{Class 2}
fax, 8 voice in this command set.
\begin{syntax}
\item \#CLS=\p{n}
\item \#CLS? (gives the mode, as \cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS?})
\item \#CLS=? (gives \rc{0,1,2,8})
\end{syntax}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{hashVLS}{\#VLS}{Voice Line Select}
The device, with the numbers of Rockwell.  0 and 4 are the line (the
modem has no speaker to monitor it).  \cmdl[A]{A} or \cmdl[D]{D}
connects the line, and each gives \rc{VCON} when the line is connected.
1 is a handset of the modem (a SmartUSB56 with \texttt{dmodemd
--usb-voice-codec}).  The modem connects it immediately (\rc{VCON}), with
the line if the line is connected.  US Robotics modems answer with
\cmdl[hashVLS]{\#VLS=0A}: \cmdl[hashVLS]{\#VLS=0}, then \cmdl[A]{A}.
\begin{syntax}
\item \#VLS=\p{n}
\item \#VLS? (gives the device)
\item \#VLS=? (gives \rc{0,4}, or \rc{0,1,4} with a handset)
\end{syntax}
\begin{results}
\res{\rc{OK}, \rc{VCON}}{As above.}
\res{\rc{ERROR}}{For a speaker or a microphone (2, 3, 5, 6, 7), or 1 without a handset.}
\end{results}
\end{command}

\begin{command}{hashVBS}{\#VBS, \#VSR, \#VSM}{Voice Sample Format}
\cmdl[hashVBS]{\#VBS} is the number of bits in each sample and
\cmdl[hashVBS]{\#VSR} is the number of samples each second.  Together,
they set the format of \cmdl[plusVSM]{+VSM}.  \cmdl[hashVBS]{\#VSM=129} is
the \std{ADPCM} of US Robotics, which is \std{G.726} at 32~kbit/s.
vgetty reads it in this way.  The modem does not have the \std{ADPCM} of
Rockwell (2, 3 and 4 bits) or the \std{GSM} of US Robotics
(\cmdl[hashVBS]{\#VSM=128}).  They are \rc{ERROR}.  For these, vgetty
must have \cmd{rec\_compression 8} (8-bit linear at 7200) in its
\cmd{voice.conf}.
\begin{vtable}{2}{|T{0.22\textwidth}|L{0.64\textwidth}|}{\#VBS and \#VSM Values}
  {\thd{Command} & \thd{Format}}
\#VBS=8 & 8-bit linear unsigned (\cmdl[plusVSM]{+VSM=1}), at 7200, 8000
  or 11025.  The 8-bit \std{PCM} of Rockwell. \\ \hline
\#VBS=16 & 16-bit linear signed, the low octet first (\cmdl[plusVSM]{+VSM=129}), at
  7200, 8000 or 11025. \\ \hline
\#VSM=129[,8000] & The \std{ADPCM} of US Robotics (\cmdl[plusVSM]{+VSM=133}), at 8000.
  Then \cmdl[hashVBS]{\#VBS=4} keeps it, and \cmdl[hashVBS]{\#VBS=8} or
  \cmdl[hashVBS]{16} changes it. \\ \hline
\#VSR=\p{r} & \p{r} samples each second, if the format operates at \p{r}
  (or else \rc{ERROR}). \\ \hline
\end{vtable}
Each command gives its value with \cmdr{?} and its values with \cmdr{=?}.
\end{command}

\begin{command}{hashV}{\#VTX, \#VRX, \#VTS, \ldots}{Voice Commands}
\cmdl[hashV]{\#VTX}, \cmdl[hashV]{\#VRX}, \cmdl[hashV]{\#VTS},
\cmdl[hashV]{\#VGT}, \cmdl[hashV]{\#VGR}, \cmdl[hashV]{\#VRA},
\cmdl[hashV]{\#VRN}, \cmdl[hashV]{\#VIT} and \cmdl[hashV]{\#VIP} are the
same as the \cmdr{+V} commands with the same names, with two differences.
The durations of \cmdl[hashV]{\#VTS} are in 100~ms
(\cmdl[hashV]{\#VTS=[440,0,5]} makes a beep for half a second).  The
modem accepts the settings in all modes.

The modem keeps these settings for \cmdr{?}, but they have no other
effect, because the modem has no silence deletion and no level controls
of Rockwell: \cmdl[hashV]{\#VTD} (\cmd{<DLE>} report masks, three hex
values), \cmdl[hashV]{\#VSD} (silence deletion, 0 or 1),
\cmdl[hashV]{\#VSS} (silence sensitivity, 0 to 3), \cmdl[hashV]{\#VSK}
(buffer skid), \cmdl[hashV]{\#BDR} (the rate of the \std{DTE}),
\cmdl[hashV]{\#TL} and \cmdl[hashV]{\#RG} (levels, hex).  The modem keeps
\cmdl[hashV]{\#VSP} (the silence period, in 100~ms) as the value of
\cmdl[plusVSD]{+VSD}.
\example{Example session: vgetty's Rockwell driver answers}
\begin{verbatim}
AT#VSP=55#VSD=0#VTD=3F,3F,3F#VSS=1       OK
AT#CLS=8                                 OK
                                         RING
AT#VBS=8#VSR=7200                        OK
ATA                                      VCON
AT#VTX                                   CONNECT  the greeting
<DLE><ETX>                               OK
AT#VRX                                   CONNECT  the message
!                                        <DLE><ETX>OK
ATH0                                     OK
\end{verbatim}
\end{command}

\section{Online Mode, the Escape Sequence and Disconnection}\label{sec:online}

In online mode, the data from the \std{DTE} goes to the far end.  The
exception is the escape sequence: three \sreg{2} characters
(\cmdr{+++}), each in 0.5 s of the character before it, with 0.5 s of no
data before them and after them.  The modem then gives \rc{OK} and goes
into online command mode.  The call stays connected.
\begin{itemize}
\item \cmdl[O]{ATO} goes back online.
\item \cmdl[H]{ATH} disconnects the call.
\item On a \std{V.92} connection, \cmdl[plusPMHR]{AT+PMHR} asks for
  modem-on-hold.
\end{itemize}
The \cmdr{+++} is also data, because the modem cannot know that it is the
escape sequence until the guard time after it.  The modem keeps the data
that the \std{DTE} sent before the escape sequence and that the modem did
not send yet.  It does not discard this data.  The data goes to the other
modem, with \cmdr{+++} last, when \cmdl[O]{ATO} goes back online.

The call ends with \rc{NO CARRIER} when the other side disconnects, or
when its carrier is absent for \sreg{10} tenths of a second.
\texttt{dmodemd} also disconnects when the \std{DTE} sets \std{DTR} off
(it sets the speed of the pty to 0).  If HUPCL is set on the pty,
\texttt{dmodemd} also disconnects when the \std{DTE} closes the pty.  It
then makes a new pty (\cmd{/dev/ttyDM0} follows it).

\section{\texorpdfstring{\texttt{dmodemd}}{dmodemd} on a \texorpdfstring{\texttt{SIP}}{SIP} Line}\label{sec:sip}

\begin{itemize}
\item \cmdl[D]{ATD}\p{number} calls \cmd{sip:}\p{number}\cmd{@}\p{domain}
  of \cmd{SIP\_LOGIN}.  A telephone number has only the dial characters
  of \std{V.250}: digits, \cmd{* \# + A B C D}, the modifiers \cmd{T P W
  , @ ! ;}, \cmd{- ( ) .} and spaces.  It goes as its digits and
  \std{DTMF} characters: \cmdl[D]{ATDT555-1000} calls
  \cmd{sip:5551000@}\p{domain}.  All other strings are the name of a
  \std{SIP} user, with no change: \cmdl[D]{ATDalice}.
\item The line has no dial tone.  Use \cmdl[X]{ATX3} (or \cmdl[X]{X1}),
  or else the result is \rc{NO DIALTONE}.
\item A dial string with a \cmdl[D]{;} (\cmdl[D]{ATDT5551000;}) makes the
  \std{SIP} call and gives \rc{OK} immediately.  The modem is then in
  command state.  The digits that \cmdl[D]{ATD} adds go as \std{DTMF} on
  the call (the choices of a menu, an extension).  \cmdl[D]{ATD} alone or
  \cmdl[A]{ATA} starts the handshake of the modem.
\item With \cmd{-L}, \texttt{dmodemd} registers and accepts calls.  While
  a call waits, the modem gives \rc{RING} every 3 seconds.
  \cmdl[A]{ATA} or \sreg{0} rings (\cmdl[S]{ATS0=1}) answer the call.
  The rings stop if the caller stops the call.  If a call comes while the
  line is in use, \texttt{dmodemd} refuses it (486, busy).  Without
  \cmd{-L}, it refuses all calls (480).
\item The call is \std{G.711} at 8000 Hz.  \std{V.90} and \std{V.92} must
  have this (\cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS=92}, the \emph{default}).  A lower
  modulation can operate better through a real \std{PSTN} gateway, for
  example \cmdl[plusMS]{AT+MS=132,0,4800,9600}.
\end{itemize}

\section{Compatibility}\label{sec:compat}

\subsection{Differences of D-Modem}

\begin{itemize}
\item \cmdl[ampK]{\&K} is the compression, as on the USRobotics modems.
  On the Rockwell and Conexant modems, it is flow control.
  \cmdl[ampK]{\&K3} keeps the compression of \std{V.42} on (\std{MNP~5}
  off).  \cmdl[ampK]{\&K0} sets it off.
\item The S-registers after \sreg{16} are specific to D-Modem.
  \sreg{29} and \sreg{30} are for the speaker (on other modems, the hook
  flash time and the inactivity timer).  \sreg{92} is the delay of the
  answer (on other modems, a fax transmit level).  The other registers
  (\cmdr{S36}, \cmdr{S37}, \cmdr{S91} \ldots) are free.  The modem keeps
  the values that you write in them.  \sreg{7} and \sreg{11} start at 60
  and 100.
\item There are no stored numbers or profiles.  \cmdr{\&Z} and
  \cmdl[D]{ATDS=}\p{n} are \rc{ERROR}.  \cmdl[ampW]{\&W} stores nothing.
\item The numbers of \cmdl[I]{ATI} are specific to the modem
  (\cmdl[I]{I0} to \cmdl[I]{I7}), but not for the call statistics.
  \cmdl[I]{I6} is as on USRobotics modems, \cmdl[I]{I11} as on Conexant
  modems, \cmdl[I]{I12} as \cmdr{S71}, \cmdr{S72} and \cmdr{S73} of
  Telebit (the carriers of a \std{PEP} call), and \cmdl[ampV]{\&V1} as on
  Rockwell modems.
\item The numeric results (\cmdl[V]{V0}) and the number of \rc{CONNECT}
  for each rate are specific to D-Modem.  The numbers of the USRobotics
  and Conexant modems are different.
\item \std{MNP} is classes 2 to 5 (\cmdl[bsN]{\bs N}, \cmdl[pctC]{\%C})
  and \std{MNP~10} (\cmdl[mnp10]{-K}).  The cellular modulations of
  \std{MNP~10EC} are not available.  A modem with \std{MNP~10EC} connects
  with \std{V.42} or with \std{MNP~10} (the modem ignores its ``EM''
  detection patterns).
  Refer to \texttt{docs/link.md}, \std{MNP}.
\item K56flex (\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=56}) is tested against the Motorola SM56
  soft modem and against this modem.  It is not tested against a hardware
  K56flex modem.  Refer to \texttt{docs/dsp/k56flex.md}.
\item The analogue modem on a digital line (\std{SIP},
  \hyperref[sec:digitalclient]{the client on a digital line}) is tested
  only against this modem, on a simulated network.  It is not tested
  against the server of a real provider.
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Not Available}

These commands are \rc{ERROR}: \cmdl[ampV]{\&V2} and higher, \cmdr{\&Z}
(no stored numbers), \cmdl[ampT]{\&T2}, \cmdl[ampT]{\&T9} and higher,
\cmdr{\bs B} (a break), \cmdl[X]{X5} and higher, \cmdl[I]{ATI8} to
\cmdl[I]{ATI10}, \cmdl[I]{ATI13} and higher, \cmd{-V90}
(\cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} selects the modulation), \cmdr{+MA}, \cmdr{+MSC},
\cmdr{+EB}, \cmdr{+ESR}, \cmdr{+ETBM}, \cmdr{+EWIND}, \cmdr{+EFRAM},
\cmdr{+A8M}, \cmdr{+A8T} and \cmdr{+PMHF}.  The
names of \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS} for modulations that the modem does not have
(\cmd{V23S}, \cmd{V27} \ldots) are also \rc{ERROR}.


\section{Glossary}\label{sec:glossary}

{\setlength{\LTleft}{0pt}\small
\begin{longtable}{|>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.24\textwidth}|>{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{0.66\textwidth}|}
\hline\rowcolor{dmhead}\thd{Term} & \thd{Meaning} \\ \hline
\endfirsthead
\hline\rowcolor{dmhead}\thd{Term} & \thd{Meaning} \\ \hline
\endhead
\textbf{A-law, u-law} & The two methods that \std{G.711} uses to code telephone audio in 8 bits.  A-law is for Europe.  u-law is for North America and Japan. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ADPCM}} & Adaptive Differential Pulse Code Modulation.  Compressed audio, one of the voice formats of \cmdl[plusVSM]{+VSM}. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ADSI}} & Analog Display Services Interface.  The screen and keys of a telephone, which tones control. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ALSA}} & Advanced Linux Sound Architecture.  The sound system of Linux.  \texttt{dmodemd} uses it to connect to a telephone line through a sound card. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{AMR}} & Adaptive Multi-Rate.  The speech codec of \std{GSM} and of other mobile networks.  It compresses speech, thus a modem signal cannot go through it. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ANS}, \stdb{ANSam}} & Answer tone and Answer tone, amplitude modulated.  The 2100~Hz answer tone, and the same tone with a 15~Hz change of amplitude that \std{V.8} modems send. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ANSI}} & American National Standards Institute.  An \std{ANSI} terminal uses its escape codes to move the cursor and to set colours. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ASCII}} & American Standard Code for Information Interchange.  The 7-bit character code of most computers.  The S-registers for characters (\sreg{2} to \sreg{5}) hold \std{ASCII} values. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ASVD}} & Analogue Simultaneous Voice and Data.  Speech and data on one modem carrier (\std{V.61}, \cmdl[plusASVD]{+ASVD}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Asynchronous, synchronous} & Asynchronous data goes as separate characters.  Each character has a start bit (0) before it and a stop bit (1) after it.  Synchronous data goes as a continuous bit stream, with no start bits and no stop bits (\std{V.80}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Automode} & A procedure that tries the modulations, from the fastest to the slowest, until the two modems find a common modulation (\cmdl[N]{N}, \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS}). \\ \hline
\textbf{B channel} & Bearer channel.  A \std{64000}~bit/s channel of \std{ISDN} that carries a call.  A basic rate line has two B channels. \\ \hline
\textbf{Baudot} & The 5-bit code (ITA2) of teleprinters, of the \std{TTY} of the deaf community, of telex and of \std{RTTY}. \\ \hline
\textbf{Bearer service} & The type of connection that a digital network gives to a call: speech, 3.1~kHz audio or unrestricted digital data (\cmdl[plusCBST]{+CBST}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Bell 103, Bell 212A} & The North American \std{300} and \std{1200}~bit/s modulations, similar to \std{V.21} and \std{V.22}. \\ \hline
\textbf{Bong tone} & The tone that some long-distance networks send to ask for a calling card number.  On other modems, the dial modifier \cmd{\$} waits for it.  This modem ignores \cmd{\$}. \\ \hline
\textbf{Call waiting} & A service of the telephone network.  A tone tells you that a different call arrives while you are on a call.  A \std{V.92} modem can answer it with modem-on-hold (\cmdl[plusPCW]{+PCW}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{CAPI}} & Common \std{ISDN} Application Programming Interface.  The programming interface of \std{ISDN} cards.  \cmdl[plusB2]{+B2} uses its numbers. \\ \hline
\textbf{CED, \stdb{CNG}} & Called terminal identification and Calling tone.  The fax answer tone (2100~Hz) and the tone of the calling fax (1100~Hz). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{CIG}, \stdb{CSI}, \stdb{TSI}} & Calling subscriber identification, Called Subscriber Identification and Transmitting Subscriber Identification.  The fax identities of the modem that polls, the answering modem and the calling modem (\cmdl[plusFLI]{+FLI}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{Class 1}, \stdb{Class 2}, \stdb{Class 2.0}} & The fax command sets.  In \std{Class 1} (\std{T.31}), the \std{DTE} does the fax protocol.  In \std{Class 2} and \std{Class 2.0} (\std{T.32}), the modem does the fax protocol. \\ \hline
\textbf{Clear channel} & A \std{64000}~bit/s digital channel that the modem uses with no change and with no modulation, as in an \std{ISDN} data call. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{CM}, \stdb{JM}} & Call Menu and Joint Menu.  The messages of \std{V.8}.  The calling modem sends its functions in \std{CM}.  The answering modem sends the common functions in \std{JM}. \\ \hline
\textbf{Codec} & Coder-decoder.  The device or the program that changes audio into numbers, and numbers into audio.  An example is the \std{G.711} codec of a telephone exchange or of a sound card. \\ \hline
\textbf{Configurator} & A screen of D-Modem that shows each setting with its value and its default.  You can change the settings on it (\cmdl[bangC]{!C}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Contact ID} & The \std{DTMF} format of \std{SIA} \std{DC-05}.  Alarm panels use it to send events to a central station (\hyperref[sec:contactid]{Section~\ref*{sec:contactid}}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{CP437}} & Code page 437.  The character set of the IBM PC, with box characters and other symbols. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{CPE}} & Customer Premises Equipment.  The equipment at the end of the line, at the subscriber. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{CRC}, \stdb{FCS}} & Cyclic Redundancy Check and Frame Check Sequence.  A checksum on a frame that shows if the frame has damage. \\ \hline
\textbf{Data pump} & The part of a modem that does the modulation and the demodulation.  The other parts (error correction, compression and the commands) operate on the data from the data pump. \\ \hline
\textbf{dBm0} & The level of a signal in dB, relative to 1~mW at the reference point of the telephone network.  The modem gives the received level and the noise level in dBm0. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{DCD}, \stdb{DSR}, \stdb{DTR}, \stdb{RTS}, \stdb{CTS}} & Data Carrier Detect, Data Set Ready, Data Terminal Ready, Request To Send and Clear To Send.  The control signals of the serial port. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{DCN}} & Disconnect.  The \std{T.30} message that ends a fax call. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{DES}} & Data Encryption Standard.  A block cipher.  Triple \std{DES} uses it three times with two or three keys. \\ \hline
\textbf{DIL} & Digital Impairment Learning.  The \std{V.90} and \std{V.92} sequence that measures the digital network. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{DIS}, \stdb{DTC}, \stdb{DCS}} & Digital Identification Signal, Digital Transmit Command and Digital Command Signal.  The fax messages that offer (\std{DIS}), ask for polling (\std{DTC}) and select (\std{DCS}) the settings of the session. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{DISC}} & Disconnect.  The \std{LAPM} frame that ends the link. \\ \hline
\textbf{DLE} & Data Link Escape (0x10).  In fax and voice modes, the character that starts a code in the data, for example \cmd{<DLE><ETX>}. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{DMT}} & Discrete Multitone.  Many carriers side by side, as in \std{V.wb} and \std{ADSL}. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{DSVD}} & Digital Simultaneous Voice and Data.  A conversation and a data connection on one modem call (\std{V.70}, \cmdl[plusDSVD]{+DSVD}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Duplex, half duplex} & A duplex modem sends and receives at the same time.  A half duplex modem sends in one direction at a time, and the direction changes during the call (fax, \std{PEP}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{EBCDIC}} & Extended Binary Coded Decimal Interchange Code.  The 8-bit character code of IBM mainframe computers (\cmdl[plusDEBC]{+DEBC}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ECM}} & Error Correction Mode.  Fax pages sent in frames.  The modem sends a frame again if it has damage. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{EDT}} & European Deaf Telephone.  A text telephone at \std{110}~bit/s (\std{V.18}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{EOL}} & End of Line.  The code (eleven 0 bits, then a 1 bit) before each row of a \std{T.4} fax page. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{EOM}, \stdb{EOP}, \stdb{MPS}} & End of Message, End of Procedure and Multi-Page Signal.  The \std{T.30} messages after a page: another document follows (\std{EOM}), the fax ends (\std{EOP}), or another page follows (\std{MPS}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Equalizer} & A filter in the receiver that corrects the distortion of the line.  The training adjusts it.  If the line changes too much, the equalizer cannot correct it, and the modems retrain. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ETC}} & Enhanced Throughput Cellular.  The cellular mode of AT\&T Paradyne (\hyperref[sec:etc]{Section~\ref*{sec:etc}}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ETSI}} & European Telecommunications Standards Institute.  The European standards organization. \\ \hline
\textbf{Fallback} & A slower modulation or rate that a modem uses when the line is not sufficiently good for the faster one.  For example, \std{V.22} is the fallback of \std{V.22bis}. \\ \hline
\textbf{Fast connect, quick connect} & Shorter handshakes.  Fast connect (\sreg{49}) is for point of sale terminals.  The quick connect of \std{V.92} uses the line data from the last call. \\ \hline
\textbf{Fine resolution} & A fax resolution of 7.7 rows for each millimetre.  Normal resolution is 3.85 rows for each millimetre. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{FSK}, \stdb{DPSK}, \stdb{QAM}} & Frequency Shift Keying, Differential Phase Shift Keying and Quadrature Amplitude Modulation.  Methods that put data on a carrier: with its frequency, with its phase, or with its phase and amplitude together. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{GSM}} & Global System for Mobile Communications.  The second generation of mobile telephones.  Its 7-bit alphabet is also the alphabet of \std{SMS} text. \\ \hline
\textbf{Guard time} & The pause before and after the escape sequence.  The modem accepts \cmd{+++} only with this pause.  On this modem, it is always 0.5~s (\sreg{12}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Guard tone} & A tone of 550 or 1800~Hz that the answering \std{V.22bis} modem sends in some countries.  This modem sends no guard tone (\cmdl[ampG]{\&G}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Hayes} & Hayes Microcomputer Products.  The company that made the first \cmd{AT} commands.  Many basic commands keep their Hayes meaning. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{HDLC}} & High-level Data Link Control.  Frames with flags and an \std{FCS}.  It is the base of \std{LAPM}, of the \std{T.30} messages of fax and of \std{PPP}. \\ \hline
\textbf{Hexadecimal} & Numbers to the base 16, with the digits 0 to 9 and A to F.  In the text, \cmd{0x} comes before a hexadecimal value (\cmd{0x10}).  In a syntax, \p{h} is a hexadecimal value. \\ \hline
\textbf{Hook flash} & A short on hook that tells the exchange to do a function, for example to answer a waiting call.  The dial modifier \cmd{!} sends it.  A \std{SIP} call has no hook flash. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{HST}} & High Speed Technology.  The modulation of USRobotics: up to \std{16800}~bit/s in one direction and \std{300}~bit/s in the other direction. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{INFO}} & Information sequences.  The messages of \std{V.34} phase 2.  In them, the modems tell each other their functions and data about the line. \\ \hline
\textbf{Init string} & A command line that sets the modem for a program.  \cmdl[bangR]{!R} shows an init string for the settings now. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{ISDN}} & Integrated Services Digital Network.  Digital telephone lines with \std{64000}~bit/s channels. \\ \hline
\textbf{ITU-T} & International Telecommunication Union, Telecommunication Standardization Sector.  The standards organization that writes the V.\ldots{} recommendations (modems), the T.\ldots{} recommendations (fax) and other series. \\ \hline
\textbf{K56flex} & The \std{56000}~bit/s modulation of Rockwell and Lucent, before \std{V.90}.  \cmdl[plusMS]{+MS=56} selects it. \\ \hline
\textbf{Kissoff} & The tone (1400~Hz for 900~ms) that the receiver of Contact ID sends to confirm a message. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{LAPB}} & Link Access Procedure, Balanced.  The \std{HDLC} procedure of \std{X.25} and \std{X.75}. \\ \hline
\textbf{Loop test} & A test in which the data goes back to its source.  A local loop tests this modem.  A remote loop tests the line and the far end (\std{V.54}, \cmdl[ampT]{\&T}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{LR}, \stdb{LD}} & Link Request and Link Disconnect.  The messages of \std{MNP} that start and end its link. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{MCF}, \stdb{CFR}} & Message Confirmation and Confirmation to Receive.  The \std{T.30} messages of the receiver: the page came correctly (\std{MCF}), or the training was good (\std{CFR}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{MDMF}, \stdb{SDMF}} & Multiple Data Message Format and Single Data Message Format.  The two forms of a caller ID message. \\ \hline
\textbf{MH} & Modified Huffman.  The most simple fax page coding (\std{T.4}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Modem-on-hold} & The procedure of \std{V.92} that puts a data call on hold, so that you can answer a different call (\cmdl[plusPMH]{+PMH}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{NMSE}} & Normalized Mean Square Error.  A measure of the quality of the received signal.  A lower value is better. \\ \hline
\textbf{Parity} & An extra bit in each character.  It makes the number of 1 bits even (even parity) or odd (odd parity).  With it, the receiver can find some errors. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{PBX}} & Private Branch Exchange.  An office telephone system, for example Asterisk or FreeSWITCH. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{PEP}} & Packetized Ensemble Protocol.  The modulation of Telebit: up to 511 carriers, half duplex. \\ \hline
\textbf{Polling} & A fax procedure in which the calling fax machine receives a document from the answering fax machine (\cmdl[plusFLP]{+FSP}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{PPP}} & Point-to-Point Protocol.  The protocol that a computer uses to carry internet data through a modem call. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{PREP}, \stdb{ACK}, \stdb{TERM}} & Preparatory, Acknowledgement and Termination.  The signals of \std{V.54} that start, confirm and end a remote loop test. \\ \hline
\textbf{Profile} & A stored set of settings.  Other modems store profiles with \cmdl[ampW]{\&W} and load them with \cmdl[Z]{ATZ}.  This modem has no stored profiles. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{PSTN}} & Public Switched Telephone Network.  The usual telephone network. \\ \hline
\textbf{Pulse dialing} & Dialing with short breaks of the line, one break for each unit of the digit (\cmdl[P]{P}).  Older exchanges use it.  Tone dialing uses \std{DTMF}. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{REJ}, \stdb{SREJ}} & Reject and Selective Reject.  The \std{LAPM} frames that ask for frames again: all the frames from a number (\std{REJ}), or one frame only (\std{SREJ}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Renegotiation} & During a call, the modems change only the rate, with no retrain.  A retrain measures the line again (see Common Terms). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{RFC}} & Request for Comments.  A standard of the internet, for example \std{RFC} 4040. \\ \hline
\textbf{Robbed-bit signalling} & A digital network uses a bit of some samples for its own signals.  A \std{V.90} modem must correct for this. \\ \hline
\textbf{Round trip delay} & The time that a signal takes to go to the far end and come back, in ms.  \std{V.34} measures it at the start of the call. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{RTC}} & Return to Control.  Six \std{EOL}s in a sequence.  They end a \std{T.4} fax page. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{RTTY}} & Radio Teletypewriter.  Baudot through radio, as radio amateurs send it. \\ \hline
\textbf{Sample} & One measurement of an audio signal.  The telephone network takes 8000 samples each second (\std{G.711}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Scrambler} & A function that changes the data into a pattern that looks random, before the modulation.  The receiver changes the pattern back.  Thus, a long row of the same bit does not make a steady tone. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{SIA}} & Security Industry Association.  The organization that wrote the Contact ID standard (\std{DC-05}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{SMS}} & Short Message Service.  Text messages.  On a fixed line, the modem sends and receives them as \std{ETSI} \std{ES} \std{201} \std{912} tells (\hyperref[sec:sms]{Section~\ref*{sec:sms}}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{SNR}} & Signal-to-Noise Ratio.  The level of the signal above the level of the noise, in dB. \\ \hline
\textbf{Symbol rate} & The baud of a modulation.  \std{V.34} has six symbol rates, from \std{2400} to \std{3429} baud. \\ \hline
\textbf{SYN} & Synchronous idle.  The idle octet of the synchronous mode of \std{V.80}. \\ \hline
\textbf{T.30} & The fax protocol: its messages, phases and timers. \\ \hline
\textbf{Terbo} & \std{V.32terbo}.  The \std{16800} and \std{19200}~bit/s extension of \std{V.32bis} from AT\&T.  USRobotics modems also have it. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{TPDU}} & Transfer Protocol Data Unit.  The coded form of one \std{SMS} message (\std{3GPP} \std{TS} \std{23.040}). \\ \hline
\textbf{Transparent and framed sub-modes} & The two sub-modes of the synchronous access of \std{V.80}.  In the transparent sub-mode, the octets go with no change.  In the framed sub-mode, the modem puts the data in \std{HDLC} frames. \\ \hline
\textbf{Type II caller ID} & The caller ID of a waiting call, which the network sends during a call. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{UCS2}} & Universal Character Set, 2 octets for each character.  The 16-bit alphabet of \std{SMS} for text that \std{GSM} characters cannot show. \\ \hline
\textbf{Unimodem} & The modem driver of Windows.  \cmdl[hashUD]{\#UD} gives its diagnostics. \\ \hline
\textbf{Upstream, downstream} & The two directions of a \std{V.90} or \std{V.92} call.  Downstream goes from the digital modem of the server to the analogue modem of the user.  Upstream goes in the opposite direction.  \std{V.92} can also send \std{PCM} upstream. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{URI}} & Uniform Resource Identifier.  The address of a \std{SIP} user, for example \cmd{sip:100@pbx}. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{UTC}} & Coordinated Universal Time.  The time at longitude 0, with no summer time. \\ \hline
\textbf{V.8} & The start of a modern call.  The modems tell each other their functions. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{V.cvm}, \stdb{V.wb}} & The D-Modem modes for data on mobile speech paths, and for a wideband modem on a dry pair. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{V.FC}} & V.Fast Class.  The \std{28800}~bit/s modulation of Rockwell, before \std{V.34}. \\ \hline
\textbf{Voice mode} & The mode for audio.  The modem plays and records audio, and it identifies \std{DTMF} tones (\cmdl[plusFCLASS]{+FCLASS=8}). \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{WAV}} & Waveform Audio File Format.  A file format for audio. \\ \hline
\textbf{X.75, V.110, V.120} & Protocols on an \std{ISDN} data channel (\cmdl[plusB2]{+B2}). \\ \hline
\textbf{x2} & The \std{56000}~bit/s modulation of USRobotics, before \std{V.90}.  \cmdl[plusX2UP]{+X2UP} enables it on a Courier. \\ \hline
\textbf{XID} & Exchange Identification.  The message of \std{V.42} that agrees the compression. \\ \hline
\textbf{\stdb{XON}, \stdb{XOFF}} & Transmit On and Transmit Off.  The characters (DC1 and DC3) that start and stop the flow of data. \\ \hline
\textbf{zstd} & Zstandard.  A modern compression that D-Modem offers between two D-Modem modems (\cmdl[plusDSZ]{+DSZ}). \\ \hline
\end{longtable}}
